10. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: "SMJ" for subject matter jurisdiction; "IPJ" for in personam jurisdiction; "DJ" for diversity jurisdiction; "FQJ" for federal question jurisdiction; "CNOF" for common nucleus of operative facts; and "DPC" for due process clause.

11. All of the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the law described in this exam. Use the scenarios only for the purposes of this exam.

---

Please review Fed. R. Civ. P. 11(a).

A dismissal for failure to comply with FRCP 11 is not on the merits for claim preclusion purposes.

Texas state rules of civil procedure include the following rule. TXRCP 11:

> **(a) Signature.** Every complaint or answer must be signed on each and every page by at least one attorney of record – or by a party personally if the party is unrepresented. Each such page must state the signer's address, e-mail address, and telephone number below the signature. The court shall immediately grant a motion to dismiss responding to a complaint that fails to comply with this rule, and must immediately grant a default judgment against a defendant whose answer fails to comply with this rule. The court

<!-- page: 3 -->

> may issue an order of dismissal or default sua sponte without notice to the party affected.

In the 2010 case of *Weasley v. Malfoy*, the Texas Supreme Court (the highest court in Texas) held that the purpose of TXRCP 11(a) was to force the attorney of record to review each page of the complaint for accuracy, something it doubted attorneys would do without the signature requirement. Given the importance of this purpose, the Texas Supreme Court held that dismissals under Texas Rule 11 are on the merits for claim preclusion purposes.

Texas, and Louisiana, otherwise follow federal common law regarding claim and issue preclusion. Except as otherwise stated in this exam, Texas and Louisiana have rules of procedure and procedural law that are the same as their federal counterparts.

In all jurisdictions relevant to this exam, a tort exists called "fraud in the inducement to contract" (usually shortened to "fraud in the inducement"). The elements of a claim for fraud in the inducement are as follows:

1. a contracting party intentionally makes a statement (oral or written), or fails to make a statement, during contract negotiation with an opposite party;
2. such statement, or omission, is false or misleading;
3. such statement or omission is material;
4. the opposing party reasonably relies on the false or misleading statement or omissions by entering into a contract; and
5. the opposing party suffers damages as a result of entering into the contract.

The last event needed to make the tort of fraud in the inducement complete is that the plaintiff/opposite party suffers damages (which may be earlier than when she discovers the harm).

Case law in both Texas and Louisiana has established that, if a state or federal statute specifies that a contracting party must make a particular disclosure to the opposite party during contract negotiations/formation, then an omission of that disclosure, or a false statement made during that disclosure, is conclusively deemed to be "material" for the purposes of a fraud in the inducement tort.

In Texas, punitive damages are available for the tort of fraud in the inducement, if the plaintiff can prove that the defendant's commission of the tort was malicious, wanton, and beyond the bounds of common decency. Louisiana, in contrast, has by statute banned punitive damages for the tort of fraud in the inducement. You may assume during this exam that the availability of punitive damages is substantive for both horizontal and vertical choice of law purposes.

TX uses the Second Restatement for HCOL purposes. LA uses the First Restatement. Both jurisdictions' long-arm statutes go to the limits of the Constitution.

Texas has four United States District Courts (Northern, Southern, Eastern, and Western). The Northern District of Texas is located in the northern portion of the state, and the primary courthouse is in Dallas. The Southern District of Texas is located in the southern portion of the state; the primary courthouse is in Houston.

Delaware has only one District, the United States District Court for the District of Delaware.

<!-- page: 4 -->

Louisiana has three United States District Courts (Western, Middle, and Eastern). The Eastern District of Louisiana is located in the eastern portion of the state, and the primary courthouse is in New Orleans. The Western District of Louisiana is located in the western portion of the state, and the primary courthouse is in Shreveport.

New York has four United States District Courts (Western, Southern, Northern, and Eastern). The Northern District is located in the Northern portion of the state, with its primary courthouse in Albany.

In 2012, reacting to the Great Recession, Congress passed and the President signed the Truth in Bank Consumer Investment Accounts Act ("TBCIAA") of 2012. Section 9.75 of the Act provides as follows:

> **Section 9.75**
>
> Any investment account provided by a bank to a consumer shall be memorialized by a written contract signed by both the consumer and an appropriately empowered officer of the bank. If such investment account empowers the bank to invest the consumer's funds in bonds issued by business or corporations, such written contract shall specify the ratings level of bonds that constitute permissible investments, according to a large ratings agency.

No other portion of the Truth in Bank Consumer Investment Accounts Act of 2012 is relevant to this exam.

Gringotts Bank is incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Albany, New York. It has for some time offered investment accounts in which consumers could deposit money with Gringotts, which would then pool it with other such accounts for investment purposes. Gringotts' typical investments were corporate bonds. You are a lawyer in the office of general counsel of Gringotts Bank; your office is in Albany, NY. On August 17, you receive a forwarded copy of the complaint immediately below. NOTE: The following pages are an exact copy of the complaint as you received it, except for the signature in the square brackets, which you should consider to be a real signature.

<!-- page: 5 -->

> **United States District Court for the Northern District of Texas**
> **Dallas Division**
>
> ---
>
> Alicia Spinnet, Plaintiff
>
> v. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Case No. 17-000713
>
> Gringotts Bank, Inc., Defendant
>
> ---
>
> 1. This court has subject matter jurisdiction pursuant to sections 1331 and/or 1332 of Title 28 of the United States Code.
>
> 2. Venue is proper in this court under section 1391.
>
> 3. The plaintiff is Alicia Spinnet, a citizen of the State of Texas.
>
> 4. The defendant is Gringotts Bank, which was incorporated in Delaware and has its principal place of business in Albany, New York. It has a single branch in Texas, which is located in Houston.
>
> 5. Since September of 2014, Ms. Spinnet has been a student at the University of Houston, located in Houston, TX.
>
> 6. Since entering into the University of Houston, and residing in a dormitory there, Ms. Spinnet has made substantial sums of money playing online poker. In late April of 2017, Ms. Spinnet had approximately $70,000.00 in a savings account at a bank in Houston.
>
> 7. On or about May 2, 2017, Ms. Spinnet visited the Houston branch of the defendant, Gringotts Bank, seeking an appropriate place to invest her poker winnings.
>
> 8. During her visit, Ms. Spinnet spoke to Gringotts Customer Service Agent ("CSA") Bellatrix Lestrange. CSA Lestrange suggested that Ms. Spinnet consider a Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Account. In the conversation, CSA Lestrange explained that the name "Grade AAA" referred to ratings applicable to the kind of bonds that an investment account of this type would buy. More specifically, CSA Lestrange explained, Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Account

<!-- page: 6 -->

> funds would be used to buy only bonds rated AAA by the Standard & Poor's ratings agency; AAA is Standard & Poor's highest rating, *i.e.*, in the company's opinion, AAA bonds are the least risky available on the market. Ms. Lestrange further explained that Gringotts would take a share of the return on the investment as its fee, "So we will keep your money safe for you because that's what is best for us." Ms. Spinnet told Ms. Lestrange (using the words that follow, or words to that effect) that she was interested only in "extremely safe investments," and that "AAA bonds sound OK, but if y'all were going to use anything more risky, I'd rather stick with the savings account that I have. It may be boring, but at least it's safe." Ms. Lestrange responded, "The Grade AAA Investment Account is perfect for you, then. We're experts. You can rely on us."
>
> 9. After hearing these statements, Ms. Spinnet explained that she wanted to open a Grade AAA Investment Account with Gringotts, but that she did not have a transfer slip with her that would allow her to deposit the funds (which were currently on deposit from another bank). Ms. Spinnet stated that she was to travel the next day to New Orleans to visit a friend, and asked if she could deposit $70,000.00 at the Gringotts branch in New Orleans. Ms. Lestrange said that would be fine, but she urged Ms. Spinnet to execute a contract opening the Grade AAA Investment Account immediately. So, on May 2, 2017, in the Houston Gringotts branch, Ms. Spinnet signed a contract opening a Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Account. The contract that specified, "Funds deposited in this account shall be invested only in corporate bonds graded AAA by Standard & Poor's." Ms. Lestrange signed for Gringotts, as she was authorized by Gringotts policy to do.
>
> 10. On May 3, 2017, Ms. Spinnett traveled to New Orleans. While there, she visited the New Orleans branch of Gringotts and deposited $70,000.00 into her Grade AAA Investment Account, using a cashier's check from the other (non-Gringotts) bank in which she'd previously had a savings account.

<!-- page: 7 -->

> 11. Unbeknownst to Ms. Spinnet, on April 20 of 207, Ms. Lestrange, along with a handful of other CSAs working in Gringotts branches in adjacent states, had received an email from Fenrir Greyback, who supervised all of the CSA email recipients. Mr. Greyback lives and works in Albany, NY, and he sent his email from his office there. In this email, Mr. Greyback stated, "Our profits on the Grade AAA Investment Accounts are way down. Everyone on this email needs to do two things immediately: (i) sign up as many people for Grade AAA Investment Accounts; (ii) any time you sign someone up, send me an email letting me know immediately. I'm going to make sure that the funds are invested in highly risky 'junk bonds.' These high-risk bonds might end up being worthless, but it's not our money, so I don't really care. Maybe these high-risk junk bonds will make a lot of money, and we'll make more on our commissions."
>
> 12. In the afternoon of May 4, Mr. Greyback received from Ms. Lestrange a reply-to-all email in response to his email of April 20. In her reply-to-all email. Ms. Lestrange stated, "Hey, Boss, I just wanted to let you know that I caught us a big fish on the Grade AAA Investment Account thing today. Check out the Spinnet account. $70,000.00! She made it all on online poker, so she probably won't even care when you buy high-risk junk bonds with the money. Go for it!".
>
> 13. In late May of 2017, Mr. Greyback, using his authority as the investment manager for Gringotts, invested all of Ms. Spinnet's $70,000.00 in high-risk bonds issues by the Voldemort Corporation. The Voldemort Corporation runs oil rigs in the Gulf of Mexico near New Orleans. Standard and Poor's rated these bonds CCC, signifying that they were high risk and extremely speculative.
>
> 14. In late June of 2017, several of the Voldemort Corporation's oil rigs malfunctioned, resulting in oil spills in the Gulf of Mexico. The Voldemort Corporation immediately declared bankruptcy, with liabilities (including those from environmental lawsuits) far exceeding its assets. The Voldemort Corporation's bonds became worthless.

<!-- page: 8 -->

> 15. By email dated July 11, 2017, Greyback informed Ms. Spinnet as follows: "Due to circumstances beyond our control, your Grade AAA Investment Account balance now stands at $0. Because there are no funds on deposit, we are closing the account. We hope you have a nice day." Ms. Spinnet received the email in New Orleans, where she was visiting a friend, on the same day that it was sent.
>
> 16. Sole count of the complaint: fraud in the inducement to contract. By making the statements recited in the paragraphs above, Gringotts, through the speech of its authorized agent, Ms. Lestrange, and through its written contract, intentionally made a false statement to Ms. Spinnet; these statements are material, and are conclusively deemed material because they violated Section 9.75 of the federal Truth in Bank Consumer Investment Accounts Act of 2012; and Ms. Spinnet reasonably relied upon these statements in her decision to open an investment account with Gringotts. As a result of these false statements, Ms. Spinnet suffered actual damages in the amount of $70,000.00. In addition, the false statements were malicious, wanton, and beyond the bounds of common decency. Wherefore, Ms. Spinnet prays that this court award her $70,000.00 in compensatory damages and $700,000.00 in punitive damages.

At your boss's request, you hire a private investigator to do a little fact finding. A couple of days later, the investigator provides her report, which informs you as follows: Alicia Spinnet was born in Delaware, where she lived with her family until she entered college at the University of Houston. While there, she began playing online poker, and discovered that she was good at it. By April of 2017, in her junior year at the University of Houston, Spinnet had amassed a fortune of around $70,000.00 in poker winnings. On April 15, 2017, Spinnet wrote an email to Seamus Finnegan, her boyfriend since high school who still lived in Delaware, saying:

> "I love it here! I've thrown away my coat and all of my cold weather clothes. I have $70,000.00 in the bank from playing the online poker game that I learned how to play in one of my classes. The professor who taught me how to play poker says as soon as I graduate, she'll hire me as a permanent TA for the class. I'm shopping right now for a condo near my university dorm to buy after graduation. Texas has no state income tax! It's the best! Come live here with me, or we'll probably have to break up."

## Question 1 (35 points)

Your boss asks you for a memo discussing grounds for a motion to dismiss, or in the alternative, to transfer. What thoughts do you have? (Discuss all grounds fairly raised by the fact pattern. Note: you may assume that process was properly served.)

---

## Question 2 (5 points)

For the purposes of this Question 2 only, assume the following. On October 24, the court sua sponte dismisses Spinnet's complaint in No. 17-000713, issuing a one-sentence opinion that cites "failure to comply with applicable signature requirements." Angelina Johnson, Spinnet's lawyer, notifies you via email that she intends to file a new case in federal court in the Eastern District of Louisiana, using the same complaint. Your boss asks you whether you think there are any grounds for a motion to dismiss or summary judgment, other than those you discussed in your Question 1 memo. What do you say?

---

Picking up where we left off at the end of Question 1: Assume for the remainder of this exam that the court in No. 17-000713 does NOT dismiss the complaint, but rather transfers the case to the Eastern District of Louisiana without further comment.

## Question 3 (4 points)

Your boss asks you whether there are any new grounds for a motion to dismiss or summary judgment, other than those discussed in your Question 1 memo. What do you say? (Note: if there are any facts that you would like your private investigation firm to find out, state what they are and why they matter. Also, do not discuss the possibility of yet another transfer in the case.)

---

Gringotts decides to litigate in the E.D.LA and answers the complaint. The parties proceed to very rapid discovery, and your investigative unit goes to work. The investigation finds out the following: Greyback's email went to supervisors in multiple states, and resulted in the opening of Gringotts Grade

<!-- page: 10 -->

AAA Investment Accounts in multiple states. In each case, the Gringotts Customer Service Agents, following Greyback's emails, made representations (regarding the risk level of the corporate bonds eligible for purchase) to customers functionally indistinguishable from those Spinnet asserted that Lestrange made in Houston. In each case, the CSAs used a written contract identical to that used in the Spinnet account to open Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Accounts, then notified Greyback. In each case, Greyback invested the money in high-risk corporate bonds. Several of the accounts have become worthless or lost substantial value. The location and number of the accounts that followed this pattern and as to which a substantial loss of money occurred are as follows: Houston (5 accounts); New Orleans (3 accounts); Jackson, Mississippi (25 accounts); Oklahoma City, Oklahoma (40 accounts); and Atlanta, Georgia (45 accounts). In each instance, the account holder opened the account in her home town using a Gringotts branch located there.

## Question 4 (7 points)

Your boss is worried that Spinnet (or, more accurately, her lawyer), if she finds out these facts in discovery, may move for leave to amend her complaint to attempt to turn the case into a class action. Do you think you could defeat a class certification motion?

---

Continuing with the E.D.LA. case: It turns out that no class certification motion is ever filed. Instead, after neither party moves for summary judgment, an appropriate pretrial order is entered, and the case goes to an accelerated trial before a jury. In summary, the trial proceeded as follows.

- Spinnet testified to the events that she personally witnessed, as described in the complaint above, including statements recited in the complaint by Lestrange and Spinnet herself. Spinnet authenticated the Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Account contract that, according to her, Lestrange and she had signed, as well as a Gringotts receipt for deposit of $70,000.00 in the Gringotts New Orleans branch. Both documents were admitted into evidence. Spinnet testified that despite her efforts, she has never received any of her money back from Gringotts.

- Fenrir Greyback testified for Spinnet. Greyback explained that he had recently had a religious conversion and felt obligated to repair the wrongs he had done by testifying to the truth. He testified that he had sent and received the Greyback and Lestrange emails described in the complaint, and that at the time he had meant what he said in his emails. When presented with printouts of those emails, he authenticated them as genuine. He testified that he did, in fact, mis-invest Spinnet's funds, which became worthless when Voldemort Co. went bankrupt. He authenticated a printout of his email to Spinnet telling her that her investment fund was now worthless. He testified that the contract Spinnet had authenticated earlier was the standard form contract that Gringotts used for its Gringotts Grade AAA Investment Accounts. He also stated that the handwriting on the signature on the contract matched Lestrange's signature, which he had seen in other Gringotts documents.

- The Greyback-Lestrange emails and the Greyback-Spinnet email were entered into evidence.

- Lestrange testified for Greyback. She denied making any statements or representations to Spinnet about the risk level of the corporate bonds in the investment account. When presented with a copy of the contract Spinnet had earlier identified, Lestrange denied that the document was genuine and denied that her signature was on it, stating, "I know that looks like my signature. The handwriting looks the same and everything. But it must be a forgery. Plus, I don't think that's a contract form that we use at Gringotts." Lestrange denied having sent or received the emails that Greyback testified about, stating, "I can see that those emails were sent

<!-- page: 11 -->

to and from my email account, but I never saw them. Someone must have hacked into my account. I would never say any of those things, and I would never defraud anyone. I can certainly understand Spinnet's point that if someone had said those things to her, about looking for a safe place to put money and all that, it would have made the difference about whether she invested with Gringotts. But I never said those things."

There was no other evidence. There were no erroneous procedural or evidentiary rulings. At the close of all the evidence, Spinnet moved for judgment as a matter of law. The judge deferred ruling on the motion. After being properly instructed on the elements of the fraud in the inducement cause of action, the jury returned a verdict for Gringotts.

## Question 5 (3 points)

The day after the jury verdict, your boss sends you an email, saying, "Can Spinnet immediately appeal the jury's verdict"? How do you respond?

## Question 6 (6 points)

The next day, your boss sends you another email: "Can Spinnet file any post-verdict motions to upset the jury's verdict? Would any succeed?".

## Question 7 (25 points)

In a hypothetical small claims (state) court, there is a $5000 amount-in-controversy upper limit. No counterclaims or joinder are permitted. Judges need not, and frequently are not, lawyers. There is no record made of the proceedings, other than the complaint and the judgment (which must be in writing and which the judge must sign). There are no motions allowed. The complaint is filed, the court sends out a summons and a copy of the complaint to the parties containing a trial date approximately six weeks later, and a trial is held on that date. Continuances can be granted for good cause only. Trials proceed by swearing in each party and receiving testimony, and by examining any sworn affidavits that either party produces together with whatever documents each party produces. The rules of evidence do not apply; judges consider whatever they deem sufficiently reliable. There is no appeal from the trial judge's ruling. The parties can be represented by counsel, but there is no right to counsel at government expense. The court can issue money judgments only, not injunctions.

- Is this scheme constitutional?
- As a matter of access to justice, what are its advantages and disadvantages?
- Do you think this scheme is a good idea?

If you need more information to answer these questions, specify what it is, and explain why you need it.

**End of Exam**

## 2017-final · Instructor model answer

Question 1:

The court will dismiss or transfer for lack of venue.

There is likely SMJ via diversity but not AUJ. Gringotts = NY, DE. Sp used to be Delaware, which would
destroy diversity, but her email suggests that, while living in TX, she formed an intent to stay there by
looking for a condo and demanding that her boyfriend move, which is probably enough to change her
domicile to TX. $70K in compensatory damages < $75K, so AIC depends on the HCOL viability of the
$700K punitive damages claim. TX allows the punitive damages for fraud, LA does not. The N.D.TX
(Klaxon) will use the 2d Restatement. The Sp-Gringotts relationship formed in TX at a bank branch
located there. The alleged misconduct, part of a scheme including a TX resident (triggering TX's interest
in regulating misconduct) occurred in TX. True, Sp deposited the money and found out she'd been
damaged while in LA, and her bonds became worthless due to an accident that occurred near LA, but
her location in these events seems gratuitous, due to unrelated travel (and CCC bonds are available in
numerous locations).
Regarding AUJ, there is no federal cause of action (Mottley). Accepting complaint paragraphs 8-9 as
true, Gringotts violated section 9.75, the misstatements will conclusively be deemed material for the
fraud case. Thus, the federal law violation is embedded within the state fraud claim (Smith-Grable). But
Gringotts is unlikely to contest that embedded federal issue; instead, it is likely to defend on the facts
(making the case's issues are likely factual). Plus, there is no federal government agency whose actions
are at issue, and holding that any violation of federal law that became part of a state cause of action
triggered AUJ would sweep too many cases into federal court.

There is no general IPJ (one branch is nowhere near enough of Gringott's business to make it "at home"
in TX, Daimler) but there is easily specific IPJ. FRCP 4(k)(1)(A) plus TX's long-arm going to the limits of
the Constitution result in a focus on whether the Houston negotiation between Sp and Lestrange
constitutes minimal contacts relating Gringotts, the alleged fraud, and TX (yes). Litigation in TX is fair
and reasonable because witnesses (Lestrange, Spinnet) and documents (contract, emails) are there;
because TX has an interest in regulating misconduct in its borders; and because TX law will likely apply.

Venue is improper. No event giving rise to the case occurred in north Texas (1292(b)(2)), nor does
Gringotts reside in the N.D.Tex (1291(b)(1)) because it has no branch or contacts to the N.D.Tex (1291(d,
c(2)). Spinnet could lay venue in S.D.Tex (1291(b)(2)), so no 1291(b)(3).

The complaint does state a claim with sufficient specificity (Twiqbal). We ignore paragraph 16 as
conclusory, but otherwise, there the complaint alleges with specificity via emails that the misstatement
was intentional and false, via Sp's statements that it was material, via Lestrange statements that
reliance was reasonable, and that damages resulted via the investment in risky securities, all nudging
the complaint across the line from possible to plausible.

The disparate signature requirements require a VCOL analysis in this diversity case. Under York, the
difference is outcome determinative (TXRCP 11 requires dismissal for failure to sign every page, FRCP
not so), so we use state law and dismiss. Under Harlan, the difference addresses how lawyers file
complaints not how clients act pre-litigation, so use the federal rule. Under Scalia, the signature
requirement is valid under the REA because it really regulates the manner and means of case processing,
and it is applicable because it speaks to what signatures are required, so use FRCP 11 and do not
dismiss. Under Ginsburg, one can avoid a conflict by interpreting FRCP 11 as requiring a signature but
being silent on multiple signatures, and the Weasley court held that the TXRCP purpose of encouraging
attorneys to review complaints was important, so probably use state law and dismiss.

Because venue is improper, the court must transfer. Even if venue were proper, the court should still
transfer to either the S.D.Tex., the E.D.LA, D.Del., or N.D.N.Y. Under Hoffman, the former two have
specific IPJ (key banking relationship events occurred there) and venue (ditto, 1291(b)(2)), the latter two
have general IPJ (place of incorp & PPoB) and venue (1291(b)(1)). But only for the former two do the
public and private interest factors support transfer. The events, witnesses (only Greyback is outside the
deep South), and documents described above all occurred in those two venues, suggesting convenience
of litigation and an interest in litigating the matter.

Question 2:
The new E.D.La. case should be dismissed on claim preclusion grounds. It is likely (see above) that the
N.D.Tex. was sitting in diversity, in which case FCL will likely demand that the E.D.LA. give at least as
much preclusion as Texas state law (analogous to Semtek), The E.D.La. case has the same plaintiff
(Spinnet), the N.D.Tex. dismissal is final, and it involves the same transaction/fraud. On the merits? TX
state law says yes for TXRCP 11 dismissals (Weasley).

SMJ, specifically AIC may also be lacking, depending on HCOL with LA's Restatement 1 (Klaxon) analysis
(see Q1 re TX/LA conflict on punitives). Here, the last event necessary is to complete the tort is the loss
of the money, which likely occurred when Voldemort Co.'s bonds became worthless; the accident
occurred near but perhaps not legally in LA, and where were the bonds, anyway? RPerhaps where
Voldemort was incorporated (we don't know plus see Shaeffer, which implies otherwise). Perhaps
where Sp was (TX?) at the time her account became worthless. Restatement 1 makes little sense here.
So hard to say.

Question 3:
Because venue was not proper in the N.D.Tex., the HCOL analysis does not follow the transfer, so the
E.D.La. court will apply the LA First Restatement (Klaxon) for HCOL. See second paragraph of Q2
response for possible SMJ/AIC defect.

Question 4:
With Sp as a named pla, we have anchoring SMJ (see above) and Ben-Hur/Exxon means no diversity or
AIC problems with unnamed plaintiffs.

Given the Greyback email inducing a common scheme, and the similarity among the statements and the
written contracts used in the various transactions, there will probably be predominance/commonality
(Walmart involved no centrally-induced scheme, rather (according to the majority) the opposite of one),
and because Spinnet is a victim of the Greyback-induced fraud scheme, representativeness and
typicality are probably satisfied. Conflicting state laws on punitives seem of lesser concern. Superiority
seems satisfied given the common policy involved and the geographic dispersion of those affected by it.

But numerosity will be lacking. Per our class discussion, LA cannot exercise IPJ over the cases that arose
outside LA, except Spinnet's (which does have substantial connection to LA) because those cases have
absolutely no contacts to LA. Three LA accounts is not numerous.
Question 5:
No. The jury's verdict is not a final order and no judgment has been issued. The jury's verdict is, as
opposed to collateral to, the merits (Cohen); there is no injunction (1292(a)(1)); there is no trial judge
certification of an important issue (double discretion); there is only one claim (FRCP 54); there is no class
action (FRCP 23); there is no irreparable harm, nor a pattern of judge misconduct (1651).

Question 6:
Spinnet may renew her FRCP 50 motion. Construing the facts (including credibility determinations)
reasonably in Gringotts's favor, could the jury could reasonably credit Lestrange's self-serving conspiracy
theories? The only element of the cause of action in dispute is whether Lestrange made false
statements to Spinnet, and whether the contract entered into evidence was genuine. The Greyback-
Lestrange emails, which Lestrange concedes came from her computer, are powerful evidence that she
did and that it is. Spinnet's testimony that the contract (with her own signature) is genuine is also
uncontradicted. Lestrange offers no story for who forged her signature or who hacked her computer or
how the hacker could have known who Spinnet (mentioned specifically in the email) was or how Spinnet
made her money, nor does she suggest why a hack occurred. Lestrange's testimony conceded
materiality, and the deposit receipt plus Greyback's and Spinnet's uncontradicted testimony
demonstrates the harm. Scott v. Harris. The R50 motion seems likely to succed.
A Rule 59 motion arguing the verdict is against the great weight of the evidence, in which setting the
trial judge has greater leeway to make credibility calls, is likely to succeed for the same reasons (Yeatts).

Question 7
Lots of good answers

# EXAM 2018-final — 2018 Greiner Civil Procedure 2 Final Exam
