## 2018-final · Exam text

Civil Procedure 2
                                                     Fall 2018

                                         Professor D. James Greiner

                              The exam mode for this exam is TAKEHOME

Available for download: Monday, December 17 between 7:30am - 8:30am

Must be electronically submitted by: Monday, December 17, 8 hours from download or by
4:30pm, whichever is earlier

This exam 10 pages long. Please check to see that you have all 10 pages.

There are four unequally weighted questions.

Exam4 will automatically print your Anonymous ID and word count on the exam.

Word limit: 2500 words

1. HLS will administer the exam according to standard one-day take-home time limits. Spend a
substantial portion of your exam time getting organized before writing. You will botch up this
exam if you don't take the time to get organized before you start writing.

2. Your response must be typed using the Exam4 exam software. Your response can be no
more than the word limit above, everything included. Should you depart from the length
limit, the first portion of your exam, up to the word limit above, will be graded and the rest
ignored. HLS will administer the exam delivery and submission system.

3. You may not communicate with any person other than the exam administrators regarding the
substance of this exam until after the 1L exam period is over. The reason for this prohibition
is that, each year, some students take the exam on a date other than its scheduled
administration date. Thus, do not think that you may discuss this exam immediately after
you hand it in. Wait until the 1L exam period is over. Note also that the prohibition above
includes librarians. According to HLS policy: "[S]tudents may not communicate with the
instructor nor raise questions or concerns about the content of an examination once it is in
progress and the Registrar's Office will not disseminate additional information about an exam
once it is in progress. To maintain anonymity and to ensure compliance by students with this
policy, instructors should not communicate in any way, including through email, with students
during an examination."

4. If you feel that there is a mistake in the exam, such as that a question is unclear or that it
refers to something that it should not or that there is a critical typographical error, then do the
following. First, in your answer, explain what you believe to be the mistake. Second, explain
the implications of the mistake. Third, make an assumption that is reasonable given the fact
pattern and applicable law to resolve the error. Fourth, proceed with the remainder of the exam.
5. Your answer must be your own work. Any quotation from any source must be accompanied
by a corresponding reference (but use short references, e.g., "International Shoe," or "FRCP
23").

6. Each question's weight is specified (out of 65 total points). Time and word-count may be
issues for you during the exam. Answer questions efficiently, and feel free to take into account
the number of points available for each question. Don't forget your test-taking skills.

7. Do not put your name on your response.

8. Deal with all issues fairly raised, even if you think one or more would be dispositive in the
real world.

9. If you believe additional information (factual or legal) is needed to deal with an issue, say so
and make explicit whatever assumptions you need to proceed, but make these assumptions
reasonable and interesting. Similarly, if at any point in this question you find that you do not
know what the law is on a particular subject, identify the issue as to which there is uncertainty,
then discuss the various options as to what the law could or should be, noting the advantages or
disadvantages of each option.

10. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: "SMJ" for subject matter
jurisdiction; "IPJ" for in personam jurisdiction; "DJ" for diversity jurisdiction; "FQJ" for federal
question jurisdiction; "CNOF" for common nucleus of operative facts; and "DPC" for due
process clause.

11. All of the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the
law described in this exam. Use the scenarios only for the purposes of this exam.

                              ********************************

In 2007, Congress passed the Disposition of Property Deriving from Federal Lands Act (the "DPDFLA").
The statute created an agency to deal with litigation relating in any way to property deriving from
federal land. You may assume that Congress had the constitutional power to enact this statute. The
statute in relevant part provides as follows.

Section 29: Creation of the Federal Land Dispute Resolution Agency ("FLDRA"), Jurisdiction, Procedures

    (a) The FLDRA shall be composed of administrative law judges (ALJs) appointed by the Director of
         the Bureau of Land Management. It shall adjudicate cases relating in any way to property
         derived from federal government public lands.

    (b) There shall be one division of the FLDRA in each state, and each division shall exercise
         jurisdiction over persons or property as though the division were subject to and empowered by
         Fed. R. Civ. P. 4(k)(1)(A); thus, each FLDRA division shall, for the purposes of personal
         jurisdiction, act as if it were a federal district court in located in the state in which that division
         sits. Each FLDRA division shall consist of a panel of three ALJs that, unless otherwise specified in
         this statute, shall adjudicate cases as a three-judge panel.
    (c) The FLDRA divisions shall conduct adjudications with an eye toward minimum formality of
         adjudication. The rules of evidence do not apply; the FLDRA divisions shall consider all
         submissions that the parties might make. Any concerns about the admissibility of the evidence
         shall instead be directed toward the weight that such evidence should receive.

    (d) A party seeking relief from an FLDRA division shall initiate a proceeding therein by filing a short
         and plain statement of the grounds for the FLDRA division's jurisdiction, of the claim showing
         that the party is entitled to relief, and of a demand for the relief sought, which may include
         relief in the alternative or different types of relief.

    (e) Notice to the opposing party of the FDLRA division proceeding may be made in any manner
         reasonably likely to apprise the opposing party of the proceeding, but shall include a copy
         (electronic or otherwise) of all papers filed at the proceeding's initiation.

    (f) In general, an FLDRA division will allow an initial submission by both parties of evidence to
         support their contentions, followed by a second submission of legal arguments and rebuttal
         evidence. The FLDRA divisions shall make their findings of fact and conclusions of law based
         only on written submissions from the parties. The record may include affidavits, as well as
         authenticated documents and other exhibits. There shall be no live hearings or oral
         presentations, nor shall there be any discovery. The FLDRA divisions shall make maximum
         reasonable use of Internet communications devices to facilitate its adjudication.

    (g) The FLDRA divisions shall have jurisdiction only with respect to claims by a single plaintiff against
         a single defendant, although they may hear joined claims and counterclaims. In particular, the
         FLDRA divisions may not allow joinder of parties, intervention, or class actions.

    (h) In all other matters, and subject to subsection (c) of this section, the FLDRA divisions may use
         such procedures as they deems appropriate, including any principles or rules used in federal
         courts, such as the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

Section 30: Creation of a Cause of Action that Can Be Asserted before the FLDRA, Law Applicable, Non-
Preemption of State Court Litigation, Waiver of Sovereign Immunity

    (a) A party who has suffered a tort, breach of contract, or other wrong relating in any way to
         property derived from federal public land may initiate a proceeding within the relevant FLDRA
         division for appropriate relief. The FLDRA division may award any remedy in law or equity that it
         finds just and appropriate, including damages, declaratory relief, and/or an injunction. Within
         the confines of these limits, the FLDRA shall otherwise have the capacity to hear any case or
         controversy within the judicial power of U.S. Const. Art. III.

    (b) Notwithstanding any other provision of law, the federal courts will have no jurisdiction over any
         dispute subject to the FLDRA jurisdiction. Nothing in this statute shall be deemed to prevent
         any litigation otherwise available in state court, and there shall be no removal of such actions to
         federal court or to the FLDRA.

    (c) The laws of the several states, except where the Constitution or treaties of the United States or
         Acts of Congress otherwise require or provide, shall be regarded as rules of decision in the
         FLDRA divisions, in cases where they apply.

    (d) All decisions of the FLDRA shall be final and unappealable to any other adjudicatory body,
         including any federal or state court.

Section 31: Pre-adjudication Seizure of Property
A party may request an emergency order from the relevant FLDRA division for immediate seizure and
appropriate disposition of property deriving from federal land. The FLDRA division shall deploy the
following procedures with respect to such a request.

    (a) Initial filing: A party requesting an emergency order shall file
              1) a short and plain statement of the claim showing that the FLDRA division has jurisdiction
                   over the claim, that the party is entitled to relief, and that the ultimate relief sought in
                   the case is appropriate;
              2) a copy of any documents relevant to either the claim and to the emergency order
                   request in the claimant's possession;
              3) affidavits or sworn statements, if any, relating to the claim for relief or to the request for
                   the seizure order; and
              4) an emergency request for immediate adjudication of whether an order of seizure should
                   issue.

    (b) Order of seizure: A single FLDRA judge shall immediately examine the papers referenced in
         subsection (a) of this section. If the ALJ finds that good cause exists, she shall immediately issue
         an order authorizing an appropriate law enforcement officer to seize the specified goods and to
         dispose of them in an appropriate manner, including but not limited to holding the goods until
         the conclusion of the FLDRA division proceeding. In the event that the goods are sold, the
         proceeds of the sale shall be deposited in an account the FLDRA division controls, and the FLDRA
         division shall thereafter make appropriate disposition of such proceeds.

    (c) Post-seizure proceedings: After issuing its order of seizure, the FLDRA division shall proceed
         according to its ordinary procedures to adjudicate the case.

Section 32: Promulgation of Regulations

A randomly selected panel of seven FLDRA division administrative law judges shall have the power to
promulgate regulations, binding on all FLDRA divisions, for the purpose of creating procedures for the
divisions to use. The procedures promulgated pursuant to this section shall preserve the informal and
flexible character of FLDRA proceedings, but will not violate Section 29, above. No rule created pursuant
to this section may abridge, enlarge or modify any substantive right.

**********

Under Washington and Oregon state law, a plaintiff may sue for tort called negligent interference with
contractual relations. To prevail in this cause of action, a plaintiff must prove that the defendant (i)
committed an act or omission that is a departure from ordinary care, that (ii) the plaintiff knew, or
should have known, would cause the plaintiff to suffer damages in the form of an inability to receive the
benefit of a valid contract not involving the defendant, (iii) that the plaintiff did in fact suffer such
damages.

Under California, Delaware, and Idaho state law, there is no tort for negligent interference with
contractual relations. There is, however, a cause of action called intentional interference with
contractual relations. To prevail in this cause of action, a plaintiff must prove that the defendant (i)
committed an act or omission with the intention of causing the plaintiff to suffer damages in the form of
an inability to receive the benefit of a contract not involving the defendant, and (ii) that the plaintiff did
in fact suffer such damages.
For both the negligent and the intentional version of the interference with contractual relations tort, the
last event necessary to complete the tort is the breach of the contract with which the defendant
interfered.

All states relevant to this exam have a standard cause of action for breach of contract. To prevail on a
common law breach of contract claim the plaintiff must show: (1) The plaintiff and defendant made a
valid enforceable contract, (2) the Plaintiff performed his/her part of the contract or offered a
justification for nonperformance, (3) the Defendant failed to perform his/her part of the contract, and
(4) damage resulted to the plaintiff. The Plaintiff must prevail on all elements to win.

Oregon and Washington follow the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Delaware, California,
and Idaho follow the Second Restatement.

Oregon and California preclusion law matches the federal common law of preclusion. Delaware,
Washington, and Idaho preclusion law provides that a limitations dismissal is considered on the merits;
there is no mutuality requirement for issue preclusion; and multiple independent grounds for a jury
verdict are considered resolved in favor of the prevailing party.

Oregon, Idaho, and Delaware have only a single federal district. Washington has two federal districts,
the Eastern and Western. The city of Potomac is located in the Eastern District of Washington.
California has four federal districts. The city of Sacramento is in the Eastern District of California.

Oregon, Idaho, Delaware, Washington, and California all have long-arm statutes that go to the limits of
the Constitution.

Florean Fortescue was a farmer. He leased federal land in Oregon, where he was born and lived his
entire life, to grow crops. His primary farmland was located in an isolated area of northwestern Oregon.
Due to recent fires, washouts, and other natural disasters, Florean's farmland was accessible only by a
logging road that, in the sleepy town of Hogsmeade, Oregon, passed over the Hogsmeade Bridge. The
town of Hogsmeade raised revenue by charging any vehicle larger than a passenger car a toll to cross
the bridge.

In early 2017, the Commissioners of the town of Hogsmeade decided that the Hogsmeade Bridge, which
was also used by the town's few residents, was in need of repairs, although the repairs were not urgent.
They contracted with Cho Chang Contracting Corp., incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of
business in Potomac, Washington, to do the repairs. Chang Corp does business exclusively in the states
of Washington and Oregon. Chang Corp. advised the Commissioners that the repairs would require that
the Bridge be shut down for approximately two weeks. The contract for the repairs stated that Chang
Corp. would take care to schedule the shutdown "keeping in mind the needs of farmers in northwestern
Oregon to access the bridge for the purpose of delivering their harvests to markets in the central and
eastern portions of the state, and to give due notice to such farmers of the dates of any shutdown."
Otherwise, the contract allowed Chang Corp. to choose the time of the shutdown, so long as all repair
work was complete by January of 2021.

In January of 2018, Florean signed a contract with Bathilda Bagshot Industries, Inc, incorporated in
Delaware. The contract was negotiated via email exchange, with Florean writing from
FloreanFortescueFarmer(at)gmail.com and Bathilda writing from BathildaBagshotBuyer(at)gmail.com. It
was signed by both parties using an online signature verification service. In the contract, Bagshot
agreed to buy 200,000 bushels of Florean's corn at $2.00 per bushel. The contract specified that Florean
was to deliver the corn to a facility in Sacramento, California, adjacent to Bagshot's headquarters.
Delivery was to be made at any time during the month of September, 2018. According to the contract,
Bagshot had seven days to inspect the corn. If within those seven days, Bagshot believed that the corn's
condition was not in accordance with industry standards, it could refuse delivery, in which case either
party could pursue appropriate contract remedies. The contract further specified that Bagshot could
assign or sell "any portion of its rights to the corn specified in this contract to any third party, which
third party shall be bound by provisions of this contract."

In February of 2018, Bathilda sold the rights to 50,000 bushels of Florean's corn to Ted Tonks Industries,
Inc., incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Idaho. The sales contract was
negotiated over the telephone, with calls made between Bathilda's offices in Sacramento and Tonks'
offices in Boise, Idaho. The contract was signed on the Internet via an online signature verification
service.

On Friday, August 31, 2018, a fleet of approximately 200 semis gathered at a distribution facility in one
of Florian's farms, loaded up 1000 bushels of corn each, and set off for Sacramento. They drove through
part of the night, but when they arrived at the Hogsmeade Bridge, they found that Chang Corp. had
closed it for repairs. The fleet of semis was unable to get through. Upon hearing of these events,
Florean immediately drove to Potomac to meet with Chang Corp.'s Chief Executive Office, Ms. Marietta
Edgecombe. Edgecombe told Florean that she, from her office in Potomac, had scheduled the
Hogsmeade job herself. She agreed that she could have scheduled the Hogsmeade Bridge repairs at any
time, from the August of 2018 to February of 2019 (note that there is no farm harvest in Oregon in
January or February), but stated that she thought it best to "get them out of the way." Florean
demanded that Chang Corp. reopen the bridge immediately to allow his corn harvest through, but
Edgecombe said, "No, now that we've started, I want to finish."

The Hogsmeade Bridge remained closed for about two weeks, during which time Oregon experienced
unseasonably hot weather. The fleet of semis finally arrived in Sacramento on September 17, 2018.
Bathilda and Tonks immediately sent in their inspectors, and one of Florean's farm hands who had
accompanied the fleet, a Mr. Dobby Kreacher, also examined the corn's condition.

Question 1 (25 points):

Assume the following for the purposes of this Question 1 only (there will be different facts for you to
assume in Question 2).

On September 18, Bathilda filed (via internet upload) an emergency action in the California FLDRA. In its
complaint, Bathilda recited the contract with Florean, the fact that Florean leased his farmland from the
United States government (and thus that the corn related to a product derived from federal land); the
sale of a portion of its interest in the corn shipment to Tonks; and the arrival of the corn on the 17th. It
further stated that by email to FloreanFortescueFarmer(at)gmail.com, Bathilda had notified Florean that
it considered the corn spoiled, meaning not in accordance with industry standards, and thus that it was
refusing delivery of the corn. Bathilda further alleged that the price of corn had risen over the summer
to $3.00/bushel, and sought damages in the amount of the difference between the agreed-upon price
and the summer price, namely, 150,000 * ($3.00 - $2.00) = $150,000.00.
Despite the refusal to accept the corn, Bathilda's complaint sought an emergency order from the
California FLDRA for immediate seizure of 150,000 bushels of the corn "so that it can be immediately
sold on the open market as livestock feed, so that there shall not be a total loss of economic value of the
corn." Bathilda's complaint further stated a claim for breach of contract, stating that Florean's failure
to deliver acceptable corn constituted breach of its obligations under the January, 2018 agreement.
Bathilda included a copy of the contract and an affidavit signed under penalty of perjury from its CEO,
Mr. Thomas Riddle, which consisted in its entirety of the following sentence, "I understand, based on
the statements made to me by my employees, that the facts in the Bathilda complaint against Florean
Fortescue are true and correct."

On the morning of September 19, California FLDRA administrative law judge Wilhelmina Grubbly-Plank
contacted Bathilda's attorney by internet message stating, "Please provide proof of notice to
Fortescue." Bathilda's attorneys then uploaded electronic copies of three emails through which the
Fortescue-Bathilda contract had been negotiated (showing the use of the
FloreanFortescueFarmer(at)gmail.com email address); an email it had sent to Florean at the same email
address in the morning of September 18 attaching the documents uploaded to the California FLDRA
internet site; and an automatically generated return email from Google stating that the email address
FloreanFortescueFarmer(at)gmail.com had been deleted at the request of the owner due to a hack of
the account.

In the afternoon of September 19, ALJ Grubbly-Plank issued an order directing an FLDRA marshal (a
marshal is an enforcement officer of the court) to seize 150,000 bushels of corn from the Sacramento
facility, to sell them on the open market, and to deposit the proceeds of the sale in a specified bank
account that the California FLDRA controlled. ALJ Grubbly-Plank's also included a schedule for filings in
Bathilda's breach of contract claim. Each party's initial submission of documents and affidavits were to
be uploaded onto the California FLDRA's website by October 19th, 2018. Written arguments, and any
rebuttal evidence, from both sides would be due on November 2. The California FLDRA division would
issue its final order on November 9, 2018.

Also in the afternoon of September 19, Tonks' inspector sent an email to
FloreanFortescueFarmer(at)gmail.com stating that Tonks had also decided to refuse the corn on the
grounds that its condition was not industry standard. Tonks' inspector immediately received an
automated delivery-failure email from Google similar to that received by Bathilda.

In the morning of September 20, the FLDRA marshal posted signs that 150,000 bushels of corn would be
up for auction on September 25.

Kreacher, who up until then had been unable to keep track of what was going on, tried to stop the
posting of the signs, but when the marshal showed him ALJ Grubbly-Plank's order, he realized that he
could do nothing. Kreacher did his best. He wrote out a description of the events as he had witnessed
them and faxed the description, using a machine available at the Sacramento facility, to a number he
looked up on the internet for the office of his boss's farm. Immediately after sending the fax, he called
the farm office at a number listed on the same Internet screen as the fax number and reached Florian to
give him additional details.

You were a lawyer who had represented Florean in the past. Florean called you in the afternoon of
September 20, 2018. Florean was in an absolute rage. He gotten wind of Bathild's suit from Kreacher,
and had eventually been able to download the documents Bathilda filed, as well as Judge Grubbly-
Plank's seizure order, from the FLDRA website, and he provided them to you via a new email. He told
you that Kreacher's notes documented that the corn was in perfectly fine condition when it arrived at
the Sacramento facility, so that he should not have suffered the near-total loss of the value of his corn.
Further, in his view, the contract specified that he could not be held liable if he failed to deliver due to
events beyond his control. Finally, Florean showed you a faxed letter he received earlier that day from
Tonks, Inc. In the letter, Tonks stated that it intended to observe the results of the Bathilda-Florean
litigation in the California FLDRA division, and that if Bathilda prevailed, Tonks would sue Florean in
Oregon state court for breach of contract for 50,000 * ($3.00 - $2.00) = $50,000.00. Florean hated the
whole idea of litigating this issue in the FLDRA, which he called a "kangaroo court." He wanted to know
the following:

    a) Upon what basis could he challenge or argue against the seizure and auction order, either within
         the California FLDRA division or in a separate lawsuit? (Note: limit your answers to principles
         we discussed in Civil Procedure, not to any ideas you learned in any other class.)

    b) Were there any grounds to challenge the way that the FLDRA's litigation mechanisms would
         allow the case to go forward, or the way in which Judge Grubbly-Plank proposed to proceed, or
         to prevent the FDLRA division from litigating the breach of contract claim entirely?

    c) Suppose Florean presented evidence and argument contending that the corn was in fine
         condition when it arrived in Sacramento, and that in any event the contract excused any
         delivery failure caused by circumstances beyond his control, specifically, the bridge closure. But
         suppose the California FLDRA division nevertheless ruled against him on the breach of contract
         action and ordered him to pay damages, finding specifically that under the contract, the bridge
         closure was not an excuse for his failure to deliver market-quality corn.
             I. Would Tonks be able to make any use of California FLDRA division's decision in a
                   subsequent lawsuit by Tonks against Florean in Oregon state court?
            II. Could he, Florean, make any use of the California FLDRA division's adjudication in a
                   subsequent lawsuit by Tonks against him in Oregon state court?

What is your response?

Question 2 (20 points):

Reread the fact pattern and remind yourself where Question 1 began. The facts assumed in Question 1
are no longer true. Thus, assume that at this point, the corn has just arrived at the Sacramento facility.
Kreacher called Florean at the farm office to say that, in his view, the corn was none the worse for wear,
meaning that it conformed with industry standard. Because of the two-week delay in delivery, Kreacher
had called ahead, and had arranged for three corn industry experts not associated with any of the
contracting parties to be on site on September 17 to inspect the corn. All three agreed with Kreacher
that the corn was in perfectly fine condition.

Also, assume for the purposes of Question 2 only that prior to the initiation of these facts, Congress
repealed Section 30(b), above, so that a plaintiff could proceed on a claim relating to goods derived from
federal property in any otherwise appropriate state court, otherwise appropriate federal court, or within
the FLDRA.

Initially, the Bathilda and Tonks inspectors agreed. On September 18, Bathilda and Tonks each emailed
Florean stating that they accepted the corn. On the basis of this email, Bathilda and Tonks wired the
amount of the purchase price for their contracts to a Florean Fortescue bank account, which credited
the money to Florean. On September 19, however, both Bathilda and Tonks handed Kreacher, still in
Sacramento, a "Letter." The Letter was composed in California, at Bathilda's headquarters. According
to the Letter, further inspection of the corn by Bathilda and Tonks, and an attempt to sell the corn to
retail customers, had revealed that the two-week wait in the heat had damaged the corn. The corn
could be sold, but only at a discounted price of $1.25 per bushel. The Letter demanded that Florean pay
150,000 * ($2.00 - $1.25) = $112,500 to Bathilda, and that Florean pay 50,000 * ($2.00 - $1.25) =
$37,500. Otherwise, the Letter stated, Bathilda and Tonks would sue Florean in court. Kreacher had
immediately texted a photo of the demand letter to Florean. (Note: to the extent that there is a
horizontal choice of law issue vis-à-vis the Florean-Bathilda/Tonks relationship, this Letter, if deemed
unlawful, would be the last event necessary to constitute a breach.)

You were a lawyer Florean has consulted in the past. Florean called you in a rage. He told you that that
Bathilda's and Tonk's Letter constituted a breach of contract. The corn was fine, Florean tells you.
Florean does not want to wait to be sued. Instead, Florean wants to sue in federal court, seeking a
declaratory judgment that he owed nothing to Bathilda or Tonks.

Florean was also in a rage about Chang Corp.'s closure of the Hogsmeade bridge. If he had to pay
anything to Bathilda and Tonks, then Chang Corp. should have to pay that full amount, meaning
$150,000.00.

Moreover, Florean tells you, he was not alone in suffering from Chang Corp.'s incompetence. He tells
you that there were a group of about 45 independent farmers in northwestern Oregon who had the
same trouble with the Hogsmeade bridge. These farmers lost crop loads of different sorts of vegetables
and fruits (with different susceptibility to prolonged heat) in values of $5,000.00 to $60,000.00, all
because of the delay that the Hogsmeade bridge outage caused. The crops were headed to distribution
points, for sale to different companies, across the country. The Hogsmeade Bridge closure was a total
disaster.
Florean wanted to know the following:

    · Could Florean sue Bathilda Corp., Tonks Corp., and Chang Corp. in a single lawsuit in some
         federal court? He wants to request a declaratory judgment of no breach of contract against
         Bathilda and Tonks, and wants to state a claim against Chang Corp. for interfering with the
         contracts with Bathilda and Tonks. Can he do so? (Do not concern yourself with whether, as a
         matter of substantive contract or tort law, any of these theories of relief would succeed.)

    · Assuming (which might not be true) that Florean can sue all three in the same lawsuit in some
         federal court, where (geographically) could he sue, and where would he be most likely to
         succeed?

    · Assuming Florean can sue all of these parties in some federal court, he wants to move for an
         immediate preliminary injunction preventing Bathilda and Tonks from taking any further action
         about the sale of the corn, such as filing a new lawsuit. Would such a motion succeed?

    · Assuming Florean can sue in some federal court, he wants to request that the court certify a
         class of independent farmers in northwestern Oregon against Chang Corp. for interference with
         their sales contracts for their products. Is the court likely to certify the class? (If you need more
         information here, specify what information you need.)

Question 3 (10 points)
Assuming that the FLDRA divisions can be constitutionally administered, is this DPDFLA scheme a good
idea? Why or why not?

Question 4 (10 points)

Now assume that section 30(b), above, is part of the statutory scheme (meaning that there was no
repeal of it). Suppose that the FLDRA invoked its power under Section 32, above, to promulgate a
regulation stating that for all claims asserted in an FLDRA division, the applicable limitations period shall
be one year. But state limitations periods are typically longer for most causes of action. Thus, suppose
a claimant filed an action in an FLDRA division 18 months after the relevant facts, in compliance with an
otherwise applicable two-year state-law limitations period. The defendant in the FLDRA division claim
argues that the claim should fail for failure to comply with one-year regulatory limitations period. Apply
the Erie tests we discussed in class to the question of whether an FLDRA division should dismiss such a
case, or allow it to go forward. In addition, discuss how some of the justifications for the Erie doctrine
we reviewed in class inform this question.

## 2018-final · Instructor model answer

Question 1

The emergency order is probably unconstitutional.   To begin, Bathilda’s efforts to provide notice of the proceeding, and thus of the emergency filing, fail under Flowers: the email delivery failure notification should have prompted Bathilda to investigate further reasonable steps, such as a quick internet search for Florean’s fax or telephone number.  Kreacher showed both of which were easily available.

Moreover, section 31(a)(3) does not require any sworn testimony, referring only to “affidavits or sworn statements, if any.”  True, section 31(a)(2) requires that the claimant file any relevant documents in her possession, and an ALJ (not a clerk) makes the decision.  But in many FLDRA cases (including this case), the key issue (as here, the corn’s condition) will turn on testimony, not documents (Doehr).  There is no requirement that the defendant be allowed to submit evidence and argument when time allows (Florean had no opportunity here).  There is no claimant bond requirement, and no counterbond procedure allowing the defendant to regain possession (Doehr, concurring opinion).  The claimant need not (and did not here) have any underlying property right.  Section 31 does not articulate what would count for urgency justifying seizure (such as spoliation of goods).  Nothing says that a seizure order be followed by a speedy adjudication (although the procedure here was expeditious).

Florean can prevent the FLDRA division from litigating the case (including the emergency order) by demanding a jury trial.  Bathilda’s states an ordinary common law breach claim for damages, for which there has always been a jury trial right (no need for a Chauffeurs analysis).  The FLDRA division cannot sit a jury.  No public rights exception applies because no government entity acting as a sovereign/regulator is involved.

Regarding the overall FLDRA procedure, there is probably constitutional SMJ due to minimal diversity (easily present) and derivation of corn from federal land (sufficient under the expansive reading of Art 3 in Osborne), and the statute’s jurisdictional grant invokes the Article 3 constitutional power.  Under 29(b), there is specific IPJ due to the CA longarm (limits of constitution) plus the minimal contacts (corn sale in CA) plus fairness/reasonableness factors, such as the testimonial evidence from individuals in California, the contracting with a CA-based party, and CA’s interest in disposition of property within CA.

Whether Tonks could use ONMCE to preclude Florean, e.g., from denying that the Hogsmeade bridge closure is not a sufficient excuse for the breach probably depends on whether the FLDRA division procedures comply with the R2 of Judgments Section 83(2), meaning PDP norms.  If so, then under federal common law, which matches the state law of Oregon (and California, if needed, Semtek), ONMCE would probably be allowed because Section 29(g) prevented Tonks from joining the Bathilda-Florean FLDRA proceeding.  The other elements of issue preclusion are met:  same issue per immediately above (see specific FLDRA finding of no excuse); Florean would litigate both hard to avoid liability; and were liability imposed, decision on the bridge closure defense would necessary to an award for Bathilda.  So we turn to a procedural due process analysis, which we would need anyway to assess the constitutionality of a California FLDRA division judgment in the Bathilda-Florean proceeding.

The California FLDRA’s procedures probably are sufficient under Section 83(2) and PDP minimums.  Aside from Flowers issue discussed above, the notice procedure calls for reasonable efforts, the Mullane standard, satisfying 83(2)(a).  Under section 29(f) as illustrated by Judge Grubbly-Plank’s order, there is a right to present evidence and rebut the opposing parties’ evidence with legal argument and evidence, satisfying 83(2)(b).  There is no indication that FLDRA divisions will create new law, and Judge Grubbly-Plank’s order specifies a moment of final judgment, satisfying 83(2)(c-d).  The FLDRA does not allow discovery, a live hearing, an appeal, or legal assistance at state expense, but the Restatement requires none of these things, nor does the DPC in a purely commercial dispute (contrast Vitek (liberty) and Goldberg (subsistence)).

It is unlikely that Florean could use claim preclusion to obtain summary judgment in a suit by Tonks in Oregon state court.  The FLDRA division judgment would be final and on the merits, and Tonks is likely in privity with Bathilda because although it did not control the Bathilda litigation, it did step into Bathilda’s shoes with respect to legal interests when it purchased the right to corn from the same batch as Bathilda and agreed to be bound by the contract.  But Tonks’ 50,000 bushels are probably a different claim from Bathilda’s 50,000 bushels; although Gonzalez is to the contrary, a holding of same claim here would inhibit a market for corn (like bonds).  The fact that some corn is substandard does not necessarily mean that other corn is substandard, suggesting a degree of factual separation.

Question 2

Florean can sue Bathilda, Tonks, and Chang for a declaratory judgment of no breach of contract or, in the alternative, that Change is responsible for any breach (because of tortious interference) in a single federal court lawsuit.  Breach of contract and tortious interference are state-law claims, so there is no AUJ.  Complete diversity exists (Florean = OR, Bathilda = DE & CA, Tonks = DE & ID, Chang = DE & WA), but the dispute with Tonks is only $37,500.00 < $75K, so that claim would have to be supplemental to either the Bathilda (AIC = $112,500 > $75K) or the Chang (AIC = $150K > $75K) dispute.  The Tonks dispute is easily part of the same case or controversy as the Bathilda dispute (same corn harvest and shipment), 1367(a), and no 1367(c) factor suggests refusal to exercise supplemental jurisdiction.  But this would be a claim by a plaintiff (Florean) against a person (Tonks) made a party under FRCP 20, which would appear to be prohibited by 1367(b).  There a miniscule hope that a court would allow the suit on the theory that all that Exxon/1367(a-b) focused on was complete diversity (per the Exxon contamination passage).  The better argument is that this is a declaratory judgment suit, and the coercive action/imaginary lawsuit would have been a multi-plaintiff (Bathilda, Tonks) suit against Florean, permissible under Exxon’s interpretation of the 1367.

Under FRCP 20, Florean’s claim against Bathilda, Tonks, and Chang probably arise from the same transaction or occurrence (note that this means the scope of the T&O test under Rule 20 is probably different from the T&O test for claim preclusion, which makes sense given that Rule 20 is for litigation efficiency, and the clam preclusion in this situation is concerned with markets) in the sense of a common corn shipment affected by a common bridge closure. There are common issues relating to each of the defendants, including the legality and effect of the bridge closure.  If necessary, Florean could sue Bathilda and Tonks for a declaratory judgment, wait for them to counterclaim under FRCP 13, then implead Chang under Rule 14 to shift liability.

Florean can sue all three in D. DE. for sure, perhaps also in D. OR.  Under 4(k)(1)(A), there would be no IPJ over Bathilda or Tonks in Idaho (no indication of any contacts), no IPJ over Chang in California (only does business in OR and WA), and no IPJ over Bathilda in WA (no indication of any contacts).  DE has GIPJ over all three, the state of incorporation, and thus 1391(b)(1) venue.  OR’s long-arm goes to the limits of the constitution.  Minimal contacts exist as to Bathilda (purposeful availment to do business via a contract with Florean) and Chang (bridge job in Hogsmeade).  Tonks, which never directly contracted with an OR entity is less clear.  The best argument is that it agreed to be bound by a contract with an OR farmer knowing that it would need direct interaction with that OR farmer (the decision to accept/reject the corn).  If minimal contacts over Tonks were found, it would be fair and reasonable for OR to exercise IPJ because the key events of the bridge closure occurred there (thus evidence located there), plus OR has an interest in providing Florean, an OR resident, a convenient forum and in regulating the bridge closure events.  The bridge closure would provide 1391(b)(2) venue in D. OR.

D. DE would be a better place to file than D. OR.  IPJ & venue in D. DE. are beyond question there.  Under Klaxon, D. OR would apply OR’s R1 HCOL rules.  The last event necessary to give rise to the alleged breach of contract, and thus Chang’s derivative liability, was the Letter, which occurred (was delivered) in CA.  But CA requires intentionality for a tortious interference claim, and the facts probably do not support the theory that Chang intentionally interference with the Florean-Bathilda/Tonks contracts.  Edgecombe showed negligent indifference to the damage she could cause farmers by not scheduling the bridge repairs in the winter at time with no harvest, but did not intend to cause the harm.  Delaware, however, is an HCOL R2 state, which would start with a presumption of CA law, but would look to the center of gravity of the relationship between, and events linking, Florean and Chang.  That’s OR, where the bridge closure occurred.  Per Babcock, OR has a much stronger interest in regulating the legality of conduct that occurred in OR, and OR has an interest in protecting an OR resident.  OR requires only negligence.

The preliminary injunction motion will be denied because there is no irreparable harm.  This is a standard commercial dispute about a fungible commodity (corn), and damages or the lack thereof are enough to redress any wrongs. There is no need for urgency.  Florean might be likely to succeed on the merits (because of the three experts).  It’s hard to see how the balance of the equities in this private commercial tips in one way or the other.  For the same reason, the public interest is unaffected, and thus does not counsel for an injunction.

Re the class action, Florean is diverse from Chang (Ben-Hur) and has more than $75K at issue, providing the anchor for SSMJ over the unnamed class members’ suits (Exxon).  There is IPJ D. Or and 1391(b)(2) venue over all plaintiffs’ actions because of the bridge closure issue common to all.  On the class certification motion, we have numerosity (45 is too many for joinder), and Florean’s claim is probably representative and typical of the class because he suffered from the same event as they did, but it is unlikely that common issues predominate (predominance swallows commonality in this (b)(3) damages class) or that the class action is superior.  Although the bridge closure is common to all class members, the underlying contracts likely all have different contract terms about excuse; and the crops at issue have different prices, different markets, and would have different spoilage rates after two weeks in the sun.

Question 3

(Policy Question, lots of ways to go)

Matthews v. Eldridge reflects an exclusive focus on cost and accuracy.  The FLDRA online dispute resolution system could reduce litigation costs for the judiciary and parties by eliminating discovery, hearings, and appeals (items the R2 Judgments deemed superfluous to a PDP-providing scheme), and by allowing electronic litigation from one’s living room.  The scheme should be speedier than Article 3 courts.  Issues of public import will rarely arise in the kind of private commercial disputes likely to fill most of the divisions’ dockets.  The relaxation of the rules of evidence and the other formality-driving trappings of the proceedings, as well as the online submission of all documents and evidence, should promote access to justice for small businesses who struggle to afford lawyers.  Online notice is long overdue (the law in this area must evolve, see Flowers, regular versus certified mail).

The cost in accuracy of adjudication is hard to predict.  Live testimony versus written transcripts is an unresolved battle.  The elimination of discovery might tilt adjudication results towards defendants, as plaintiffs often have greater need for discovery, but by how much?  Specialized judges may gain expertise and thus accuracy (but weren’t state judges already expert on these mostly state-law commercial cases?).

There are at least reasons to question this scheme, apart from concerns of cost and accuracy.  Issues of public import (constitutional issues) will sometimes arise, and when they do, there is no requirement of a written justifications (see Rule 52 justifications) and no appeal to allow orderly development of the law.  Next, the prohibition of joinder and class actions may make some adjudication less efficient and decrease access to justice for those with low-value claims.  Finally, are disputes related to goods related to federal public land a priority for access to justice and efficiency?  Some such disputes will involve small farmers who might struggle to afford lawyers, but commercial entities can better afford and navigate ordinary litigation than low-income individuals.  The commercial disputes likely to dominate the agency’s docket usually involve contractual relationships, in which case the parties can contract for arbitration or other cost-reducing adjudicatory procedures.  Why here?

Question 4

Regarding Erie, and limitations, the outcome-determinative test suggests using state law because the case would be dismissed under federal law and would continue under state law.  The Harlan analysis depends on whether one thinks that limitations periods affect investment strategies (if yes, then use state law, if no, then use federal) or other prelitigation conduct.  The problems are with Hanna majority.  Under the Scalia approach, if one uses the Sibbach test to interpret the “abridge, enlarge, modify” language in Section 32 (tracking 2072), then the FLDRA limitations period is validly promulgated under the statute and obviously applicable.  Under Ginsburgh’s approach, it is hard to see how to render the limitations period inapplicable, and a holding that limitations periods involve substantive rights (per York) would suggest that it is invalid under Section 32.

Stepping back, the overarching problem is that we don’t have an Erie framework applicable to an agency regulation.  Erie’s federalism rationale suggests that the source of the federal law at issue should not matter, but Hanna and history contradict this rationale.  The equal protection rationale is fully applicable to the present fact pattern because a plaintiff can file in state court or the FLDRA, so the plaintiff has a choice of what law to apply even in cases outside of a defendant’s home state (assuming Article 3 power under Osborne).  But, again, the equal protection rationale simply reasks the question how much vertical disuniformity we can tolerate.  Section 30(c) mirrors the Rules of Decision Act, and Section 32 mirrors the REA, so the statutory/what-is-law rationales are fully operational.  But again, how does this apply to an agency regulation?  The transplantation of the permissive Sibbach test to the FLDRA framework would allow the agency to promulgate anything arguably procedural.  While the Sibbach test is atextual and theoretically unsatisfying, it seems to work reasonably well with the federal courts, and might function well here.  We need a theory of the importance of state law in the administrative state.

# EXAM 2019-final — 2019 Greiner Civil Procedure 2 Final Exam

## 2019-final · Exam text

Civil Procedure 6
                                                     Fall 2019

                                         Professor D. James Greiner

                              The exam mode for this exam is TAKEHOME

Available for download: Thursday, December 12 at 8am

Must be electronically submitted by: Thursday, December 12, 8 hours from download or by
4:30pm, whichever is earlier

This exam 11 pages long. Please check to see that you have all 11 pages.

There are six unequally weighted questions.

Exam4 will automatically print your Anonymous ID and word count on the exam.

Word limit: 2500 words

1. HLS will administer the exam according to standard one-day take-home time limits. Spend a
substantial portion of your exam time getting organized before writing. You will botch up this
exam if you don't take the time to get organized before you start writing.

2. Your response must be typed using the Exam4 exam software. Your response can be no
more than the word limit above, everything included. Should you depart from the length
limit, the first portion of your exam, up to the word limit above, will be graded and the rest
ignored. HLS will administer the exam delivery and submission system.

3. You may not communicate with any person other than the exam administrators regarding the
substance of this exam until after the 1L exam period is over. The reason for this prohibition
is that, each year, some students take the exam on a date other than its scheduled
administration date. Thus, do not think that you may discuss this exam immediately after
you hand it in. Wait until the 1L exam period is over. Note also that the prohibition above
includes librarians. According to HLS policy: "[S]tudents may not communicate with the
instructor nor raise questions or concerns about the content of an examination once it is in
progress and the Registrar's Office will not disseminate additional information about an exam
once it is in progress. To maintain anonymity and to ensure compliance by students with this
policy, instructors should not communicate in any way, including through email, with students
during an examination."

4. If you feel that there is a mistake in the exam, such as that a question is unclear or that it
refers to something that it should not or that there is a critical typographical error, then do the
following. First, in your answer, explain what you believe to be the mistake. Second, explain
the implications of the mistake. Third, make an assumption that is reasonable given the fact
pattern and applicable law to resolve the error. Fourth, proceed with the remainder of the exam.
5. Your answer must be your own work. Any quotation from any source must be accompanied
by a corresponding reference (but use short references, e.g., "International Shoe," or "FRCP
23").

6. Each question's weight is specified (out of 65 total points). Time and word-count may be
issues for you during the exam. Answer questions efficiently, and feel free to take into account
the number of points available for each question. Don't forget your test-taking skills.

7. Do not put your name on your response.

8. Deal with all issues fairly raised, even if you think one or more would be dispositive in the
real world.

9. If you believe additional information (factual or legal) is needed to deal with an issue, say so
and make explicit whatever assumptions you need to proceed, but make these assumptions
reasonable and interesting. Similarly, if at any point in this question you find that you do not
know what the law is on a particular subject, identify the issue as to which there is uncertainty,
then discuss the various options as to what the law could or should be, noting the advantages or
disadvantages of each option.

10. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: "SMJ" for subject matter
jurisdiction; "IPJ" for in personam jurisdiction; "DJ" for diversity jurisdiction; "FQJ" for federal
question jurisdiction; "CNOF" for common nucleus of operative facts; and "DPC" for due
process clause.

11. All of the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the
law described in this exam. Use the scenarios only for the purposes of this exam.

                              ********************************

You are a junior associate in the law firm of Lestrange, Dolohoff, and Karkaroff. On May 6, 2019,
Quirinus Quirrell, a partner in your firm, drops the following complaint in your desk.

 ***********************************************************************************

[Beginning of complaint]

________________________________________________

United States District Court
for the District of Massachusetts

Case Number 2019-CV-000713
HG Corporation, Inc., plaintiff                   May 6, 2019
         versus

Tom Marvolo Riddle and Dave Droobles, defendants
________________________________________________

Plaintiff, HG Corporation, Inc., hereby alleges as follows.

    1. This Court has subject matter jurisdiction pursuant 28 U.S.C. section 1332.

    2. Venue is proper under 28 U.S.C. section 1391.

Parties

    3. Plaintiff HG Corporation, Inc. is a Massachusetts corporation with its principle place of business
         in Boston, Massachusetts. Its principal shareholders are Harry Potter and Ginny Weasley, who
         live and work in Boston. Harry owns 65% of HG stock, Ginny owns 5%, and each of 20 other
         shareholders owns a 1.5% stake. These 20 other shareholders are spread across the west coast,
         living all over California, Washington, and Oregon. Harry runs the business from Ginny's and his
         home in Boston, and he serves as the Chief Executive Officer and Chair of the Board during all
         events relevant to this complaint. Board meetings occur once a year in Harry and Ginny's house
         in Boston. Ginny is Vice President of the Board, and each of the other 20 shareholders has a
         seat on the Board.

    4. Defendant Tom Marvolo Riddle is a citizen of Rhode Island.

    5. Defendant Dave Droobles is a citizen of New Hampshire.

Facts

HG And Its Business Model

    6. HG's business model involves monetizing the cult of Harry's personality. HG runs a website, a
         Facebook page, an Instagram account, a Twitter feed, and various other social media accounts
         and outlets. Harry uses these vectors to post photographs he takes; short videos that he makes
         of his encounters with people he meets on the street, often while he is in disguise; and other
         short videos in which Harry provides his opinion on current events and the quality of various
         products available on the market. All of the photographs and videos Harry makes feature
         recognizable landmarks in Boston or elsewhere in Massachusetts, such as the Boston Common,
         the Public Garden in downtown Boston, beaches at Martha's Vineyard (a Massachusetts island),
         Boston's Bunker Hill, Boston's Museum of Fine Arts, and the Berkshire Mountains (in western
         Massachusetts).

    7. HG generates revenue by selling space for advertisements on its social media accounts. Those
         ads are exclusively for products that Harry endorses on its social media accounts. HG never
         accepts an ad solicitation until Harry has first tested/used the product and decided whether he
         likes it. Because it sells no products of its own, HG depends entirely on the Internet traffic it
         drives to its social media accounts. As such, its reputation is essential to its business.

    8. A key component of HG's business model is the public image and reputation that neither HG,
         nor Harry, nor anyone else associated with HG ever accepts money to buy Harry's endorsement.
         A banner on HG's website and its Facebook page say, "HG Is Your Independent Voice. Gringotts
         Bank Doesn't Have Enough Gold To Buy Us." HG endorses only products he buys in
         Massachusetts (where Harry can easily obtain them), although those products may also be sold
         elsewhere. When HG endorses a product, Harry posts videos of himself consuming or otherwise
         using it in a recognizable Massachusetts location, such as the various Massachusetts landmarks
         identified above.

HG's Endorsement of Droobles Best Blowing Gum

    9. One of the products HG endorsed in the fall of 2018 was Drooble's Best Blowing Gum, a brand of
         gourmet chewing gum manufactured by defendant David Droobles, a business man, along with
         the employees he hires to work in a plant on his personal estate in Manchester, NH. The
         Droobles gum manufacturing business is not incorporated. Instead, defendant David Droobles
         operates the facility with approximately six employees who work in a building constructed in the
         back yard of his large estate.

    10. In November of 2018, Harry posted a video of himself, in disguise, handing out free samples of
         Droobles to kids on the Boston Common, then interviewing those kids to ask them what they
         thought. Droobles was popular with kids aged 8-12. The video concluded with Harry unmasking
         himself, to the delight of the children who had gathered for the free gum samples, and saying,
         "As you can see, Massachusetts loves you, Droobles Best Blowing Gum. We give you five
         bubbles out of five!."

The Riddle Offer to Buy HG

    11. In December of 2018, Harry received an email from defendant Tom Marvolo Riddle, an investor
         who personally buys and sells successful companies. The email offered to buy all of the stock in
         HG, Inc., and asked Harry to name a price to begin negotiations. Intrigued, Harry sought bids
         from other investment firms to see what the best offer might be. The highest offer he received
         was for $5,000,000. Harry emailed Riddle an offer to begin negotiations based on a minimum
         price of five million dollars. Riddle returned an email offering to begin negotiations with a
         maximum price of $4,300,000. Harry replied with an email refusing the offer and terminating
         the negotiation. Riddle ended the exchange with an email dated January 2, 2019, saying, "You
         should have taken my offer, Harry. Very shortly, your company will be worth less than my
         maximum offer. I'll make sure of that."

The Injury to HG's Business Reputation

    12. On or about February 2 of 2019, Harry received an email from a friend with a link to a Youtube
         video. The video was a short clip. It showed what purported to be a conversation in a
         conference room at the Droobles estate between Harry and defendant Droobles. In the
         conference room conversation, Harry purportedly proposed that defendant Droobles pay Harry
         $50,000 in exchange for Harry's endorsement of Droobles' Best Blowing Gum on "all of my
         social media accounts." The video ended with defendant Droobles saying, "I accept that." The
         video was exactly five minutes and 22 seconds long.

    13. In fact, Harry has never set foot on the Droobles estate. Harry has never communicated in any
         way with defendant Droobles. Harry has never met defendant Droobles in person, and has
         never been to the Droobles headquarters.

    14. This video is a "deepfake," a highly realistic fake video.

Harry Investigates And Receives an Email Implicating Defendants

    15. Harry immediately posted a video on the HG website denouncing the Harry/David video as a
         deepfake and asking anyone with information about it to come forward. Harry also hired a
         private investigation firm to attempt to seek further information about the video and about
         defendants Riddle and Droobles.

    16. On February 8, 2019, Harry received an email from an anonymous gmail account. The email
         recited that its author was, and remained, an employee of defendant Droobles, working in the
         gum manufacturing operation. The email author wished to remain anonymous so as to avoid
         losing their job. The author reported that in late December, they had been called into the
         conference room (on defendant Droobles' estate) shown on the Harry/David video. When the
         author entered the room, their boss, Mr. Droobles, had introduced the only other person in the
         conference room as "Mr. Tom,", saying, "Mr. Tom and I are going to have a conversation, and I'd
         like to have a record of exactly what was said, so there's no dispute later." Mr. Droobles handed
         the email author his own personal smartphone and showed how to record a video on it. The
         email author recorded a video in the conference room, approximately five minutes in length, in
         which Mr. Tom proposed that Mr. Droobles pay Mr. Tom $50,000 in exchange for Riddle's
         endorsement of Droobles' Best Blowing Gum on "all of my social media accounts." The video
         ended with Mr. Droobles saying, "I don't accept that." The email included no further
         information.

    17. Harry's subsequent attempts, through the efforts of the private investigation firm he had
         previously hired and through his own efforts, to find out more information about the email
         author or the video the email author recorded were unsuccessful. Neither defendant Riddle nor
         defendant Droobles responded to multiple attempts, via multiple methods, to contact them for
         more information.

Defendants' Intentional Conduct Caused Business Harm

    18. Beginning in March of 2019, HG's advertisement revenue dropped. During the summer of 2019,
         Harry received five offers to buy all of the stock in HG. The highest of those offers was $3,
         900,000.

    19. In the middle of August 2019, Harry received an email from defendant Riddle, once again
         offering to buy all of the stock of HG. In this email, Riddle stated that his best, highest, and final
         offer was $4,000.000.
    20. On information and belief, Harry, Ginny, and all shareholders of HG believe as follows:
         Defendants Droobles and Riddle reached an agreement in a conversation not captured on video
         to implement a scheme to harm HG's business so that Riddle could buy HG's stock a discounted
         price. The scheme must have taken roughly the following shape. Riddle paid Droobles some
         unknown sum of money for a copy of the video that the anonymous email author recorded.
         Riddle, either by himself or with the assistance of some tech-savvy individual, altered the video
         so as to replace Riddle's own image in the video with Harry's image and to change "I do not
         accept" in the end of the conversation to "I accept." Either Riddle or Droobles or both
         subsequently posted, or caused to be posted, the resulting deepfake video on youtube. These
         actions were intended to cause HG harm in the form of a lower business valuation, making the
         business a less expensive acquisition target for Riddle.

Count I: Business Disparagement Against Defendant Riddle

The actions described above constitute the tort of Business Disparagement. Defendant Riddle engaged
in a wrongful scheme to damage HG's business reputation by participating in the creation of, and/or
propagating, a false video.

Count II: Business Disparagement Against Defendant Droobles

The actions described above constitute the tort of Business Disparagement. Defendant Droobles
engaged in a wrongful scheme to damage HG's business reputation by participating in the creation of,
and/or propagating, a false video.

Prayer for Relief

Wherefore, plaintiff requests that this court order defendant Riddle to pay to plaintiff $950,000, and
defendant Droobles to pay to plaintiff $50,000.

Signed,

/s/

McGonagall & Flitwick, Attorneys for plaintiff

[End of complaint]

 ***********************************************************************************

The complaint was accompanied by a standard summons form, but nothing else.

Quirrell informs you that Lestrange, Dolohoff, and Karkaroff (your law firm) has been retained by
defendant Droobles1 to represent him in this matter.

1 To make 100% sure that this is clear, David Droobles is a human being, a person. In this exam, there is no
Droobles corporation or association or partnership or other incorporeal entity. Similarly, Riddle is a human being,
not a business.
You hire your own private investigation firms, talk to the law firm representing Tom Riddle, make some
telephone calls, and do some legal research. You discover the following (remembering that the
allegations in the complaint are only allegations, not proven facts).

First, your client, David Droobles, the businessman, was born in New Hampshire and has lived there his
entire life. He started his gum business when he was 17, quit school, and has made the business his
life's passion. He is registered to vote in New Hampshire and pays taxes there. Meanwhile, Tom Riddle
was born in Rhode Island and lived there through high school, until he went to college at UMass Boston.
When he graduated from college, he attended Harvard Business School, and decided he liked
Massachusetts. At age 25, upon graduating from business school, using money he borrowed from his
parents, he started his investing business. At first, he lived at home in Rhode Island with his parents,
with whom he has always stayed close. But over the next 18 months, as his business became more and
more successful, he bought a luxury apartment in downtown Boston, where he set up his business office
and conducts his meetings. He sleeps in the Boston apartment on weekdays, and returns home on the
weekends to his parents' house. He has always been registered to vote in Rhode Island, but he started
paying Massachusetts income taxes as soon as he earned money, an arrangement he has continued for
the past 15 years.

Second, Droobles' gum business depends on purchases of raw materials from all over the United States;
Droobles orders these materials online. Regarding sales, Droobles' operates a sell-by-Internet business
model. Customers visit www.DroooblesBestBlowingGum.com, create personal accounts in which they
provide their addresses but not their financial information, and order their gum, using paypal or a
similar payment service. Droobles ships to the address provided by the customer, in the volume
requested by the customer. By volume of sales, approximately 25% of Droobles' products are shipped
to Massachusetts, 20% to New York, 20% to California, 10% each to Florida and Texas. The rest is
sprinkled around various of the United States. Droobles buys no advertisements, and he maintains his
website personally. Droobles maintains no salesforce, and he solicits no business; it depends on word of
mouth referrals to sell gum.

Riddle, meanwhile, buys and invests in business all over the world. Most of the businesses he invests in
are incorporated in Delaware, but their principle places of business are located in more than 30 states
and 15 foreign nations. He uses bank and investment accounts from Boston-based financial institutions
so that he can use public transportation or app-rides to visit their locations (Riddle never learned to
drive). Riddles' next worth is approximately $150,000,000.

Third, the law in all fifty-one United States includes the tort of "Business Disparagement." Under this
tort, a defendant is liable to a plaintiff if the defendant

    (i) utters (orally or in writing), or produces, or participates in the production of,
    (ii) a false or misleading statement or omission, or false image, or false video,
    (iii) which harms the reputation, public perception, or standing of a business, and
    (iv) which harm to reputation, perception, or standing causes a loss of value to a business,

              corporation, or partnership.
Under the law in all fifty-one states, a harmed business may sue for the tort of Business Disparagement,
asserting as damages the amount of the lost value to the business or corporation caused by the false
statement or video. Also, any shareholder or partial owner of a harmed business may sue for the tort of
Business Disparagement, asserting as damages the fraction of that individual's ownership in the
business multiplied by the amount of the lost value to the business or corporation caused by the false
statement or video. The last event necessary to complete the Business Disparagement tort is the
infliction of the harm, in the form of the loss of value by the business.

Finally, Massachusetts, unlike any other state in the United States, provides an affirmative defense to
the Business Disparagement tort called the "Public Access" defense. Under Massachusetts law, the
Public Access defense means that if a defendant proves that the affected business has constant and
ready access to the public or to media outlets, then the plaintiff cannot recover.2 This defense applies
regardless of whether the plaintiff is the affected business itself or one of its shareholders, so long as the
affected business has constant and ready access to the public or to media outlets.

Fourth, Massachusetts Rule of Civil Procedure 4(a)(1) reads as follows (refer to your supplement for the
corresponding federal rule):

         (a)(1) Contents. A summons must:
                   (A) name the court and the parties;
                   (B) be directed to the defendant;
                   (C) state the name and address of the plaintiff's attorney or--if unrepresented--of the

         plaintiff;
                   (D) state the time within which the defendant must appear and defend;
                   (E) notify the defendant that a failure to appear and defend will result in a default

         judgment against the defendant for the relief demanded in the complaint;
                   (F) be signed by the clerk;
                   (G) bear the court's seal; and
                   (H) include, on separate paper not attached to the summons form, the following

         information,
                             (i) the address of the courthouse at which the defendant must appear, and
                             (ii) a description of the nearest bus stops, light rail stations subways, and any

                   other public transportation location reasonably useful for the purpose of accessing the
                   courthouse, along with any public parking lots within a .5 mile radius of the courthouse.

                             The plaintiff, or the plaintiff's counsel, is responsible for providing the separate
                   attachment needed to comply with this subsection H. A defendant who receives a
                   summons that does not comply with any portion of this subsection H may promptly
                   move to dismiss the complaint.

Fifth, Rhode Island, Massachusetts, New Hampshire, and Massachusetts all have long-arm statutes that
allow the exercise of personal jurisdiction to the limits of the United States Constitution.

Sixth, Massachusetts uses the Second Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Rhode Island and New
Hampshire both use the First Restatement.

Question 1 (25 points): Quirrell asks you to prepare a memo outlining any bases that might exist to
move to dismiss the complaint against Droobles. What possible grounds for dismissal do you identify
in your memo for Quirrell? (Note: another law firm represents Riddle, so you need not discuss bases
of dismissal that apply solely to Riddle).

2 The theory behind this "Public Access" defense is that the plaintiff can use its access to the public (direct access,
or through traditional media outlets) to counter the falsehood.
The court refuses to dismiss the lawsuit (and for the remainder of this exam, put to one side whether
the court was right to do so). You file an answer on Droobles' behalf, asserting the Public Access
defense among other things, and Riddle (through counsel) likewise answers.

Riddle's counsel moves to transfer the lawsuit to the District of New Hampshire under the transfer
statutes, a motion the Massachusetts District Court grants without comment. The New Hampshire
District Court proceeds with the case.

The parties proceed to discovery. At the close of discovery, Quirrell and you meet to discuss the case.
The two of you believe that the discovery record will be as described below.

    · Harry Potter's deposition: Harry testified to the facts described in paragraphs 3, 6-8, 10-13, and
         15-19 of the complaint.

    · Anonymous email: The parties will stipulate that a copy of the email Harry received, as
         described in paragraph 16 of the complaint, may be admitted into evidence.

    · Stipulation: The parties stipulated that paragraph 9 of the complaint is true.
    · Report of FluNetwork Cell Services: The parties will stipulate that a report of FluNetwork Cell

         Services, a telecommunications services provider, may be entered into evidence. The
         FluNetwork report will state that:

              o FluNetwork is the service provider for David Droobles' email, text, cable TV, cell phone,
                   internet, and computer services. Pursuant to a subpoena issued by the court as
                   requested by HG, FluNetwork searched the email and text of David Droobles. The
                   search revealed an email dated December 27, 2018 from Droobles' account to the email
                   address "TomRiddle(at)TomRiddle.net". The email had the subject line "Keep this
                   quiet!!!!!". It had no text. Attached to the email was an .mp4 file of a video. It showed
                   what purported to be a conversation in a conference room at the Droobles estate
                   between Riddle and defendant Droobles. In the conference room conversation, Riddle
                   proposed that Droobles pay Riddle $50,000 in exchange for Riddle's endorsement of
                   Droobles' Best Blowing Gum on "all of my social media accounts." The video ended with
                   defendant Droobles saying, "I do not accept that." The video was exactly five minutes
                   and 22 seconds long.

              o FluNetwork is the service provider for Tom Riddle's email, text, cable TV, cell phone,
                   internet, and computer services. Pursuant to a subpoena issued by the court as
                   requested by HG, FluNetwork searched the email and text of Tom Riddle. The search
                   revealed that Tom Riddle's email account "TomRiddle(at)TomRiddle.net" received the
                   December 27, 2018 email from the David Droobles' email account with the video
                   attachment. That email was forwarded three hours later to the email account
                   "AAAZZZZZAAA(at)flunetwork.com". The AAAZZZZZAAA(at)flunetwork.com email
                   account was set up with FluNetwork anonymously with payments made by PayPal. It
                   has since been deleted, and the account closed.

    · Deposition transcript of Lily Evans: Pursuant to subpoenas issued to by HG to each of Droobles'
         employees, Lily Evans, a Droobles employee, testified that she was the person who sent Harry
         the email described in paragraph 16 of the complaint. She testified to the facts described in
         paragraph 16, and that she was the employee who sent the corresponding email to Harry. She
         further testified that she had never met or communicated with Riddle on any other occasion.

    · Deposition transcript of David Droobles: Your client testified at deposition that he was suffering
         from a cold, and on cold medicine, on December 27, 2018. He testified that he had slept a great
         deal in the last week of 2018 as a result. He had some memory of meeting with Riddle on the
         evening of the 28th, but could not remember anything else that happened that day. He had no
         memory of the events in the video, nor of communicating with Riddle via email that day.
    · Deposition transcript of Tom Riddle: Riddle testified that he, also, had been sick on the day of
         the 27th, with flu symptoms. He had no memory of meeting with Droobles, nor of interacting
         with his email that day. He did acknowledge the email exchange with Harry described in
         paragraph 11 of the complaint, and agreed that he had sent those emails from his Boston office.
         He testified that his statements, "You should have taken my offer, Harry. Very shortly, your
         company will be worth less than my maximum offer. I'll make sure of that." had simply been
         standard business bravado.

Question 2 (11 points): Quirrell asks you for a memo describing whether the following motions for
summary judgment might succeed before the New Hampshire District Court: (a) Defendant Droobles,
based on the Public Access defense, and (b) plaintiff HG, requesting that the court find that
Defendants Droobles and Riddle are liable for the tort of Business Disparagement (but not requesting
summary judgment with respect to an award of damages or other relief). How do you respond?

Quirrell and you decide not to move for summary judgment. HG does move for summary judgment,
however, and the court grants the motion, finding that there is no genuine issue of material fact, and
that Riddle and Droobles participated in a common scheme to commit the tort of Business
Disparagement against HG. The court reserves for a later date the issue of damages, and does not
render judgment.

Question 3 (3 points): Quirrell asks you whether the order granting HG's motion for summary
judgment and denying the Riddle/Droobles cross motion, is immediately appealable?

Quirrell and you decide not to appeal. The parties begin settlement discussions, and eventually, the
parties reach a settlement. The amount Riddle pays to HG is not disclosed to you; that settlement is a
private contract that is not made public. HG (through its attorneys) states, however, that in light of the
settlement, HG has decided not to pursue Droobles further. HG files a stipulation with the court
dismissing the lawsuit against Droobles and Riddle "with prejudice." The New Hampshire federal court
issues a judgment dismissing the case with prejudice. No one appeals. You close your file.

Shortly thereafter, however, you receive a call from Droobles, who emails you a new complaint. This
lawsuit, filed in New Hampshire state court, is by Harry and Ginny in their personal capacities. Harry and
Ginny sue defendant Droobles. The sole count of the complaint is Business Disparagement based on the
Riddle/Harry deepfake video.

You do some research. You find that Massachusetts uses standard claim preclusion doctrine, and
standard issue preclusion doctrine, except that it has completely eliminated the mutuality requirement
for issue preclusion. New Hampshire and Rhode Island use standard claim preclusion doctrine insist on
strict mutuality for issue preclusion (meaning that the same parties must be in the same procedural
postures in the two lawsuits, i.e., plaintiffs in lawsuit one must also be plaintiffs in lawsuit two, and
