defendants in lawsuit one must be defendants in lawsuit two).
Question 4 (8 points): Quirrell asks you for a memorandum addressing whether either plaintiffs Harry
and Ginny, your client defendant Droobles, could use any preclusion doctrine in this second case.
How do you respond?

Before you can take action relating to preclusion, you receive a phone call from Harry and Ginny's
lawyer notifying you that the plaintiffs will request that the court certify a class action, naming
themselves as class representatives and the other HG shareholders as the unnamed class members.
They will seek $1,000,000 in total, prorated to each of the plaintiffs (named and unnamed) in
accordance with their share of the HG stock. You do some research and find out that New Hampshire
state rules of civil procedure incorporate FRCP 23, with all of its case law interpretations, for class action
purposes.

Question 5 (3 points): Quirrell asks you whether you think the New Hampshire state court will find
that this lawsuit meets the certification requirements of New Hampshire class action law (identical to
FRCP 23). He says that you need not consider any other possible defenses to the class action lawsuit,
such as SMJ or IPJ or venue, just the issue of class certification. How do you respond?

Question 6 (15 points): This question does not concern the hypothetical above. Consider the
following ways to increase the availability of preclusion:

    · Altering issue preclusion doctrine so that any issue that a party who lost in a first case could
         have raised, but did not, is considered to have been decided against it, and necessary to the
         decision, in a second case;

    · Expanding claim preclusion to apply to any claims that could have been brought in a first case,
         even (for example) counterclaims that are currently labeled "permissive" in federal court.

Pick one of the two ways listed above. Add to it one other way, not listed, of expanding either issue
or claim preclusion, a way that you think of yourself. Discuss the advantages and disadvantages of
these ideas. Include in your discussion a consideration of the access to justice concerns involved.

## 2019-final · Instructor model answer

Question 1

12(b)(1)/SMJ: Counts I and II assert state law causes of action, with no federal issue embedded, so there
is no FQJ nor Smith-Grable exception. There is complete diversity (HG = MA (PPoB, incorporation),
Droobles = NH) only if Riddle is not domiciled in MA. His time as an undergrad and business school
student was probably not enough to change his domicile from his RI place of birth (Mas), but his
purchase of an apartment, opening a business, and paying taxes in Boston might constitute
simultaneously living in Massachusetts with an intent to stay. If Riddle = MA, then the whole complaint,
including Count II, must be dismissed (Strawbridge). Even if Riddle = RI, however, the suit against
Droobles is AIC deficient ($50K < $75K, 1332), and section 1367 likely does not provide supplemental
jurisdiction. Joinder is proper under FRCP 20 (same T&O in the deepfake scheme, common issue is the
existence of the Riddle-Droobles scheme), and under the same reasoning, HG versus Droobles is part of
the same case or controversy as HG versus Riddle (anchoring claim $950K > $75K). But Count II is a
claim by a plaintiff (HG) against a person made a party under FRCP 20 where the exercise of such
jurisdiction would be AIC deficient and thus inconsistent with 1332, so 1367(b) prohibits supplemental
jurisdiction (Exxon dictum).

12(b)(2)/IPJ: Under FRCP 4(k)(1)(A), with no basis for general IPJ (25% MA sales volume not high enough
under Daimler) or consent IPJ, we look for a MA long-arm (present, limits of the Constitution), minimal
contacts relating Droobles and the alleged deepfake to the state of MA, and the fairness and
reasonableness of MA IPJ exercise (International Shoe). Regarding minimal contacts, 25% of Droobles'
sales are in MA, the highest volume sales state. These are direct sales, showing purposeful availablment
of MA customers, with no intermediary (contrast Nicastro), so this is no stream of commerce case. True,
this lawsuit is not as directly related to those sales as would be a suit based on defective gum sold in
MA. But the complaint concerns, and followed, the use and independent promotion of Droobles'
product in MA, which results from Droobles' direct shipments there. HG, the deepfake's target,
monetizes by operating solely in MA, with Harry's images and postings using MA landmarks to show its
connection to MA, meaning a big part of HG's reputation is in MA. Droobles and Riddle knew and
intended that the harm to HG would occur in MA. True, Droobles has no office, salesforce, advertising,
or business solicitation in MA, and his website merely responds to visitors. He does not accept checks or
monetary transfers from MA (or anywhere else). Regarding fairness and reasonableness, Massachusetts
has an interest in protecting MA companies from intentional business harm. MA is a convenient place
to litigate for Riddle, who does business there, and Droobles, who directly sells business product there.
Evidence of harm (HG records, Harry's testimony) will be in MA. The harm was felt in MA, so even an R2
state like MA could use MA law (see below).

12(b)(3)/venue: Droobles and Riddle are domiciled/reside (1391(c)(1) in different states, so 1391(b)(1)
is unavailable. But venue is likely proper under 1392(b)(2) because the following MA events form a
substantial part of the Counts: Harry's initial endorsement of Droobles' gum, Harry's portion of the
Riddle email exchanges, all of Harry's activities that generate the Internet traffic HG depends on, and the
business harm to HG. 1391(b)(3) is not available because 1392(b)(2) venue is definitely proper in D.R.I

12(b)(6)/Twibal: We might ignore only portions of the paragraph beginning "On information and belief"
as conclusory. The remainder of this detailed complaint alleges the participation in the production
(element 1) of a fake video (element 2) in paras 12-14; injury to business value (element 3) in paras 6-8
and 18-19, and causation in paras 6-11, 18-19. The only hard issue is whether there are the alleged facts
are sufficient to nudge the issue of Droobles' involvement in the deepfake scheme across the line from
merely possible to plausible. But consider the following: Harry has never met either Droobles or Riddle;
the anonymous email describes the Droobles/Riddle's meeting and suggests that the video forming the
basis for the deepfake was taken on Droobles' personal phone at Droobles' personal and explicit
request. Although it's possible Droobles' wasn't involved, it's plausible that he was.

MRCP 4(a)(1)(H): The complaint did not include the separate attachment required by this rule, so if it
applies, the court will dismiss despite the absence of a counterpart requirement under FRCP4. (MRCP
4(a)(1)H) is procedural for HCOL purposes, as it concerns how to pursue lawsuits, so MA HCOL rules,
applicable under Klaxon, would use it). Erie time. Under York, we dismiss under MRCP4 and continue
under FRCP4, so we use MRCP4. Under Harlan in Hannah, knowledgeable lawyers will (or will not)
include the attachment without bothering clients, so with no effect on primary conduct, we use FRCP4.
Under Shady Grove/Scalia, FRCP4 is valid because it really regulates procedure (Sibbach) in that the
issue here concerns the manner and means of how lawsuits proceed, and one can argue that FRCP4's
phrasing "a summons must" contain the listed items means that it need only contain the items in the
detailed list that follows, and we continue. Otherwise, Shady Grove/Scalia resembles Shady
Grove/Ginsburg, and here, an interpretation of FRCP4 as not speaking to the issue of whether a
summons must contain the separate MRCP 4(a)(1)(H) attachment, thus rendering FRCP4 inapplicable, is
available: FRCP 4(a)(1)(A-G) is not exhaustive of what a summons "must" contain.

Question 2: If, as a matter of HCOL, the MA Public Access Defense ("PAD") applies, then a Droobles
FRCP56 motion should succeed. Movant Droobles has the BoP at trial, and he can point to Harry's
deposition testimony to the facts described in paragraphs 6-7 of the complaint to meet his burden of
production that HG has ready access to the public. Even drawing all rational inferences in HG's favor, it
is hard to see how HG could demonstrate a genuine issue of material fact requiring a trial given that
HG's business model depends on public access.

The HCOL analysis suggests that the PAD is available. Under Ferrens, the NH federal district court will
use MA choice of law rules (as the MA federal court would have, Klaxon) if venue was proper in MA and
MA had personal jurisdiction over both defendants, which seems likely, see above. Even if not, NH's R1
HCOL analysis will result in MA substantive law because the last event needed to complete the harm,
the loss of HG's business value, occurred in MA. Thus, MA's R2 analysis presumes MA law. True, key
events did occur elsewhere (the Riddle/Droobles meeting, the video recording), but key events also
occurred in Boston (HG's gum endorsement, the Riddle/Harry email exchange, HG loss of value). MA
(NH) has an interested in protecting HG (Drooles), but this will matter little because MA (NH) law means
a loss for HG (Droobles). A court is unlikely to depart from the R2 presumption of lex loci delicti,
meaning MA law, for any issue, particularly the PAD, which centers on a MA party's access to the
Internet.

Without the PAD, an HG FRCP56 motion will likely succeed. HG, bearing the BoP at trial, will meet its
production burden as follows:

    · The two Harry/Riddle exchanges to suggest Riddle's intention to perpetrate the scheme and
         motive therefor;

    · Harry's uncontradicted testimony stating that the video is fake, and to the other events
         described in the complaint;

    · The FluNetwork report, and the striking similarities in length and language between the
         deepfake video and the video taken in the Droobles' conference room, to suggest how the fake
         was perpetrated; and
    · Evans' uncontradicted testimony showing Droobles' participation in the scheme. Droobles'
         called in Evans and instructed her to record the meeting on his cell phone. A reasonable jury
         must infer Droobles' emailed the video to Riddle with the note "Keep this quiet!!!!". Ergo,
         Droobles knew what he was doing and why.

Drawing all reasonable inferences in Droobles' favor, there is no genuine issue of material fact on the BD
elements and HG is entitled to judgment as a matter of law. Droobles will probably not meet his burden
to produce facts showing a genuine issue of material fact. There is nothing to counteract HG's
production. True, there is no smoking gun evidence showing Droobles and Riddle saying "We agree to
commit a tort against Riddle," no direct evidence of a payment from Riddle to Droobles, or direct
evidence of causal harm, but insisting on that seems fastidious.

Question 3: No. This is exactly the same fact pattern as Liberty Mutual, so no 1291, FRCP54(b) (no
"release" language, or 1292(b) (no certification), no irreparable harm for mandamus, no injunction. SJ is
the merits, so no Cohen.

Question 4: Droobles can invoke claim preclusion against Harry but not Ginny. We have the same
claim/transaction in the form of the deepfake video, a judgment that is final and "on the merits" in the
dismissal with prejudice. HG and Harry are different parties, but Harry is in privity with HG based on his
controlling share of HG and his (discovery could establish such if necessary) control over the HG lawsuit.
Ginny, however, is not in privity with HG; there is no evidence that she controlled the HG lawsuit, and
she does not have a controlling share of its stock.

Ginny may try to use NMOCE to preclude Droobles from denying that he perpetrated the tort. First, is
the summary judgment followed by the dismissal sufficiently firm to support issue preclusion? On the
one hand, SJ grants are subject to revision at any time. On the other hand, they hardly ever are revised,
and we want to prevent needless relitigation of issues. Second, under Semtek, we should use the
preclusion law of the state in which the rendering federal diversity court sat, but after the transfer, is
that MA or NH? Likely MA, as we likely use the happy court rule to say that with sufficient venue and
IPJ, the transfer is simply a change of courthouse, so MA preclusion rules govern. If so, Ginny can use
NMOCE (same issues, the elements of the BD tort; actually litigated in the SJ motion, subject to the
above; necessary to the judgment from the SJ grant, subject to the above) even though she is a different
party from HG. If NH applies, either because the happy court rule does not (allowing us to look only to
the face of the NH federal court's judgment to know what law to use, or because either IPJ or venue was
lacking in MA), then Ginny cannot use issue preclusion because she is not the same party as HG.

Question 5: This is a potential b(3) class, so we need numerosity, typicality/representativeness,
predominance (which swallows commonality), and superiority. Regarding predominance, all plaintiffs
were harmed (if they were harmed at all) by precisely the same scheme in precisely the same way. The
only difference among their claims is the amounts to which they are entitled, which vary according to
their HG ownership. Everything else consists of common issues. For the same reasons, Harry and
Ginny's claims are the same as those of the other class members, providing
typicality/representativeness. Because the issues are so similar, it has to be better to try them all once.
The only real issue appears to be numerousity, but with 20 unnamed class members spread across three
distant states, and small ($15,000) in controversy for each, numerosity is likely present. Certification
seems likely.
Question 6: The second suggestion would eliminate the distinction between permissive and compulsory
joinder (including counterclaims) currently in FRCP13 & 20. Particularly given the declaratory judgment
statute, so doing would provide sophisticated parties with additional power to compel litigation on all
disputes in that party's chosen forum. How much this will matter is hard to guess. For sophisticated
and reasonably financed opponents, it will probably matter little. Such parties can take advantage of
modern transportation and information transmission mechanisms to render location less important.
Geographic location can sometimes matter to HCOL (Klaxon), but given the similarity in HCOL rules
across the country (see how NY's experience with HCOL resulted in the R2 = R1 except in unusual cases),
this will infrequently. The big divide currently, and likely in the future, is whether litigation occurs in
court at all (as opposed to some kind of arbitration), and if so, whether it occurs in a United States court
using United States law ("US law, who needs it?"). The FRsCP already encourage resolution of as many
disputes as possible in a single lawsuit, and this change seems consistent with that policy, with Rule 42
providing the needed complexity-reducing discretion to the trial judge. Whatever efficiency gains are
result from the litigation, but most likely settlement, of multiple matters would be welcome.

An additional suggestion to expand the preclusion doctrine would be to expand the rules of privity, such
that (say) the Gonzalez district court's decision would have been affirmed. As applied to claim
preclusion, this would create a rule of near-compulsory intervention for anyone who wanted to have a
say in litigation of their interests, when those interests were effectively represented by a party to that
litigation, knowing that if they do not intervene, they will be precluded anyway. As applied to issue
preclusion, this would effectively expand the rules of precedent (which does not require any form of
mutuality) to include factual determinations. This proposal could result in some efficiency gains from
reduced relitigation of claims and issues, particularly on large matters of public interest, such as the
behavior of large corporations, or the legality of government policies. The proposal would, however,
involve acute information costs. Depending on how much privity is expanded, it could result in parties
being bound by litigation about which they had no notice and no prior knowledge, which would
implicate PDP doctrines (Mullane). Parties unsure of whether changes in PDP doctrines were ripe for
relaxation might need to begin to monitor courts closely; information systems regarding the nature of
ongoing litigation are not currently in place for such monitoring. Moreover, large public-interest
litigation might become absurdly unwieldy (think Smuck) unless class action certification rules were
relaxed (we'd need to reverse the trend in Walmart).

Both proposals could involve difficult access to justice costs. The declaratory judgment statute, plus the
inability of low-income parties to use modern methods of transportation and information transmission,
will make it difficult for smaller parties or low-income individuals to combat a sophisticated party's
choice of forum for tort cases (contract matters probably have FSCs anyway). And only sophisticated
parties would have a chance to monitor cases that might affect their interests, as would be required in
by the second proposed change. As is often true, the usefulness of these doctrines depends on which
world we consider, that of sophisticated and reasonably resourced parties or the other 99%.

# EXAM 2020-final — 2020 Greiner Civil Procedure 2 Final Exam

Source: content/course/exams/2020-greiner-civpro2-final.md · model answer: content/course/exams/2020-greiner-civpro2-model-answer.md

## 2020-final · Exam text

Word limit: 2500 words

1. HLS will administer the exam according to standard one-day take-home time limits. Spend a
substantial portion of your exam time getting organized before writing. You will botch up this
exam if you don't take the time to get organized before you start writing.

2. Your response must be typed using the Exam4 exam software. Your response can be no
more than the word limit above, everything included. Should you depart from the length
limit, the first portion of your exam, up to the word limit above, will be graded and the rest
ignored. HLS will administer the exam delivery and submission system.

3. You may not communicate with any person other than the exam administrators regarding the
substance of this exam until after the 1L exam period is over. The reason for this prohibition
is that, each year, some students take the exam on a date other than its scheduled
administration date. Thus, do not think that you may discuss this exam immediately after
you hand it in. Wait until the 1L exam period is over. Note also that the prohibition above
includes librarians. According to HLS policy: "[S]tudents may not communicate with the
instructor nor raise questions or concerns about the content of an examination once it is in
progress and the Registrar's Office will not disseminate additional information about an exam
once it is in progress. To maintain anonymity and to ensure compliance by students with this
policy, instructors should not communicate in any way, including through email, with students
during an examination."

4. If you feel that there is a mistake in the exam, such as that a question is unclear or that it
refers to something that it should not or that there is a critical typographical error, then do the
following. First, in your answer, explain what you believe to be the mistake. Second, explain
the implications of the mistake. Third, make an assumption that is reasonable given the fact
pattern and applicable law to resolve the error. Fourth, proceed with the remainder of the exam.

5. Your answer must be your own work. Any quotation from any source must be accompanied
by a corresponding reference (but use short references, e.g., "International Shoe," or "FRCP
23").

6. Each question's weight is specified (out of 65 total points). Time and word-count may be
issues for you during the exam. Answer questions efficiently, and feel free to take into account
the number of points available for each question. Don't forget your test-taking skills.

7. Do not put your name on your response.

8. Deal with all issues fairly raised, even if you think another issue would be dispositive of a
matter in the real world.

9. If you believe additional information (factual or legal) is needed to deal with an issue, say so
and make explicit whatever assumptions you need to proceed, but make these assumptions
reasonable and interesting. Similarly, if at any point in this question you find that you do not
know what the law is on a particular subject, identify the issue as to which there is uncertainty,
then discuss the various options as to what the law could or should be, noting the advantages or
disadvantages of each option.

10. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: "SMJ" for subject matter
jurisdiction; "IPJ" for in personam jurisdiction; "DJ" for diversity jurisdiction; "FQJ" for federal
question jurisdiction; "CNOF" for common nucleus of operative facts; and "DPC" for due
process clause.

11. All of the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the
law described in this exam. Use the scenarios only for the purposes of this exam.

                              ********************************

Law regarding self-driving trucks and front proximity detectors

                   Federal Law

In 2016, Congress passed the Willow Rosenberg Self-Driving Trucks Protection Act (the "Willow Act").
Named after a woman hit by a self-driving truck as she was standing on a sidewalk casting a spell, the
Willow Act included the following provisions:

         Section 201: Findings And Purpose
         The Congress finds that self-driving trucks pose hazards to pedestrians almost as great
         as human-driven trucks. In particular, the Congress finds that front proximity detectors1
         have proven to be a particular technological challenge for manufacturers of self-driving
         trucks. One purpose of this Act is therefore to empower the Secretary of Transportation
         (the "Secretary") to issue regulations promoting the safety and well-being of
         pedestrians through appropriate regulation, with particular attention to front proximity
         detectors.

         Section 202: Department of Transportation ("DoT") Duties
    (a) To promote the safety and well-being of pedestrians, after appropriate notice and

         comment as required by the Administrative Procedure Act, the Secretary shall issue
         regulations concerning the design, manufacture, sale, purchase, and maintenance of
         self-driving trucks, with special attention to the issue of front proximity detection.
    (b) The Secretary shall require that any business involved in the design, manufacture, sale,
         purchase, or maintenance of self-driving trucks obtain a license from the Department of
         Transportation before engaging in such business.
    (c) The Secretary shall promptly revoke the license of any such business that fails to comply
         in any material way with the Secretary's regulations governing self-driving trucks,
         including those governing front proximity detectors.

1 Front proximity detectors are devices that detect objects or obstacles in front of a vehicle. Should they detect an
object or obstacle of sufficient size too close to the front of the vehicle while it is moving forward, the vehicle's
control system should engage the brakes to stop the vehicle from hitting the object or obstacle.
In 2017, acting pursuant to the Willow Act, DoT Secretary Riley Finn promulgated the following
regulations.

         49 C.F.R. § 5X5: Front Proximity Detectors
         (a) Any corporation or other business that manufactures or sells self-driving trucks

              shall, before selling any self-driving truck to any other person or entity, conduct an
              appropriate test of the front proximity detector and corresponding braking
              mechanism (such as causing the truck to drive slowly toward an obstacle or barrier
              to see if it stops on its own) in the presence of the buyer to discover whether the
              front proximity detector on the vehicle is in good working order.
         (b) No corporation or other business shall purchase a self-driving truck without
              demanding that the test of the front proximity detector described in subsection (a)
              be conducted.
         (c) The Secretary shall promptly revoke the license of any business that fails to comply
              with this regulation.

No other provision of the Willow Act nor of Secretary Finn's regulations has any language relevant to
this exam.

                   Pennsylvania Law

In 2018, hoping to stimulate the institution and growth of a self-driving vehicle R&D and manufacturing
industry, the Pennsylvania General Assembly passed the Rupert Giles Self-Driving Vehicle Protection Act
(the "Giles" Act). The Act includes the following provision.

         Section 6.7: Heightened Pleading Standard
    (a) The General Assembly finds that lawsuits, or the threat of lawsuits, may deter investors

         from starting or investing in self-driving vehicle businesses in the Commonwealth of
         Pennsylvania.
    (b) Pennsylvania Rule of Civil Procedure 8 is hereby amended to add a new subsection (f),
         as follows: "In any action alleging negligence against a designer, manufacturer, seller,
         buyer, or distributor of a self-driving vehicle, the plaintiff shall specify with particularity
         the nature of the negligent act or omissions, including by identifying the date and
         location of the allegedly negligent act or omission. Upon a motion to dismiss by a
         defendant for failure to comply with this subsection, the court shall forthwith dismiss
         the complaint without leave to amend."

In all other ways relevant to this exam, the Pennsylvania Rules of Civil Procedure are identical to
the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure.

Law relating to personal jurisdiction, choice of law, respondeat superior, and civil procedure

All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes allowing the exercise of personal jurisdiction
whenever the Constitution so allows.

Pennsylvania, Virginia, and Delaware use the Second Restatement for horizontal choice of law. North
Carolina and South Carolina use the First Restatement.
Except as otherwise stated in this exam, all state rules of civil procedure relevant to this exam are
identical to the federal rules.

All states relevant to this exam use the doctrine of respondeat superior, meaning that a company is
legally responsible (and thus can be sued) for any action taken, or any failure to take an action, within
the scope of an employee's employment duties. For example, if a restaurant delivery-person drives too
fast on their way to a drop off a pizza at a customer's house and, as a result, hits and injuries a
pedestrian, then the pedestrian may sue the restaurant for damages as vicariously liable under the
doctrine of respondeat superior.

Law relating to the negligence cause of action

In all states relevant to this exam (the federal law of negligence is not relevant to this exam), a plaintiff
may recover for the tort of negligence by proving that (i) the defendant owed a duty of care to the
plaintiff, (ii) the defendant breached that duty by failing to act with due care, (iii) the defendant's breach
caused (iv) harm to the plaintiff.

    · For the purposes of this exam, you may assume that all persons (including incorporeal entities)
         owe a duty to act with due care to all other persons (or corporations or incorporeal entities)
         with whom they interact or anyone affected by their actions; in other words, you can assume
         that a plaintiff need not allege element (i) of negligence in a complaint, regardless of whether
         the case is brought in state or federal court.

    · With respect to element (ii):
              o A plaintiff ordinarily pleads or proves this element by alleging or showing that the
                   defendant failed to exercise the ordinary care that a reasonable person would exercise
                   under the circumstances.
              o The substantive (meaning substantive for horizontal choice of law purposes) law of
                   Virginia, Delaware, and North Carolina, but not of Pennsylvania nor of South Carolina,
                   also allows the plaintiff an alternative option with respect to element (ii). In these
                   states, to meet element (ii), a plaintiff may allege or show that the defendant violated a
                   state or federal statute established to protect a set of persons that includes the plaintiff,
                   such as, for example, Secretary Finn's regulation appearing at 49 C.F.R. § 5X5. This is
                   called a "per se" theory of negligence. In states making this option available (Virginia,
                   Delaware, and North Carolina), a plaintiff may attempt to allege or prove either the
                   standard breach of ordinary care theory or the per se theory or both, either
                   simultaneously or in the alternative. In states that do not make this option available
                   (Pennsylvania, South Carolina), a plaintiff can allege or prove element (ii) of negligence
                   not with this "per se" theory but rather by alleging or proving failure to exercise
                   ordinary care, as explained in the prior bullet point.
              o A plaintiff pursuing a negligence cause of action may seek either damages or an
                   injunction. If a plaintiff prevails in a negligence lawsuit and seeks an injunction, the
                   court will enter an order2 requiring the defendant to desist from future negligent acts
                   omissions of the same type as that which damaged the plaintiff.

2 Such an order would be an injunction after full litigation of the case (meaning as part of the final judgment). It
would not be a preliminary injunction, and so the four preliminary injunction prerequisites/factors would not be
relevant.
Law related to preclusion

Pennsylvania, Delaware, and North Carolina follow federal common law on issue preclusion. South
Carolina and Virginia require strict mutuality before issue preclusion can apply.

States and districts

Pennsylvania has three federal district courts; Philadelphia is located in the Eastern District of
Pennsylvania. Virginia has two federal district courts; Alexandria, VA is located in the Eastern District of
Virginia. South Carolina and Delaware each have a single federal district court.

Facts

Xander Harris, Inc. ("XH") is a Delaware corporation with its principal place of business in Baltimore,
Maryland. It manufactures self-driving trucks of all sizes, from light pickups all the way up to 18
wheelers. All of its manufacturing occurs in a massive plant outside of Baltimore, MD. Each vehicle that
XH produces bears a distinctive script "XH" symbol on its front fender. XH conducts no sales activity to
consumers or business, nor does it advertise. Instead, it sells its products exclusively in an enormous
purchase and sale clearinghouse market facility located in Alexandria, VA called "The Spike." At The
Spike, sellers and buyers of self-driving vehicles meet, conduct inspections, negotiate sales terms,
transfer money, and take delivery of vehicles. XH sells to various distributors at The Spike. XH insists
that any company to whom it sells one of its vehicles must subsequently sell the vehicle directly to a
consumer.

Faith, Inc. is a North Carolina corporation with its principal place of business in Columbia, South Carolina.
Faith is a large distributor (to consumers) of self-driving vehicles. It advertises and sells self-driving
vehicles in a great many states in the eastern half of the United States, including Pennsylvania,
Delaware, Maryland, Virginia, North Carolina, and South Carolina. Faith purchases self-driving trucks
from, among other sources, manufacturers who patronize the clearinghouse market at The Spike in
Alexandria, VA. In early 2020, Faith began advertising a set of XH self-driving trucks throughout its
multi-state service area, with television ads running on local TV stations in different cities in
Pennsylvania as well as all of the states mentioned immediately above. Faith also ran ads in local
newspapers in Pennsylvania cities, including Philadelphia; similar ads ran in local newspapers of cities in
other states. The ads all directed those interested in self-driving pickups to Faith's website, where
potential buyers could see complete information, including photographs, of the trucks for sale. People
interested were instructed to contact a Faith employee named Cordelia Chase. The ads that ran in
Philadelphia specified that Faith had 10 XH self-driving trucks waiting in a rented parking facility in the
area, ready for immediate inspection and delivery.

In early February of 2020, Chase received an Internet message from Buffy Summers, a Pennsylvania
domiciliary living in Philadelphia. Buffy inquired how soon she could inspect one of the XH light vehicles
she'd seen advertised on her local TV station, and how much it would cost. A series of email exchanges
followed, in which Buffy indicated an interest in purchasing one of the trucks. Buffy was delighted to
learn that she could buy the truck as soon as she liked after inspecting it, and that she could inspect it
right outside her house, because Chase could instruct the truck to drive itself to her house and park
there. Buffy was also excited that her sister, Dawn Summers, visiting from her home in Charleston, SC,
could also take a look at the truck to help her decide whether to buy it. On February 12, 2020, Chase
used the Internet to deploy the codes for one of the trucks to instruct it to drive itself to park in front of
Buffy's Philadelphia house at 11 am that morning.

At 10:45am, Buffy and Dawn bundled up and went to the sidewalk in front of Buffy's house, located at
1630 Sunnydale Drive, in Philadelphia. As they waited, the two faced each other on the sidewalk next to
Sunnydale Drive and occupied themselves by practicing their singing, a pursuit at which both were as
enthusiastic as they were unskillful. At 11:00am, the XH pickup approached the house, but neither
noticed it. Instead of stopping a couple of feet short of the curb, the pickup pulled its tires right up
against the curb. In doing so, the pickup's front right fender and front right side headlight struck Buffy
and Dawn, throwing both to the ground.

Before being placed in the ambulance, Dawn photographed the front of the truck, and later noticed
both the "XH" logo and that the front left fender and headlight (a part of the truck that did not strike the
two sisters) bore scratches and dents.

Buffy suffered a concussion, a broken leg, and several cuts; she required care in a nearby Philadelphia
hospital for 25 days and underwent physical therapy before eventually making her recovery. Dawn
suffered a broken left arm, which was painful and required her to wear a cast for three weeks. While
Dawn was visiting Buffy in the hospital, the two talked about filing a lawsuit. Dawn showed Buffy the
picture she took of the truck, and the sisters realized that the XH truck that had hit them must have hit
something else beforehand, because of the scratches and dents on the left-hand front bumper and
headlight, which was not where they'd been hit.

You are a friend of Buffy's. On May 4, 2020, Buffy emailed you, saying that she wanted to sue both XH
and Faith. Buffy told you that because she had really good medical and temporary disability insurance
(something she had to have for her job), she decided that she didn't want money. Instead, she only
wanted to make sure that this sort of thing never happened to anyone else. Because she didn't want
money, she could not find a lawyer to represent her, so she had investigated the matter herself. She
wrote that she had called XH, trying to get information on the truck that hit her. When she got through
to a human being, she heard someone say, "Oh, this is about one of those trucks that Maclay sold
before she was fired," but the person hung up before she could hear anything else. With some online
searching, she figured out that "Maclay" had to refer to a person named Tara Maclay, a former
employee of XH before she was fired. Buffy managed to find Maclay's phone number online, and called
her late one night. Maclay, half asleep, said, "Oh, are you the woman who got hit by that truck. I'm so
sorry I believed Cordelia when she said we didn't need to test that front proximity thingy before I sold it
to her at The Spike. I hope you're OK. . . . Wait, I'm not supposed to be talking to you." Maclay then
hung up and stopped answering the phone.

Buffy also did some research on the business models of XH and Faith. Using publicly available sources,
she found out all of the information described above about the companies' places of incorporation,
principal places of business, and business models/habits.

Buffy attached a copy of a complaint that she wrote herself, as follows.

************
Buffy Summers, plaintiff
         v.

Xander Harris, Inc. and
Faith, Inc., defendants

In the United States District Court for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania

    1. This Court has subject matter jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. §§ 1331 and 1332.
    2. Venue is proper under 28 U.S.C. § 1391(b).

         Parties

    3. Plaintiff Buffy Summers is a Pennsylvania citizen.
    4. Defendant Xander Harris, Inc. ("XH") is a Delaware corporation with its principal place of

         business in Maryland.
    5. Defendant Faith, Inc. ("Faith") is a North Carolina corporation with its principal place of business

         in South Carolina.

         Facts

    6. On February 12, 2020, a self-driving pickup truck manufactured by XH hit me while I was
         standing on a sidewalk, causing me serious injury.

    7. XH sold the truck to Faith at this place in Virginia called The Spike.
    8. Faith owned the truck at the time it hit me.
    9. If the truck's front proximity detector had worked properly, it wouldn't have hit me.
    10. I was a pedestrian when I was hit. I was on the sidewalk in front of my house
    11. The Willow Act and accompanying regulations say that to protect pedestrians like me, the front

         proximity detectors of a self-driving truck has to be tested before it be sold to another company
         or to a consumer. Both the seller and the buyer are responsible for the testing.

         Sole Count of the Complaint: Negligence

    12. The front proximity detector on the truck that hit me wasn't tested when XH sold it to Faith.
    13. That is negligence per se, see 49 CFR 5X5.
    14. If that truck's proximity detector had been tested, XH and Faith would have found out that the

         front proximity detector didn't work. They would have fixed it, or they would not have tried to
         show me that particular truck, and I would not have gotten hurt.
    15. As a result of getting hit, I had to stay in the hospital for about three weeks, had to pay medical
         bills, and suffered pain.

         Prayer for Relief
    16. I ask that this Court issue an injunction assuring that the defendants test any self-driving vehicle
         that they sell or buy. The defendants should have to do this even though it will cost them
         several hundred thousand dollars each.3

Signature

Question 1 (30 points): Buffy asked you whether you think the complaint would survive a motion to
dismiss or, in the alternative, to transfer. What was your response? (Note: for this Question 1, you
need not discuss to what district the case would be transferred, if it were to be transferred.)

Buffy decided not to file the complaint immediately. Instead, she kept calling Maclay and leaving
messages for her begging for her help. Eventually, Maclay called her back. Maclay told Buffy that she
had been fired from XH, and that Cordelia Chase had been fired from Faith. Maclay said that both Chase
and she felt really guilty, and they wanted to tell Buffy what happened.

On a subsequent Zoom call, Maclay and Chase told Buffy the following: In mid-December of 2019, XH
completed manufacture of a set of 50 self-driving light pickup trucks. In the middle of the night of
December 16-17, 2019, Maclay, driving an XH car, accompanied the fleet of 50 light pickups in an
overnight trip to The Spike. Maclay's instructions were to sell the trucks for a price of $25,000 each,
more if she could get it. As her fleet pulled into one of the Spike's parking facilities, Maclay noticed that
three of the trucks, while traveling at about five miles per hour, struck the lighting polls illuminating the
facility that they were supposed to park behind, causing scratches and minor dents in their front left
fenders and around their front left headlights. Maclay said to herself, "That's probably just a
malfunction," and proceeded about her duties.

On the morning of December 18, 2019, Maclay met with Chase at The Spike. Chase was excited about
the new XH trucks, so excited that she immediately began negotiations, telling Maclay, "There's no need
for us to test any of these, I know that XH does great work." Maclay and Chase quickly concluded
negotiations for the purchase and sale of the 50 light pickups, and by lunchtime had agreed on a price of
$28,000 per truck. Maclay was so delighted that, after signing the purchase and sale agreement on
behalf of XH, verifying with XH's business manager that the latter had received the corresponding funds
from Faith, and providing Chase with the required access codes needed to operate the vehicles, she
treated herself to a fancy dinner and a 24-hour binge watch of Monty Python Flying Circus episodes.

On December 20, 2019, Chase had her assistant and fellow Faith employee, Anya Jenkins, transport the
fifty pickups to Faith's facility near Columbia, SC. When the trucks were on their way out of The Spike,
Chase saw two of the trucks hit vehicles in front of them, causing dents and scratching. Chase said to
herself, "Ah, that was probably just a fluke" and took no action. Later, Chase had 10 of the truck from
this set of 50 transported up to the Philadelphia parking site in anticipation of a sale.

Question 2 (6 points): In June of 2020, Buffy emailed you again, relating all of the information she
found out in the Zoom meeting with Maclay and Chase. Buffy asked you for an email back describing

3 You may assume that compliance with such an injunction would cost each defendant enough to meet the 1332
amount in controversy requirement.
(i) how she could change the complaint against XH and Faith to make it more likely to survive a
motion to dismiss, and (ii) in what federal district court (other than the Eastern District of
Pennsylvania) she could file to make it less likely that her lawsuit would get dismissed or transferred.
What was your response?

In July, Buffy emailed you again. She said that she had had one other conversation with Maclay and
Chase. Buffy had asked them whether they had ever bought and sold any other trucks that might have
had faulty front proximity detectors that they hadn't tested. Maclay and Chase both agreed that the
two had arranged for XH to sell approximately 900 trucks to Faith over the past two years. The two
agreed that their two companies only did business with each other through the two of them, and that in
no instance did the two arrange to have any of the front proximity detectors of the trucks tested during
the corresponding sales. Chase then used her old username and password to log into Faith's computer
system. There, she discovered an internal memo from one of her supervisors documenting that Faith
had received word of 94 instances of an XH truck hitting a buyer, a prospective buyer, or a piece of a
buyer's property. There were about 20 incidents in each of South Carolina, North Carolina, Virginia, and
Pennsylvania, with the rest scattered around the eastern portion of the United States.

Question 3 (6 points): Buffy said in her email that she was thinking of trying to become the named
plaintiff in a class action lawsuit. She concluded her email by asking whether, if she sued and got a
good lawyer, and if she switched her lawsuit so that she asked for damages (the same as the other
members of the class), a federal court might let her turn her lawsuit against both XH and Faith
together into a class action. Also, she asked whether it mattered in which federal district she sued.
What do you say?

Buffy ended up deciding not to pursue a class action, and she decided to pursue her original plan of
pursuing an injunction instead of damages. She managed to find a lawyer, who filed an edited
complaint against XH and Faith in the Eastern District of Pennsylvania in early August of 2020. The new
complaint still alleged negligence, but it stated two independent theories of negligence: (i) the original
per se negligence theory stating that XH and Faith's failure (through the inaction of Maclay and Chase)
to conduct a test of the front proximity detectors of the truck fleet was negligence per se because Buffy
was a pedestrian, the class of people supposed to be protected by the Willow Act and accompanying
regulations, and because the failure to test the detectors violated the regulations, with the failure to
test subsequently causing Buffy's injuries, and (ii) Maclay's failure to take any action after seeing three
of the trucks run into lighting polls at The Spike, and Chase's failure to take any action after seeing two
of the trucks hit other vehicles at The Spike, constituted a failure by XH (through Maclay's inaction and
the respondeat superior doctrine) and Faith (through Chase's inaction and the respondeat superior
doctrine) to exercise due care under the circumstances, and was thus negligent, separately from the
failure to test.

Immediately after the filing of the complaint, Buffy's lawyer got in touch with lawyers for XH and Faith.
Rather than go through costly pretrial litigation, the parties and the court agreed to proceed directly a
rapid bench trial. In August, 2020, Buffy, Chase, and Maclay all testified to the facts as described above,
and there was no other evidence offered. XH and Faith's lawyers called no witnesses, and argued that
the evidence of causation (that Maclay's and Chase's inactions caused Buffy's injuries) was insufficient.
At the end of the trial, the judge made the following statements on the record orally (the statements
were transcribed by the court reporter, and the transcript was made part of the record).
                   I find that XH and Faith were each negligent. I hereby enjoin XH and Faith from failing to
                   comply with applicable law. Any future failures on their part will constitute contempt of
                   court, and I will fine each of them $10,000 per violation. These are my findings of fact
                   and conclusions of law under FRCP 52. The clerk will enter judgment immediately upon
                   my verdict with this injunction as I have just stated.

Later the same day, the clerk did as instructed.

Question 4 (3 points): Could XH or Faith have appealed as this point? If so, what might they have
argued that the district judge did wrong (hint: remember the purposes of FRCP 52)?

XH and Faith did appeal, and as of December 16, 2020 (today), that appeal is pending.

On December 16, 2020, you receive and email from Buffy's sister, Dawn. She says that she wants to sue
XH and Faith for $30,000.00 each. She wants to sue in Virginia state court. She says that she's tried to
get a lawyer to represent her, but no lawyers she's spoken to will take the case because it's only worth
$60,000.00 total, so she's going to have to litigate the case on her own. She doesn't understand the
whole negligence per se and testing law thing, but she does understand that Maclay and Chase saw
some of trucks hit something before the one truck hit her in Pennsylvania, and she thinks it's negligent
and terrible that neither Maclay nor Chase did anything, particularly because the photo she took makes
it pretty likely that one of those very trucks hit her. That's the theory she wants to sue on.

Question 5 (10 points): Dawn asks whether, if she files her lawsuit in Virginia state court, she could
use the judge's verdict for Buffy in the Pennsylvania litigation and the issue preclusion doctrine to
avoid having to prove in her case that XH and Faith were negligent when Maclay and Chase saw trucks
from that group of 50 bumping into things and took no action, apart from any failure to test. How do
you respond? Would there be any difference in your response if Virginia law gave XH and Faith the
right to a jury trial (assuming either XH or Faith made a jury trial demand)?

Question 6 (5 points): Short answer: Several procedural doctrines we discussed in the class are
premised on the idea that judges, particularly trial judges, are either omniscient or usually correct in
their rulings. For example, federal appellate jurisdiction law implements a strong preference to wait
until the end of trial court litigation before allowing an appeal of trial judge decisions occurring early
in the case, with part of the justification for this preference being the belief that most trial judge
decisions are correct. Chose two examples, other than appellate jurisdiction, of doctrines we
discussed that are built on a strong belief in the likely correctness of trial judge decisions. Provide a
short explanation (1-2 sentences for each of your two examples) of how each example embodies this
belief, and a short assessment (1-2 sentences for each of your two examples) of whether you think the
belief is justified.

Question 7 (5 points): Short answer: Our last module concerned access to justice problems, and we
discussed potential non-lawyer solutions. Identify one possible non-lawyer solution to the problem of
access to justice in the United States, and provide 3-4 sentence discussing its
disadvantages/drawbacks.

## 2020-final · Instructor model answer

Question 1

The court has diversity jurisdiction because plaintiff Buffy is domiciled in and thus a citizen of
Pennsylvania, while defendant XH is a citizen of Delaware (incorporated) and Maryland (PPoB), and Faith
is a citizen of NC (incorporated) and SC (PPoB), with a sufficient AIC (per the footnote). There may also
be AUJ (see below), but we don't need that yet.

The two defendants can be joined in a single lawsuit under FRCP 20 because they are both sued out of
the transaction of Buffy's accident and because a common issue is whether a proximity test was
conducted.

The E.D.Pa. can exercise IPJ over the defendants if a PA state court could (FRCP 4(k)(1)(A)), and PA has a
long-arm that goes to constitutional limits. For Faith, its advertising in PA, and its efforts to
consummate multiple sales in PA, constitute purposeful availment and therefore minimal contacts
(McGee). PA exercise of IPJ would be fair and reasonable because PA law will likely apply to at least
some substantive issues (maybe not all, see below), PA has an interest in protecting its residents from
dangerous products sent into its borders, Buffy has an interest in a convenient court location, key
evidence (Buffy & Dawn's testimony, hospital records, the truck) is in PA, and Faith's business in the area
suggests little inconvenience to it. With respect to XH, there are likely insufficient minimal contacts
relating Buffy's negligence theory and injury, XH, and Pennsylvania. Nicastro is almost directly on point
in that XH sold to a distributor over whom it exercised little control, and with the distributor selling to
the consumer. Stepping back, XH only sells to distributors in one location, and there is no suggestion
that it has any subsequent relationship to the trucks it sells. Buffy's best arguments to distinguish
Nicastro are (i) XH is domestic, so there is no prospect of international friction, and (ii) it appears that
several (10 from this set, according to Faith's advertising) of XH trucks, as opposed to a single scrap
metal cutter, were offered in PA (there may be more XH products in PA from Faith and from other
distributors, we don't know). But XH does no business in PA, does not advertise there, and maintains no
offices there. Faith's unilateral activity in moving a truck to PA does not create minimal contacts to XH
(WWW, Hanson). The fair and reasonable analysis for XH is largely the same as for Faith, except that the
absence of any XH business activity in PA suggests substantial inconvenience.

Venue is likely proper under 1391(b)(2) because sending the car to Buffy's house and the resulting
accident, a substantial part of the events giving rise to the claim, occurred in the E.D.Pa. (b)(1) does not
apply because there is no IPJ over XH, whose thus does not "reside" in the E.D.Pa. (b)(3) is not available
because venue is proper in E.D.Pa.

Regarding 12(b)(6), per the exam, the plaintiff need not allege negligence element (i), duty. Regarding
element (ii), we need to know first, whether the per se theory is available as a matter of HCOL (PA does
not recognize negligence per se, other states do), and (ii) whether the complaint alleges negligence per
se plausibly (Twiqbal). Causation and damages are alleged with specificity in paragraphs 6, 9, 10, 14,
and 15.

Regarding HCOL, the availability of negligence per se theory is analogous to the sections in the first
restatement handout as governed by the law of the place of the wrong, and is therefore substantive for
HCOL purposes. The E.D.Pa., using the Second Restatement rules applicable in PA (Klaxon), will begin
with a presumption that PA law applies because the injury occurred there. Perhaps remembering that
under the Second Restatement, we can apply the law of a state other than PA to the separate issue of
whether the per se theory is available, the presumption might well be overcome through the section 6
factors in favor of Virginia because (a) the alleged misconduct, the failure to test, occurred there, (b)
Virginia has an interested in regulating (mis)conduct within its borders (no misconduct occurred in PA),
(c) the availability of negligence per se has less to do with the injury (as to which PA law will govern)
than it does the XH-Faith relationship and sales transaction, which were centered in Virginia, where they
met and made their sales contract, (d) the VA per se negligence theory was design to prevent precisely
what happened here, so the policies of the area of law point to VA law. Under VA law, per se negligence
is available, and the complaint likely survives. Under PA law, with no per se negligence theory, the case
will be dismissed because the complaint does not otherwise allege a breach of the duty of care.

Regarding Twiqbal, the only conclusory, formulaic, or law-stating paragraphs are 11 and 13, so ignore
those. Paragraph 14, made coherent by the facts alleged in paragraphs 6-10, provides a plausible
allegation of breach of duty under the per se theory (element (ii)) and causation (element (iii)).
Paragraph 15 provides a plausible allegation of harm (element (iv)). The complaint likely survives a
Twiqbal attack.

The complaint does not comply with PA's Giles Act (procedural for HCOL purposes, as it governs
complaint content) because it does not specify the date of the failure to test (at least), something FRCP
8(a) does not require. Under the York test, non-compliance with this provision results in dismissal,
which is outcome-determinative, so the we use the Giles Act. Under the Harlan concurrence, Giles Act
Section 6.7(a) states PA's belief that this provision affects investment strategies, which is primary
conduct; if we believe that, we would use state law. For both the Scalia and Ginsburg applications of the
Hanna majority test, Rule 8(a) is valid because "really regulates procedure" (Sibbach) and thus does not
conflict with 2072(b). It concerns what must be alleged in a compliant, which is the manner and means
of administering a lawsuit. Regarding applicability and a conflict with the Giles Act, FRCP 8(a) specifies
that a plaintiff need file only a short, plain statement (bonus for noting that 8(d)(1), which we did not
cover in class, says that each allegation must be "simple and concise"), while the Giles Act requires more
(section 6.7 is even labeled "Heightened Pleading Standard"). This is likely a conflict, so Scalia would use
federal law, meaning no dismissal. Ginsburg might argue that a federal requirement of a short plain
statement does not speak to the facts that must be pled shortly and plainly, and that the Giles Act
addresses the latter issue, so there is no conflict, and we can apply the Giles Act and dismissed (see
Walker, and perhaps Scalia's dissent in Stewart, for analogous reasoning).

Because there the E.D.Pa. likely cannot exercise IPJ over XH, the case will likely be transferred or XH will
be dismissed. Whether it can be transferred depends on whether is another district where venue is
proper that could exercise IPJ over XH.

Question 2

The events that Maclay and Chase describe constitute negligence that does not depend on the per se
doctrine. A trier of fact would likely infer from the scratches on the front left fender and headlight that
truck that hit Buffy and Dawn was one of those that struck a lighting pole at The Spike, or perhaps the
truck that rear-ended the one in front of it at Faith's SC facility. If so, then Maclay or Chase or both had
reason to know that the proximity detector on that truck did not work. At a minimum, they had reason
to know that something was likely wrong with many of the trucks in the set. Both perceived this risk,
and took no action. That supports negligence without need for a per se theory. Buffy should allege in
detail this theory, adding as much detail as possible for both this theory and the per se negligence
theory.
NC, and SC likely would lack IPJ over XH (no contacts). DE and MD might lack IPJ over Faith (although
Faith sells vehicles there, none of those sales related to this lawsuit, and it would not be fair &
reasonable to adjudicate there given no witnesses, plaintiff interest, or state interest). E.D.Va is best.
Venue will be proper under (b)(1)/(c) (see below) and 1391(b)(2) because the failure to test (the per se
theory), the knowing disregard of the perceived risk (Maclay and Chase, simple negligence theory), and
the waiving off of a test (Chase, simple negligence) occurred there. Under 4(k)(1)(A) and VA's to-the-
limits long-arm, both XH and Faith purposefully availed themselves of the right to transact business in
E.D.Va. (selling and buying the trucks), with the way that business was conducted (see previous
sentence) giving rise to this lawsuit. That's minimal contacts. It would be fair and reasonable for the
E.D.Va. to exercise IPJ because VA has an interest in regulating wrongful conduct that occurs within its
borders, VA law may apply to some issues (see Q1 response), and key evidence (anything relating to the
collisions, copies of the sales contacts, witness testimony about the trucks) is located there.
Alternatively, for XH only, there may be Daimler "at home" general IPJ because it sells all of its vehicles
at The Spike, but this theory is unnecessary.

Question 3

Per above, in the E.D.Va., the jurisdictional and venue theories from Question 2 would apply equally to
the claims of all class members (Bristo-Meyers). E.D.Va. is also best because as an R2 state, we might
well get VA law on the HCOL issue of the availability of per se negligence, which would help
commonality (under Shutts, it would be permissible to apply VA law to all class claims because in each
case the failure to test occurred in VA). Because Buffy, the likely named plaintiff, would have complete
diversity and would be AIC-sufficient, supplemental SMJ would if necessary be available for the class
despite the presence of non-diverse (Supreme Tribe) or AIC-deficient (Exxon) unnamed class members.
In this (b)(3)/damages class we look for

    · numerosity (present because 94 >> 40);
    · superiority (likely met because the plaintiffs are spread out and a common course of conduct

         applied to all, although if Buffy's claim is representative, the amounts at issue might be enough
         to attract lawyers to individual litigation);
    · predominance/commonality (likely met if as a matter of HCOL the negligence per se theory for is
         available for all claims, see above, because then all were injured as a result of non-testing, but
         might not work otherwise because negligence would be specific to circumstances, such as what
         MacClay and Chase saw for each set of trucks, plus there might be different damages
         facts/evidence for injuries to body versus property);
    · typicality (likely met if the negligence per se theory works because Buffy's harm occurred in the
         same way as the unnamed class members, with the contrary argument being that Buffy's
         damages were extensive and therefore may be atypical);
    · and representativeness (same as commonality plus typicality, there being no indication of
         conflicts of interest).

Question 4
There is appellate jurisdiction under either 1291 (the judgment constitutes a "final decision") or
1292(a)(1) (the appeal from an issuance of an injunction). Regarding the appellants' argument, the
district judge's FoF are so vague as to constitute a failure to make them, violating FRCP 52. The
appellate court cannot understand the nature of the district court's reasoning, and per Q5, we cannot
apply preclusion law well because we cannot know what the judge decided.
Question 5
Claim preclusion is irrelevant; Dawn does not want to preclude herself.

The rendering court was a federal court in PA, so federal common law ("FCL") tells us to use either the
uniform FCL non-mutual offensive collateral estoppel (NMOCE) framework from Parklane and Russell (if
the basis of SMJ was AUJ) or PA NMOCE law (diversity). But PA NMOCE law is the same as FCL, so this
doesn't matter. XH and Faith had sufficient incentive (fear of an injunction) and opportunity (trial) to
litigate well, and there's no suggestion of access to proof issues. The pending appeal is irrelevant. We
have the same factual issue (whether XH and Faith were negligent) and actually litigated (trial).
Necessary to the decision is lacking (Russell/FCL). The courts FoF do not distinguish between negligence
per se and failure to act after seeing collisions, so Dawn cannot prove this element. Finally, under
Parklane, NMOCE likely depends on whether there was Smith-Grable SMJ in Buffy's lawsuit. If Buffy's
case was diversity only, then Dawn could not have joined/intervened because (a) as a SC domiciliary, she
is non-diverse from Faith, and (b) under 1367(b), her AIC-deficient suit is a claim by a plaintiff (Dawn)
against a person made a party (pick either defendant) under Rule 20 (listed). But if Smith-Grable AUJ
was available to Buffy, FRCP 20 (same accident, common issue in failure to exercise due care) would
have allowed joinder, with 1367 providing SSMJ. (Note AUJ unavailable to Dawn because she is not
asserting a negligence per se claim.)

Smith-Grable was likely unavailable, even if VA law were used to allow the per se negligence theory.
Violation of the Giles regulations is embedded within the state law/negligence COA, but non-compliance
with the regulation was not contested (XH and Faith contested causation, not breach). Further, this is a
fact-bound question, not interpretation of federal law but rather compliance with it. Finally, allowing
negligence per se based on a violation of federal regulations to support AUJ would sweep too many
cases into federal court.

The VA right to a jury trial does not prevent preclusion (Parklane & Beacon).

Question 6:
The Twiqbal standard relies on the premise that judges can tell, before discovery of facts and based on
allegations only (no evidence), whether a claim will succeed. Judges have not just truth sense but crystal
balls. But judges have neither. Judgments about how to apply 12(b)(6)/8(a)(2) should be based on
empirical examination of complaints, not judicial guesswork about the quality of trial judge guesswork.
In contrast, the abuse of discretion standard of review, which is justified in part on the idea that trial
judges' practice at making certain kinds of decisions renders them better than appellate courts at
making those decisions, seems justified. Actively managing lots of cases will provide trial judges with
some idea of how litigants react to ruling and pretrial structures. Here, experience seems likely to
matter.

Question 7:
One solution is court-based self-help materials. Such materials are hard to write well because they
require casting complex legal issues into simple language. They are hard to scale because U.S. civil
procedures (especially the unwritten ones) are so localized, so this idea requires as many sets of
materials in a state as one has counties or municipalities. Finally, parties may not be able to obtain self-
help materials when they need them and in a format they can access, given search costs, the digital
divide, and/or photocopying costs.

# EXAM 2021-final — 2021 Greiner Civil Procedure 2 Final Exam

Source: content/course/exams/2021-greiner-civpro2-final.md · model answer: content/course/exams/2021-greiner-civpro2-model-answer.md

## 2021-final · Exam text

Word limit: 2400 words

1. HLS will administer the exam according to standard one-day take-home time limits. Spend a
substantial portion of your exam time getting organized before writing. You will botch up this
exam if you don't take the time to get organized before you start writing.

2. Your response must be typed using the Exam4 exam software. Your response can be no
more than the word limit above, everything included. Should you depart from the length
limit, the first portion of your exam, up to the word limit above, will be graded and the rest
ignored. HLS will administer the exam delivery and submission system.

3. You may not communicate with any person other than the exam administrators regarding the
substance of this exam until after the 1L exam period is over. The reason for this prohibition
is that, each year, some students take the exam on a date other than its scheduled
administration date. Thus, do not think that you may discuss this exam immediately after
you hand it in. Wait until the 1L exam period is over. Note also that the prohibition above
includes librarians. According to HLS policy: "[S]tudents may not communicate with the
instructor nor raise questions or concerns about the content of an examination once it is in
progress and the Registrar's Office will not disseminate additional information about an exam
once it is in progress. To maintain anonymity and to ensure compliance by students with this
policy, instructors should not communicate in any way, including through email, with students
during an examination." You may consult with any non-human resource as you do this exam,
but I encourage you not to consult anything other than class materials. You need nothing other
than the material we covered in class to respond fully to the questions on this exam, and time
spent searching for such materials will take away from your analysis.

4. If you feel that there is a mistake in the exam, such as that a question is unclear or that it
refers to something that it should not or that there is a critical typographical error, then do the
following. First, in your answer, explain what you believe to be the mistake. Second, explain
the implications of the mistake. Third, make an assumption that is reasonable given the fact
pattern and applicable law to resolve the error. Fourth, proceed with the remainder of the exam.

5. Your answer must be your own work. Any quotation from any source must be accompanied
by a corresponding reference (but use short references, e.g., "International Shoe," or "FRCP
23").

6. Each question's weight is specified (out of 65 total points). Time and word-count may be
issues for you during the exam. Answer questions efficiently, and feel free to take into account
the number of points available for each question. Don't forget your test-taking skills.

7. Do not put your name, or otherwise identify yourself, on your response.

8. Deal with all issues fairly raised, even if you think another issue would be dispositive of a
matter in the real world.
9. If you believe additional information (factual or legal) is needed to deal with an issue, say so
and make explicit whatever assumptions you need to proceed, but make these assumptions
reasonable and interesting. Similarly, if at any point in this question you find that you do not
know what the law is on a particular subject, identify the issue as to which there is uncertainty,
then discuss the various options as to what the law could or should be, noting the advantages or
disadvantages of each option.

10. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: "SMJ" for subject matter
jurisdiction; "IPJ" for in personam jurisdiction; "DJ" for diversity jurisdiction; "FQJ" for federal
question jurisdiction; "CNOF" for common nucleus of operative facts; and "DPC" for due
process clause.

11. All of the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the
law described in this exam. Use the scenarios only for the purposes of this exam.

12. Citing authority is optional. You can get full credit without citation if you explain and apply
the relevant principle. That said, citing authority could well save you time & words. For
example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, International Shoe," instead of, "Under the test the
Court articulated in International Shoe and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam
jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such
exercise, and only if the defendant has `minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the
exercise of such jurisdiction would be fair and reasonable under the circumstances."

                              ********************************

                                                    Relevant state laws

All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes extending to the limits of the United States
constitution. All states relevant to this exam, except Ohio, have declaratory judgment statutes
equivalent to 28 U.S.C. § 2201. Ohio's declaratory judgment statute is the same as § 2201 but also has
the following provision:

Ohio L. Ann § 2201(b)(26.9): Deposit Required in Declaratory Judgment Statute Actions
The Legislature of Ohio finds that too many frivolous "strike suits" seeking declaratory judgments of
non-liability have been filed in Ohio courts, frequently by large corporations seeking declarations of non-
liability. These strike suit plaintiffs are engaging in risky conduct that they then attempt to immunize
from liability by using declaratory judgment lawsuits against injured parties who cannot adequately
respond to their suits. We the Legislature of Ohio seek to deter such risky conduct. Therefore, when
initiating any declaratory judgment lawsuit filed in any court in Ohio, the plaintiff shall deposit with the
court 10% of the amount in controversy in the lawsuit, to be held by the court until final judgment in the
lawsuit to pay the defendant's litigation costs and attorneys' fees if the court ultimately decides not to
issue the declaratory judgment. Any lawsuit in which the plaintiff does not comply with this requirement
shall be immediately dismissed.

Ohio, Delaware, and Nevada use the Second Restatement for horizontal choice of law. Texas, Vermont,
Michigan use the First Restatement for horizontal choice of law.
All states relevant to this exam have a standard breach of written contract cause of action. Breach of
written contract requires a plaintiff to prove that there was a written contract, that the defendant
breached it, and that the breached caused the plaintiff harm. Punitive damages are never available in a
breach of contract lawsuit.

All states relevant to this exam have a standard products liability cause of action. Products liability
requires a plaintiff to prove that the defendant sold the plaintiff a product, that the product was
defectively designed or manufactured, and that the defect caused the plaintiff's injuries. Products
liability is a tort. Ohio does not allow punitive damages in products liability cases. All other states
relevant to this exam do allow punitive damages in products liability cases, but in these states punitive
damages are only available for a plaintiff who has suffered physical injury. You may assume that the
availability of punitive damages is substantive for horizontal choice of law purposes.

Texas' limitations period for products liability lawsuits is three years. Ohio, Nevada, and Delaware all
have five-year limitations periods for products liability cases. Michigan and Vermont have six-year
limitations periods for products liability cases. All states relevant to this exam have ten-year limitations
periods for breach of a written contract lawsuits. All limitations periods relevant to this exam begin to
run on the date that the plaintiff suffered the harm/injury forming the basis of the lawsuit.

In Texas, Delaware, and Nevada, limitations-based dismissals are on the merits for claim preclusion
purposes. In Ohio, Michigan, and Vermont, limitations-based dismissals are not on the merits for claim
preclusion purposes. Under federal common law, limitations-based dismissals are not on the merits for
claim preclusion purposes.

Texas law includes the following provisions:

Tex. L. Ann. § 4.10: Pre-judgment seizure of property in support of suit for breach of a written
contract
A party to a contract who alleges that they have suffered injury caused by another's breach of a written
contract, which contract includes a right to pursue a pre-judgment seizure of property, may petition a
court of this state for an order seizing any property pursuant to the contract, provided that

    (a) the petitioner/plaintiff files with the court
              (1) a complaint containing a short plain statement of the facts constituting the alleged
                   breach of the written contract, of the amount of damages the breach caused, and of the
                   property to be seized;
              (2) a copy of the written contract; and
              (3) a statement swearing under oath (i.e., under penalty of perjury) that the facts alleged in
                   the short plain statement are true to the best of the petitioner/plaintiff's knowledge,
                   and that the copy of the contract filed is true and correct.

    (b) In addition, the plaintiff/petitioner shall file a litigation bond equal to the value of the property
         seized assuring payment of the respondent/defendant's damages in the event that the seizure is
         adjudicated to be wrongful and the petitioner/plaintiff is unable to pay such damages.

Tex. L. Ann. § 4.20: Seizure to establish traditional quasi-in-rem jurisdiction in tort lawsuit
A party who alleges that they have been damaged by the commission of a tort by a defendant may
establish jurisdiction over an action in a court of this state using a traditional quasi-in-rem theory, as
follows. The plaintiff shall file with the court

    (a) a complaint including a short plain statement
         (1) identifying the property to be seized,
         (2) describing the facts constituting the alleged tort, and
         (3) stating the resulting damages; and

    (b) a statement swearing under oath (i.e., under penalty of perjury) that the facts alleged in the
         short plain statement are true to the best of the plaintiff's knowledge.

Tex. L. Ann § 4.50: Procedure to be followed in actions initiate under §§ 4.10 and/or 4.20
A judge of a court of this state, having reviewed the petitioner/plaintiff's filings in an action pursued
under sections 4.10 or 4.20 (or both simultaneously) and being satisfied of a petitioner/plaintiff's
compliance with those sections, shall order seizure of the property named in the complaint(s). The
property shall remain in the court's control until further order of the court. The judge shall schedule a
hearing to take place within 20 days of its seizure order, at which time the judge shall permit the
respondent/defendant to introduce any evidence and to make any argument to the effect that the
seizure order should be rescinded. In addition, at the time of the seizure order, the judge shall require
the plaintiff/petitioner to use means reasonably likely of success to provide the respondent/defendant
with notice of the pending proceeding and the seizure, including paper or electronic copies of the filings
in the lawsuit.

[Note: Throughout this exam pattern, remember that a state court can constitutionally seize property
only if that property is located within that state's geographic limits.]

Texas has four U.S. Districts; Dallas is in the N.D. Tex. Ohio has two U.S. Districts; Toledo and Cleveland
are on the N.D. Ohio. Michigan has two U.S. Districts; Detroit and Ann Arbor are in the E.D. Mich.
Vermont has one U.S. District, the D. Vt. Delaware has one U.S. District, the D. Del. Nevada has one U.S.
District, the D. Nev.

                                                             Facts

Inigo Montoya was born in Detroit, MI, and grew up without family, living largely in foster homes. He
loved vintage computers and computer games, particularly games with revenge themes. After
graduating from high school in Detroit, and wanting eventually to pursue a career in the revenge
business, he began to support himself by obtaining computers from the 1970s and early 1980s, restoring
them so that they could play games from that era, and selling them to customers with whom he
connected on the Internet. His business was a modest success, and having always wanted to travel, he
bought himself a full-service recreational vehicle ("RV"), which he christened "Roberts," to use as his
home. Hating the cold, in 2015 at the age of 23, he drove Roberts to Pensacola, FL, and rented an RV
plot for one year, registering to vote there, obtaining a FL driver's license, and receiving mail at a local
post office box. Financially, he opened a business checking account with the Bank of Florin at a
Pensacola Branch, giving the bank his RV plot address and his post office box. The Bank of Florin was
incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Reno, NV. The Bank of Florin operated a
massive computer complex in warehouses outside Reno, where it kept all of its business records
(including backups). The bank had 2000 branch locations, and approximately 15,000 ATM machines,
across the United States. Although Montoya did almost all of his banking online (he received all of his
statements electronically), occasionally he visited Bank of Florin branches wherever he happened to be,
