sometimes to deposit old-style personal checks that his customers sent him as payment. Perhaps
illegally, Montoya never paid taxes.

In 2016, when the lease on his Pensacola RV plot expired, Montoya secretly closed his Pensacola post
office box (telling no one) and began to drive around the country in Roberts, parking for as long as he
felt like staying, then moving on. He never stayed in one location for more than four months. He
received any necessary mail at a FedEx facility near wherever he happened to be (he always told people
with mail he wanted to receive, which FedEx facility to use, and did not worry about any other mail). As
he began to travel, he bought an old Polaroid instant camera and a scanner, and he began to document
his travels on his personal website, youkilledmyfather.net. He also ran his business from this website.
He listed his (now-closed) Pensacola P.O. Box on the website, but he never used it. His business model
was as follows. Montoya perused various traditional and non-traditional outlets, mostly online, across
the world for no-longer-functioning vintage computer hardware as well as vintage software packages,
particularly pieces that no longer worked. He bought these items cheaply and had them shipped to a
FedEx wherever he happened to be at the time. He then refurbished and repaired the vintage items,
and marketed them on youkilledmyfather.net, which could be viewed anywhere in the world. He sold
the products to customers from whom he received orders online, shipping them to addresses and
receiving payments via Paypal. He transferred the payments to his Bank of Florin account electronically
whenever he needed funds. Montoya paid money in and out of this Florin account as needed to support
his business, the upkeep on Roberts, and his few personal needs.

In early 2017, Montoya finished work on a multi-month project, the restoration of a rare first edition of
a Xerox Soprano personal computer, capable of running Guilder, one of the first multi-game personal
computer software packages. Montoya listed the device on youkilledmyfather.net, with accompanying
software, at $110,000.

Buttercup and Westley, an unmarried couple in their 40s whose relationship had lasted over 15 years
but who lived in separate apartments, contacted Montoya about the device via youkilledmyfather.net's
chat function. Buttercup lived all her life in Toledo, Ohio, but had met Westley on one of her trips to
Ann Arbor. Westley grew up and lived all his life Ann Arbor, Michigan, about an hour north of Toledo.
Neither made a lot of money, but both were fanatic about vintage computer systems, and they decided
to spend much of their life savings on the Soprano computer, thinking that they could play on it for a
while and then resell it at a profit. Buttercup conducted all of the negotiations via the
youkilledmyfather.net chat function from her Toledo apartment, keeping Westley informed about the
status of the negotiations when the two met in Ann Arbor. Buttercup began all of her posts to
youkilledmyfather.net's chat function, "Hello from not-so-sunny northern Ohio! Almost as bleak as a
fire swamp!". At the time of his posting to the website's chat functions to negotiate with Buttercup,
Montoya happened to be in Oklahoma, parked outside Oklahoma City.

The negotiations between Montoya and Buttercup resulted in the following arrangement: Westley
agreed to pay Montoya $30,000 via a personal check drawn on a Michigan bank. Buttercup agreed to
pay $80,000 via a personal check drawn on an Ohio bank. Each would separately mail their checks to a
Fed Ex facility in Oklahoma City. When he received the checks, Montoya would ship the computer and
accompanying disks to a FedEx facility in Ann Arbor, Michigan, with instructions to allow Westley to pick
it up. Buttercup told Montoya that Westley and she were looking forward to using their new machine
for the first time on a vacation they were planning just outside Burlington, Vermont, where they
intended to stay in their favorite bed and breakfast (in a historic building, constructed in the 1800s) and
visit nearby lakes. All three parties electronically signed1 a written contract that, in addition to
describing the computer to be sold and specifying the price, included the following provisions:

    · Warranty of fitness: The seller warrants that the product sold pursuant to this contract is safe
         and suitable for its intended use.

    · Right to prejudgment seizure: The parties agree that in the event of a breach of this contract by
         either the seller or the buyers, the damaged party may initiate a lawsuit to seize any property
         owned by the breaching party in a court with jurisdiction over that property to assure payment
         of any damages that the same court finds, after an appropriate adjudication, to be owed. The
         parties agree, however, that nothing in this provision waives any constitutional rights of any
         party, and that any seizure pursuant to this paragraph must be accomplished consistently with
         the United States Constitution.

The transaction went off without a hitch. Westley and Buttercup mailed their separate checks to the
FedEx location that Montoya specified outside of Oklahoma City. Montoya received the checks, but did
not immediately cash them. After mailing the computer from Oklahoma City to the specified FedEx
location in Ann Arbor, MI, he hopped into Roberts and started driving south, intending to spend some
time in Dallas, Texas. When he arrived, he rented an RV plot for Roberts in Dallas. The "lease" on the
plot was for four months. When he arrived in the Dallas area, he visited a local bank outlet of Florin bank
in person, deposited several customer checks including those of Westley and Buttercup, and did some
other banking there related to his ongoing business.

On April 14, 2017, Westley retrieved the vintage computer from the FedEx in Ann Arbor, Michigan. He
then drove his car to pick up Buttercup in Toledo, and the couple drove through the night to Burlington,
Vermont, arriving on April 17, 2017. Too excited to go to bed, they checked into their bed and
breakfast, plugged it in, and started to play the vintage games. Buttercup was playing, Westley
watching, when the computer caught on fire and exploded. Buttercup suffered moderate burns. She
was transported via helicopter to a hospital in Boston, MA, where she spent three days before being
released to recover back at her home in Ohio. Her medical bills were approximately $100,000. Westley
suffered no physical injury; he stayed at a friend's house in the Boston area to be with Buttercup while
she recovered, responding to her every request with, "As you wish." Back in Burlington, the fire had
been contained in the couple's bed and breakfast room before spreading very far. The fire chief on the
scene determined that the fire started at or near the outlet into which Buttercup and Westley had
plugged their new computer, but did not attempt to determine further whether the wiring on the outlet
was old or faulty, or whether there was something wrong with the computer's power cable. Buttercup
took the damaged computer back with her to her Toledo, Ohio apartment.

Near the end of May, 2017, Westley and Buttercup received their bank statements and saw that
Montoya had cashed their checks at the Bank of Florin branch in Dallas, TX. They wrote to Montoya
using the chat function of youkilledmyfather.net. They told Montoya about the fire and Buttercup's
injuries, and informed him that they thought that faulty wiring on the computer's power cable caused
the fire. They demanded that Montoya pay Buttercup $100,000 in medical bills plus $50,000 in pain and
suffering, and that he pay Westley $25,000 for emotional distress. Alternatively, they demanded that
Montoya refund exactly what they each paid for the computer. Montoya refused, writing, "There was
nothing wrong with the computer's power system. I wired that part of the computer myself, and I know
what I'm doing. If you want to sue someone, sue the bed and breakfast where you stayed. Its wiring
caused the accident."

1 You may assume that this contract is deemed to be in writing and is binding on the parties.
In early June of 2017, Westley and Buttercup consulted Valerie of the law firm of Valerie, Vizzini, &
Fezzik in Toledo. Valerie told the couple that she thought she could storm the castle for them (that is,
represent them), but that she would need a several-thousand-dollar retainer before proceeding. Not
having that much money on hand (they had just bought the expensive computer and had Buttercup's
medical deductibles to pay), Buttercup and Westley started saving.

Meanwhile, Montoya traveled around the country. After his four months in Dallas, he spent time in the
Pacific Northwest, in the Arizona dessert, and in Utah. Valerie kept track of where Montoya was by
reviewing his travel log entries on youkilledmyfather.net.

It took them about three years, but Buttercup and Westley finally managed to scrape together enough
money to pay Valerie's retainer. When they paid it, Valerie checked youkilledmyfather.net again, and
saw that Montoya was heading back to the Dallas area from Utah. In early June of 2020, Montoya
arrived and rented the same RV plot he'd used in 2017 for Roberts, with the lease to last for three
months. He posted several photos of the plot, along with a map showing its exact location, on
youkilledmyfather.net. Valerie took note of the location and contacted Buttercup and Westley to tell
them it was time to take action.

On June 16, 2020, Buttercup (with Valerie as her lawyer) filed a lawsuit in a Texas state court in the
County of Dallas, which encompasses both the plot Montoya rented for Roberts and the Bank of Florin
branch he visited. Westley did not participate in this lawsuit. The lawsuit was filed pursuant to Tex. L.
Ann. § 4.20. To initiate the lawsuit, Valerie, on Buttercup's behalf, filed

    · A complaint detailing the facts described above and alleging that the wiring around the power
         cable in the computer Montoya sold to Buttercup (and Westley) was defective, causing the fire
         that resulted in Buttercup's injuries. The lawsuit sought $500,000, calculated as $100,000
         medical bills, $50,000 for pain and suffering, and $350,000 in punitive damages.

    · A statement under penalty of perjury from Buttercup swearing that the facts alleged in the
         complaint were true.

    · A document titled "Motion to Seize Bank Account Pursuant to § 4.20" naming Montoya's Bank
         of Florin account as the property to be seized. This document provided the address of the Bank
         of Florin Dallas Branch as well as the address for the Bank of Florin's principal place of business
         in Nevada.

    · A document titled "Proposal Regarding Notice Pursuant to Tex. L. Ann § 4.50" suggesting that
         notice of the seizure be given to Montoya by special overnight certified mailing to the Pensacola
         P.O. Box listed on youkilledmyfather.net, and via the chat function on the same website.

On July 7, 2020, Judge Humperdinck issued an order seizing Montoya's Bank of Florin account and
scheduling a hearing on the seizure for July 27, 2020. A copy of the order was provided by hand to the
Bank of Florin Dallas branch the same day, and another copy was mailed to the Bank of Florin's Nevada
headquarters. On July 8, the Bank stopped all withdrawals of funds out of Montoya's account. In
addition, on July 7, Judge Humperdinck ordered Valerie, as Buttercup's lawyer, to provide notice as
specified in the "Proposal," meaning via special overnight certified mailing to the Pensacola P.O. Box and
posting to the chat function of youkilledmyfather.net. When Valerie attempted to use the website on
July 8, however, she found it unavailable due to a server malfunction. Because the P.O. Box had been
shut down years earlier, the certified mail receipt came back unclaimed. On July 27, 2020, Montoya did
not appear at the hearing Judge Humperdinck had scheduled, so the judge entered an order stating that
"this court has traditional quasi-in-rem jurisdiction in this matter" and that "Montoya's Bank of Florin
account will remain seized until further order of this court. Meanwhile, adjudication of Buttercup's
products liability lawsuit shall proceed."

Meanwhile, Montoya was in distress. Youkilledmyfather.net had gone down due to a server error on
July 6, 2020. He called his internet service provider, but by July 21, he still had not succeeded in reviving
the website. He had also tried to do his online banking, but found he could make no withdrawals from
his Bank of Florin account. On July 22, he sought help from the Dallas law firm of Rungen & Max.

Question 1 (17 points): You are an associate at Rungen & Max. Max, a partner, asks you for a miracle.
He has interviewed Montoya and has otherwise used his wizard-like powers to find out the facts
detailed above. Max asks what doctrines and arguments discussed in your civil procedure class he
could assert on Montoya's behalf to argue against the court's seizure order and/or its assertion of
jurisdiction. Explain your answer.
Note: Do not consider limitations issues in your response to this question.
Hint: If you do not know where something is located, identify places where it might be located, and
articulate the policy strengths and weaknesses of each location.

**********************

Max decides to file an answer to the complaint on Montoya's behalf and also a motion to dissolve the
seizure order. In response to the motion to dissolve the seizure, Valerie argues on Buttercup's behalf as
follows: Before the court seriously considers dissolving the seizure order in the tort lawsuit, it should
consider the fact that Buttercup wanted to assert a breach of contract lawsuit and to try to seize
Montoya's bank account pursuant to Tex. L. Ann. § 4.10. The problem was that § 4.10(b) would have
required Buttercup to purchase a litigation bond, and she could not afford one, having barely saved
enough to afford Valerie's fees. Valerie asks that the court declare that the imposition of a litigation
bond requirement against someone who cannot afford to buy one violates Buttercup's procedure due
process and access to justice rights.

Question 2 (4 points): Max asks you whether you think Valerie's argument on the litigation bond will
succeed. What do you think? Explain your answer.

**********************

Judge Humperdinck denies the motion to dissolve the seizure order, and litigation continues in the
Dallas court. The parties proceed in discovery.

As the state court litigation is ongoing, Max comes into your office again. Putting aside the issue of the
legality of the bank account seizure for a moment, Max is worried about the losing on the merits, and
he's particularly concerned about punitive damages. He's also concerned about a future lawsuit from
Westley, perhaps also in a Texas state court. He thinks that Montoya might do better in federal court.
He is considering a recommendation to Montoya that the latter file a federal court declaratory judgment
action against both Buttercup and Westley requesting a declaration that the computer Montoya
manufactured was not defective and that even if it was, punitive damages are not available.
Meanwhile, Max knows that Montoya cannot raise a lot of money immediately to pay litigation
expenses or deposits because, among other things, the Florin bank account remains frozen.
Question 3 (22 points): Can Montoya sue Buttercup and Westley, together in one lawsuit, in any
federal court for a declaratory judgment of the type that Max describes? Assuming he could do so, in
what federal court (geographically) can he sue? And which location would be best for Montoya?
Explain your answers.

*********

Question 4 (6 points): Max is thinking some more about the idea of a federal court declaratory
judgment lawsuit. He has two additional questions. First, are there any arguments (from our civil
procedure class) not covered in your answer to Question 3 that Valerie might assert on Buttercup's
behalf to prevent the federal court lawsuit from going forward, and if so, would any such arguments
work? Second, Max would rather not bother with a jury in these circumstances. If Montoya sues for a
declaratory judgment, will Buttercup and Westley have to respond with their own federal court
lawsuit; and regardless of whether they have to respond, if they demand a jury trial, will they be
entitled to one? Explain your answers.

**********

Max decides not to recommend a federal court lawsuit to Montoya. Instead, he files on Montoya's
behalf a motion in the Texas state court case to dismiss for a failure by Buttercup to file the products
liability claim within the applicable limitations period. Max then winds up back in your office.

Question 5 (8 points): Max asks whether you think the Texas court, as a matter of horizontal choice of
law, is likely to grant the motion to dismiss Buttercup's products liability lawsuit on limitations
grounds? Next, Max asks you, suppose the Texas court does dismiss on limitations grounds. Max is
worried about another lawsuit by Buttercup, maybe joined this time by Westley, for either products
liability or breach of contract, in a court of a different state, or in federal court. If Buttercup or
Westley or both together sue Montoya in another state or federal court, could Montoya use the
doctrine of preclusion against either or both? Explain your answers.

**********

Note: this question does not use the hypothetical fact pattern articulated above.

Question 6 (8 points): In our last few sessions of the semester, we discussed how the law sometimes
treats a certified class as a new legal entity (separate from the individual class members' claims), and
sometimes treats a certified class as "joinder on steroids," that is, a large-scale case of joinder.
Identify one legal doctrine we discussed in which a class is treated as a separate legal entity, and one
legal doctrine in which a class is treated as a large-scale joinder. Next, identify one doctrinal area in
which it is still not clear whether the court system will treat classes as new legal entities or large-scale
joinders. Explain your response briefly. Then write a few sentences explaining which of the two
treatments you prefer, and why.

## 2021-final · Instructor model answer

Question 1
The initial attempt to provide notice to Montoya was likely reasonable (Mullane) in that Montoya
regularly communicates, and did communicate with Buttercup and Westley ("BW"), via
youkilledmyfather.net, and because mailing to a P.O. Box Montoya listed there is logical. When Valerie
found the website down and received the certified slip unclaimed, however, she had a duty try other
reasonable means (Flowers). The easiest and highly reliable means available would be to serve Montoya
personally at the RV plot he'd rented; Valerie had taken note of that location. Insufficient notice likely
renders the seizure invalid.
The seizure scheme likely does not provide sufficient opportunity to be heard. On the positive side, a
judge (not a clerk) issues the order, the plaintiff must file a FRCP8-like statement, the statement must be
sworn true (all lowering the risk of erroneous determination), and a hearing on the seizure must occur
somewhat promptly (20 days) of the order (reducing the threat to the defendant's interests). But torts,
including this one, are fact-specific and often (as here) require live testimony (Doehr) to adjudicate well;
the plaintiff need not file a bond (these two show high risk of error); the plaintiff need not, and this
plaintiff did not, have a property interest in the item seized (lower plaintiff interest); there is no exigency
(Humperdinck did not hurry his seizure order, plaintiffs filed 3 year after the explosion) because an
ordinary IPJ suit can protect BW's interests (lower plaintiff interest); and Montoya needs the bank
account to live and work (high interest). Montoya's wanderlust might make SoP difficult, but he is
domestic and makes his whereabouts known, and there is no concern of asset waste (lower plaintiff
interest)
The assertion of quasi-in-rem jurisdiction is likely invalid because the bank account is likely not
"located" in Texas. Location options for the incorporeal bank account include:

    · Dallas: Perhaps HvB suggests that the account follows the account-holder, and the account
         holder's current location is somewhat ascertainable, but that means the location will be
         transient and uncertain, plus a bank account for Montoya is an asset not a debt.

    · Pensacola: Montoya and Florin might anticipate this location as the place where their
         relationship vis-à-vis this account began, and is the likely state of Montoya's domicile (see
         below), but no third party or to a court contemplated a pre-notice seizure would know, making
         it hard to administer. In addition, account holders sometimes have little current relationship
         with the place where they opened the account, making this location seem arbitrary (although
         the same is often true of a human being's domicile).

    · Reno or Delaware: These are certain and easily ascertainable. HvB suggests one of these two (a
         bank account is a debt the bank owes the account holder). Reno is likely stronger because a
         bank's PPoB is a reasonable proxy for its system of records (all a modern bank is). Either choice
         might be fair to Montoya because he can be deemed to have had knowledge of these locations
         when he chose BoF as his bank; if he wanted a different location, he could have chosen a
         different bank.

Even if the account is in Texas, QIR might itself be unconstitutional. One interpretation of Schaeffer,
focusing on the strangeness of the DE sequestration statute there, leaves QIR undisturbed; if so, QIR
might be OK here if the account is in Texas. But taking Schaeffer at its word (despite the Scalia Burnham
opinion), we'd apply Shoe. Texas' statutes authorize the seizure, and its long-arm goes to constitutional
limits, but non-resident Montoya lacks minimal contacts connected to this litigation and Texas. None of
the (i) work on or shipping of the computer, (ii) negotiations with BW, or (iii) receipt of the checks,
occurred in Texas. Montoya's check cashing and mostly temporary physical presence in Texas is likely
not enough. His website is visible everywhere in the world, and nothing in it is Texas-specific. Nor
would the exercise of jurisdiction be F&R: Texas has little interest in this transaction (no conduct
occurred in Texas nor Texas resident is involved), Texas substantive law will not apply, no evidence is
located in Texas, and Buttercup has little interest in litigating so far from home.
Question 2
Valerie's argument will fail under Matthews. The requirement that one seeking to seize property file a
litigation bond is less threatening to their interests than is a refusal to appoint counsel when a
fundamental interest is at stake. Buttercup's interest, assuring that assets will be available if she
prevails, is monetary and of lesser magnitude than either incarceration (Turner) or parental rights
(Lassiter), for which there was no A2J remedy. Buttercup can still sue for BoWC (or products liability)
without the seizure, admittedly with more risk that Montoya will hide his assets (no indication that he
will do so, despite is wanderlust). Courts believe that the market signal the bond provides reduces the
risk of erroneous determination; they ignore the fact that inability to pay even a small amount distorts
that market. A bond protects Montoya's interests (considered under Doehr) by assuring compensation
for costs and damages if the seizure is unlawful. Texas has little separate interest.

Question 3

Rule 20 allows Montoya to sue BW together in a single lawsuit for a declaratory judgment of non-
liability (i.e. for a declaration of the $ he must pay to BW, which he contends is $0 because the computer
wiring was not defective) because there is only one transaction (the computer fire) and a common issue
(computer (non)defectiveness).

There might not be SMJ in a suit that includes Westley, a Michigan domiciliary. There is no federal issue,
embedded or COA, so no AUJ. Re DJ, if Montoya's domicile is Michigan, there is no complete diversity
(Strawbridge). Perhaps a one-year RV plot rental, registering to vote, and obtaining a PO box constitutes
living plus an intention to stay indefinitely (Mas), in which case Montoya's domicile is now Florida. But
the declaratory judgment against Westley is AIC-deficient (at most $55,000), so 1332 is unavailable. And
while the claim against Westley is from the same case or controversy (see R20 discussion) as that against
Buttercup (OH domicile, $500,000 AIC, so diversity, and anchoring under 1367(a)), that claim is by a
plaintiff (Montoya) against a person (Westley) made a party under Rule 20 where the claim is
inconsistent with 1332 (AIC deficient), so 1367(b) prohibits SSMJ.

Regarding venue, 1391(b)(1) is unavailable because BW are domiciled in different states (see above),
1391(c)(1). But under 1391(b)(2), substantial events likely occurred in N.D. Ohio (Buttercup's
negotiations), perhaps the E.D. MI (W picked up computer there, not clear if that's enough), and
definitely D. VT (accident), making 1391(b)(3) irrelevant.

Regarding IPJ, under Rule 4(k)(1)(A), we'd ask if any of Ohio, Michigan, and VT state courts could
exercise over IPJ over both BW. For any specific IPJ, all states have long-arms to constitutional limits.

    · Ohio: Ohio has general IPJ over Buttercup. Regarding minimal contacts relating Westley, the
         litigation, and Ohio, Westley made Buttercup, in Ohio (as she always told Montoya), his agent
         for the computer purchase negotiations; that said, the negotiations were online. Ohio might
         fairly and reasonably exercise IPJ over Westley, given the parties' expectations that Ohio law
         might be involved (from the negotiations), the convenience for Westley (he's in Ohio all the
         time), and his relationship with Buttercup. Some evidence, definitely the computer (key for
         deciding who is at fault) and perhaps any notes Buttercup took during the negotiations, are in
         Ohio. Montoya has no special interest in litigating in Ohio. Ohio has an interest in protecting
         Buttercup, but none vis-à-vis Westley.
    · Michigan: Michigan has general IPJ over Westley. Regarding Buttercup and minimal contacts,
         she instructed Montoya to ship the computer to Ann Arbor and instructed her "agent" (Westley)
         to pick it up there. Michigan might fairly and reasonably exercise IPJ over Buttercup given her
         relationship with Westley and the resulting lack of inconvenience, but Michigan has no interest
         vis-à-vis Buttercup, no Michigan law will apply, no evidence relevant to the case is in Michigan.
         Montoya may be familiar with Michigan, but has no interest in litigating there.

    · Vermont: BW drove the computer to the state and used it there, where the accident occurred,
         likely constituting minimal contacts. Vermont could fairly and reasonably exercise IPJ over BW
         because it has an interest in regulating dangerous products brought inside its borders, its law
         may apply, it's somewhat convenient for BW (they vacation there), and key evidence (wiring) is
         there.

    · Likely, no other state has minimal contacts. Regarding Oklahoma, in particular, nothing
         whatsoever connects Westley to OK.

Regarding HCOL, E.D. Mich. and D. Vt. would use R1 (Klaxon) and thus apply Vermont (place of injury)
law to the products liability tort, making punitive damages available. The N.D. Ohio is the best forum for
Montoya because it would apply R2, raising at least a chance that the court would apply Ohio's
prohibition on punitive damages. The parties might have expected Ohio law to apply because the
centers of their negotiation relationship, to the extent it was anywhere (negotiations were online), were
Oklahoma/Ohio. Oklahoma was just where Montoya happened to be at the time, while Ohio was where
Buttercup lived. Per above, some evidence might be in Ohio (especially the computer), so the needs of
the interstate judicial system would be served by Ohio law, and Ohio has some interest in the case
(although applying its law disadvantages Buttercup). These factors might be enough to overcome the R2
presumption that the law of Vermont (injury location) law would apply.

A disadvantage to Montoya is that the N.D. Ohio might apply Ohio's 10% deposit requirement to the
federal diversity declaratory judgment statute, raising a VCOL issue. A Montoya inability/refusal to
make the deposit would kill the lawsuit, while 28 USC 2201 has no such requirement, so this is outcome
determinative under York, use state law. Harlan might analogize the 10% deposit law to the bond
requirement in Cohen (discussed in his Hanna concurrence), particularly given the Ohio legislature's
findings regarding frivolous lawsuits and risky pre-litigation (primary) conduct, and thus apply state law.
Scalia would say that 2201 is valid, citing Congress' constitutional power to provide remedies available in
the lower federal courts it creates (Art. III), and applicable because it speaks to what federal district
courts may do vis-à-vis declaratory judgments; Scalia would not require the 10%. Ginsburg would agree
with Scalia on validity; on applicability, she might say that the Ohio law speaks to an issue (a deposit
necessity) about which 2201 is silent, allowing the apparently important state law policy to flourish, but
might also be unwilling to interpret a federal statute (as opposed to a federal rule) that narrowly.

Question 4

Rule 13 would compel BW to counterclaim (raising both their tort and contract theories); if they don't,
they will lose both with the contemplated federal case went to judgment. Because that might raise the
prospect of substantially the same parties litigating substantially the same subject matter as in the Texas
litigation, BW will likely move for a Colorado River stay. Montoya can respond that the federal court
case would involve an additional party (Westley), and that there are no exceptional circumstances: no
hint of congressional intent akin to the McCarren amendment, and no risk of adjudication on the rights
of non-parties (akin to other water users in CR). Further, litigation in northern Ohio would be more
convenient for BW, and no federal law is involved. To support their stay motion, BW can point out that
the Texas case has gotten farther, and that a Texas court has seized the most likely asset to use in
satisfaction of any judgment against Montoya (his bank account).

A jury trial would be available because the BW counterclaim would seek damages in a traditional BoWC
law claim (Beacon). (If necessary: issues, such whether hotel or computer wiring caused fire, are
obviously factual on law/fact divide.)

Question 5
The Texas state court will apply its own 3-year limitations period because limitations are procedural for
HCOL purposes; it will dismiss because the lawsuit was filed 38 (>36) months after Buttercup's injury.
Under Texas law, limitations dismissals are on the merits for CP. Because any subsequent lawsuit by
Buttercup would be by the same party (her) over the same transaction (the computer fire), dismissed on
the merits (see above) in a final judgment, Texas would apply CP to dismiss a subsequent lawsuit under
tort or contract or both theories. Under section 1738, that means any U.S. court (state or federal) must
similarly preclude any suit such suit by Buttercup.
Issue preclusion would not run in a lawsuit in another state because the only issue actually litigated was
compliance with Texas limitations law, which is different from any other state's limitations law.
Neither claim nor issue preclusion would apply to any suit by Westley. He was not a party to Texas
litigation, he is not in privity with B because he exercised no control there nor is he B's successor in
interest.

Question 6
SCOTUS treats a class as a new entity in diversity-only class actions, deciding diversity only by reference
to the named plaintiff (Supreme Tribe). SCOTUS treats a class as massive joinder in Exxon's
supplemental jurisdiction analysis (it considered a simple joinder case and class case simultaneously
under the same reasoning). We do not know which SCOTUS will to IPJ; a new entity theory would mean
that BMS might not prevent exercise of jurisdiction over a defendant with respect to unnamed out-of-
state class members' claims, while a massive joinder theory would suggest that BMS might disallow IPJ
exercise. I prefer the new entity theory. Sometimes differences in scale become a difference in kind.
The massive joinder theory appeals to a formalistic ideal that unnamed class members could still litigate
their claims (Scalia in Shade Grove). Like many formalisms, this ideal has relevance only in a world ruled
by fantasy. Unnamed class members have little control, and frequently little practical interest in, their
claims. They trust plaintiffs' attorneys with those claims. So let's treat the class's claims, which must
meet newly-stringent commonality (Walmart) requirements, thus binding them together, as a single
new entity, akin to a trust. Doing so minimizes litigation shenanigans, such as multi-national
corporations pretending as though they care about venue, or about IPJ and separate state sovereignty
when they are in fact forum shopping (supposedly a type of evildoing in which only plaintiffs' attorneys
engage).

    10. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: "SMJ" for subject matter

    11. All of the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the law

    12. Citing authority is optional. You can get full credit without citation if you explain and

         words. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, International Shoe," instead of,
         "Under the test the Court articulated in International Shoe and subsequent cases, a court
         may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a

    13. The word limit for this exam 2200 words. If you exceed this word limit, only the first 2200 words

Consider the following hypothetical. In January of 2015, after completing the process required
under the Rules Enabling Act, the Supreme Court voted to adopt the following rule of civil
procedure.

         Fed. R. Civ. P. 87: Default Limitations Period
         Unless Congress has otherwise specified (by, for example, creating an alternative
         limitations period, or specifying that a limitations period from another source (such as
         another statute) should be used), in any lawsuit filed in a federal district court after the
         effective date of this rule, the period of limitations shall be three years.

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 2 of 9
                                 Law School of Harvard University / 2022-2023

Fed. R. Civ. P. 87 became law in mid-2015.
In Massachusetts, the limitations period for any lawsuit arising out of the death or injury to a pet
is two years. The statute providing for this time limit occurs in the portion of the Massachusetts
General Laws governing pet ownership, as part of the same statute that defined the
Massachusetts causes of action for death or injury to pets. No federal statute discusses the
appropriate statute of limitations for death or injury to pets specifically.
In December of 2019, Professor Greiner drove down Massachusetts Avenue in Cambridge and
ran over a dog owned by a student domiciled in New Hampshire. The dog was a rare breed; it
regularly won dog shows and was valued at $100,000.00. In August of 2022, the student sued
Professor Greiner in the United States District Court for the District of Massachusetts, asserting
negligence as the cause of action and requesting $100,000.00 in damages. Professor Greiner
moved to dismiss, citing the student's failure to file within the two-year Massachusetts
limitations period.

Question 1(a) (5 points): How would the court rule under each of the four Erie tests we
discussed in class?

Question 1(b) (2 points): How would the court rule if it were to apply the reasoning of
Justice Stevens in Shady Grove?

Question 1(c) (5 points): In your own view, how SHOULD the court rule? Include in your
response a discussion of at least three of the justifications for the Erie doctrine we discussed
in class.

Question 2 (6 points): In your view, are class actions useful to promote access to justice?
Discuss briefly. Include in your discussion a sentence or two about the relevance to this
topic, if any, of the Supreme Court's reluctance to require appointment of counsel in civil
cases (as embodied by Tuner and Lassiter). Also, include in your discussion at least one
situation in which a class action might promote access to justice goals, and another in
which a class action might be less useful to promoting such goals.

The following facts will be used for Questions 3-8 of this exam.

All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes providing that their state courts may
exercise jurisdiction to the extent permissible under the U.S. Constitution.
All states relevant to this exam have a tort cause of action for "conversion," also known as theft.
A defendant commits the tort of conversion if they exercise wrongful dominion or control of the
plaintiff's property, assets, or money to the detriment of the rights of the actual owner.
"Property" in this context encompasses digital assets. The last event necessary for the
completion of this tort is the removal of the item converted/stolen from the control of the
plaintiff, so the tort occurs where the item was located at the time of the theft.

All states relevant to this exam have a tort cause of action for negligence. The elements of this

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 3 of 9
                                 Law School of Harvard University / 2022-2023

cause of action are that the defendant owes a duty of care to the plaintiff, that the defendant
breached this duty by failing to act with ordinary care under the circumstances, and that the
breach caused quantifiable harm to the plaintiff. In all states relevant to this exam, a plaintiff can
sue a defendant for negligence if the defendant failed to exercise ordinary care in guarding an
item (including a digital item) that the plaintiff entrusted to the defendant's care, where such
failure caused the loss (due to theft or otherwise) of that item. In such cases, the last event
necessary for the completion of the tort is the theft/conversion of the item from the
possession/control from the defendant such that the plaintiff no longer has access to the item. In
other words, in this context, the tort occurs where the item was located at the time of the theft.
In all states relevant to this exam, an affirmative defense exists to a negligence claim, but the
nature of the affirmative defense differs from state to state. In Oklahoma and South Dakota, the
defense is called "contributory negligence," and it specifies that if the defendant persuades the
trier of fact that the plaintiff's own failure to exercise due care contributed in any way to the
plaintiff's loss, the court may not award the plaintiff anything, regardless of whether the
defendant was negligent. In Massachusetts, South Carolina, Delaware, and Texas, the defense is
called "comparative negligence," and it specifies that if a defendant persuades a trier of fact that
the plaintiff's own failure to exercise due care contributed to their loss, the trier of fact reduces
the amount that the plaintiff recovers by the fraction of the loss due to the plaintiff's own failure,
and the court (if it finds the defendant negligent) orders the defendant to pay the remaining
fraction.

Texas, Oklahoma, South Dakota, and South Carolina follow the Second Restatement for
horizontal choice of law. Delaware and Massachusetts follow the First Restatement for
horizontal choice of law.

Texas has four federal district courts. San Antonio is in the Western District of Texas. Dallas is
in the Northern District of Texas.

Oklahoma has three federal district courts. Oklahoma City is in the Western District of
Oklahoma.

California has four federal district courts. San Diego is in the Southern District of California.

South Dakota, South Carolina, and Massachusetts each have only one federal district court.
Delaware and South Carolina follow federal common law in all aspects of preclusion (claim and
issue). In Oklahoma, Texas, South Dakota, and Massachusetts, if two or more separate and
independent bases support a ruling key to a judgment, they are all considered to have been
decided against the losing party for issue preclusion purposes (meaning that these jurisdictions
do not follow the Supreme Court's decision in Russell v. Place).

Sylvania, Inc. was incorporated in Delaware with its principal place of business in Oklahoma
City, OK. Its programmers all work in a massive facility in Oklahoma City, OK. The company
specialized in selling cheap and un-hackable data storage. Sylvania was the first company to use
quantum computing to encrypt its information on its servers, so anyone hacking into its systems
except by way of a user account would obtain only gobbledygook. Users of Sylvania's data

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 4 of 9
                                 Law School of Harvard University / 2022-2023

storage could access their data from anywhere in the world. They did so through a username and
a password. Unlike most data storage companies, Sylvania did not require users to choose
passwords of any particular length, nor did passwords have to include a particular mix of
characters (e.g, numbers, letters, and special characters). Also unlike most data storage
companies, Sylvania allowed, but did not require, users to opt to enable two-factor authentication
("2FA") to log into their data storage accounts. In 2FA, a user must provide a username and a
password to initiate the login process; the system then sends a code to the user's cell phone,
which the user enters into the system to complete the login process. Thus, with 2FA, a hacker
attempting to gain access to a user's account would require both knowledge of the user's
username and password as well as possession of the user's cell phone (or some other method of
acquiring and entering the system-generated code).

Sylvania's storage methods were as follows. When a user uploaded data to one of its accounts,
Sylvania used patented technology to split the information into three pieces, none of which could
be used without the other. It stored the three pieces separately on its servers located in Dallas,
TX (in the Northern District of Texas); in Boston, MA (in the District of Massachusetts); and in
Columbia, SC (in the District of South Carolina).

Sylvania did no advertising of any kind. It depended entirely on word of mouth and
recommendations from its users. Users created accounts on its website,
sylvanianotfreedonia.com. Users paid in advance via credit card or cryptocurrency through an
interface on sylvanianotfreedonia.com. Sylvania was especially popular among artists who
created NFTs and other forms of digital crafts, and its customer base spanned the globe with no
particular geographic concentration, but was overwhelmingly European and Asian. It had few if
any US-based customers at the time period relevant to this exam.

Groucho, who grew up and lived all his life in San Antonio, was an art collector. Although he
dabbled in artworks of all kinds, his specialty was depictions of waterfowl. He owned statues,
photographs, paintings, and lithographs of egrets, herons, geese, and swans. In late 2019, while
visiting a friend in Pierre, South Dakota, Groucho experienced a virtual reality work of art
entitled "Duck in Soup." When run on an advanced virtual reality headset, "Duck in Soup"
depicted in an astonishingly realistic way the point of view of a duck while the animal was
swimming in a large pot of water filled with vegetables. The temperature of the water slowly
increased during the 90-minute simulation until it reached boiling, at which point the experience
ended. The author of the work, an artistic coder named Harposy, was one of the world's only
users of a programming language called "Malbolge." Programming in Malbolge is so difficult
that anything written in it is difficult to hack. In addition, the code for "Duck in Soup" was
programmed to destroy itself when copied, leaving only the copy in existence. Thus, absent an
advanced hacking program, which had to be written in Malbolge, only one copy of "Duck in
Soup" could exist at a time.

Entranced, Groucho bought "Duck in Soup" from Harposy for $300,000.00. Harposy provided
the computer code to Groucho on an external hard drive. After making the purchase, Groucho
returned to his hotel in Pierre. Groucho had heard of Sylvania through a friend while traveling in
Europe. Using his laptop computer from his hotel room, he opened a Sylvania account on

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 5 of 9
                                 Law School of Harvard University / 2022-2023

sylvanianotfreedonia.com and uploaded the code for "Duck in Soup" onto his Sylvania account
(at that moment, the copy of the "Duck in Soup" on the hard drive self-destructed). He then
returned home to San Antonio. After purchasing his first-ever advanced virtual reality headset,
he experienced "Duck in Soup" several times, and continued to be more enthusiastic.
In early 2020, Groucho visited a friend named Chico at the latter's house in Dallas, TX. Also
visiting Chico was a recent acquaintance named Zeppo, a Delaware domiciliary. Zeppo was on a
driving trip around the country, and in particular he was headed to San Diego, CA (in the
Southern District of California) by car, where he planned on spending a couple of weeks with a
friend named Firefly who lived on the coastline there. Zeppo had stopped at Chico's house to
spend the night. All Groucho could talk about that evening was his recent acquisition of "Duck
in Soup." To mollify his friend, Chico got out his VR headset. Using Chico's computer,
Groucho logged into is Sylvania account and accessed "Duck in Soup." Chico did not prefer the
production, but after finishing his first experience, Zeppo asked for a second and a third viewing,
and Groucho obliged. The next morning, Zeppo continued on his way to San Diego, and
Groucho returned home to San Antonio.

Two weeks later, Groucho logged into his Sylvania account, ready to enjoy "Duck in Soup"
again. He found, however, that the work of art no longer resided in his account. He went
immediately to sylvanianotfreedonia.com and, using the online chat function, demanded to know
what had happened. The Sylvania technician informed Groucho that four days earlier, his
account had been accessed by a computer in the coastal area of San Diego (Sylvania could not
further localize the account) via Groucho's username and password, and that the data for "Duck
in Soup" had been transferred out of Sylvania's systems onto a hard drive in that location. The
Sylvania technician regretted any inconvenience.

You were a litigator practicing in San Antonio. In the early spring of 2020, an enraged Groucho
walked into your office. He wanted to sue both Sylvania and Zeppo. He was convinced that
Zeppo stole "Duck in Soup" and that Sylvania's incompetent security protocols, particularly its
allowing users to use short passwords and its not requiring users to enable 2FA, enabled the
theft. He asked you to advise him on the following scenario.

Question 3 (8 points): Groucho asked you to suppose that he sued Zeppo for theft and
Sylvania for negligence in a Texas state court located in San Antonio, serving process
promptly and legally. Against Zeppo, he demanded $250,000. Against Sylvania, he
demanded $50,000. You inform Groucho that if he did so, the defendants were likely to
work together to remove promptly to the United States District Court for the Western
District of Texas, then move to dismiss or, in the alternative, to compel a transfer to
another federal district court. Groucho asked you two questions. First, if he were to file a
prompt motion to remand back to state court, how would the federal court rule? Second,
assuming that the court denied the motion to remand, on what grounds might the
defendants (either together or separately) move to dismiss or to transfer, and how would
the federal court rule? (Note: you need not discuss in your response whether the federal
court may transfer even if it did not have to, nor to which district the court would transfer
if it chose to do so, nor what would happen if the court did transfer). Explain your answer.

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 6 of 9
                                 Law School of Harvard University / 2022-2023

Groucho decided that he wanted to consider a different option.

Question 4 (5 points): Groucho asked you to suppose instead that he sued Zeppo for theft,
demanding $200,000, and Sylvania for negligence, demanding $100,000, in the United
States District Court for the Western District of Texas. In what ways, if any, would your
answer to the previous question change? Explain your answer.

Groucho decided that he preferred the second option. So he sued Zeppo for theft, demanding
$200,000, and Sylvania for negligence, demanding $100,000, in the United States District Court
for the Western District of Texas.

Question 5 (5 points): Supposed one or both of the defendants moved to transfer the case,
and despite Groucho's objection, Judge Teasdale of the Western District informed the
parties that she would in fact transfer (meaning she would grant the motion). Groucho
asked you to tell him: to which federal districts could Judge Teasdale transfer the case,
and of those, to which federal district would Judge Teasdale most likely transfer? Explain
your answer.

Judge Teasdale entered an order transferring the case without further explanation to the United
States District Court for the District of Delaware. Groucho immediately informed you that he
hated Delaware. He hated everyone in the whole state. He told you that he was once invited to
live in Delaware, but he refused, saying that he'd "refuse to live in a state that would have me as
a citizen."

Question 6 (5 points): Groucho asked you if he could immediately appeal Judge Teasdale's
transfer order to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit, and if he could,
what standard of review the court would most likely use in the appeal. What do you say?
Explain your answer.

When the case file arrived in the Delaware District Court, it was assigned to Judge Vera Marcal.
Sylvania answered the complaint, asserting the defense of contributory negligence or, in the
alternative, comparative fault. Zeppo also answered, denying that he stole "Duck in Soup."
Both defendants demanded jury trials.

The parties engaged in discovery. Sylvania and Zeppo deposed Groucho and Chico. Groucho
and Sylvania deposed Zeppo. Groucho propounded interrogatories on Sylvania. At the end of
discovery, Sylvania (but not Zeppo) moved for summary judgment, requesting that the court rule
that Groucho's own negligence was a causal factor in the theft/loss of "Duck in Soup," and that,
therefore, Groucho was barred from recovering anything from Sylvania. For the purposes of
litigating the motion, the parties stipulated that the court would assume that the facts described

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 7 of 9
                                 Law School of Harvard University / 2022-2023

above (basically, all of the facts described after the "Fact Pattern" heading, above) were true and
need not be supported/proven by evidence.

In its Memorandum in Support of Motion for Summary Judgment, Sylvania argued that there
were two separate, and independent, grounds for the motion. First, Sylvia argued, Groucho had
been negligent in choosing a too-easy-to-steal username and password for his Sylvania account,
and that he had been negligent in displaying it to those who might steal it, including Zeppo. That
is the first separate and independent ground for Sylvania's motion. Sylvania's second separate
and independent basis for its motion was that Groucho had been negligent in failing to enable
2FA when he set up his Sylvania account. The 2FA is the second separate and independent
ground for the motion. Sylvania attached the following to its Memorandum in Support of
Motion for Summary Judgment:

    · An affidavit from one if its engineers stating that at the time of the theft/loss of "Duck in Soup,"
              o Groucho's username for his Sylvania account was "Groucho" and his password was
                   "Groucho", and
              o Grouch had not enabled 2FA.

    · A transcript of part of Chico's deposition, at which Chico testified that at his house, on the
         evening in which Groucho had encouraged Zeppo and Chico to view "Duck in Soup," when
         Grouch was logging into his Sylvania account, he (Groucho) had pulled a full-size piece of paper
         out of his pocket. The piece of paper said, "username = Groucho, password = Groucho" in large
         type. He held the paper up in view of everyone in the room for several seconds as he logged in.

Groucho attached to his Memorandum in Opposition to Sylvania's Motion for Summary
Judgment a transcript of part of his own deposition, in which he testified that he had no memory
of his username or password at the time of the theft of "Duck in Soup," had no memory of how
he kept track of those pieces of information at that time, and had no memory of whether he had
enabled 2FA at that time. He testified instead that he "tried to forget as much as he could" about
that whole visit to Chico's house.

There was no other stipulated fact or evidence in consideration for Sylvania's motion.

Question 7 (12 points): Groucho asked you how Judge Marcal would rule on Sylvania's
motion requesting that the court rule that Groucho's own negligence was a causal factor in
the theft/loss of "Duck in Soup," and that, therefore, Groucho was barred from recovering
anything from Sylvania.. How do you respond? Explain your answer.

Rightly or wrongly, Judge Marcal granted Sylvania's motion for summary judgment, ruling that
Groucho's own negligence was a causal factor in the theft/loss of "Duck in Soup," and therefore
that Groucho would recover nothing from Sylvania. She then commented orally on the record,
"I know that Fed. R. Civ. P. 56(a) says that I `should' state on the record the reasons for granting
this motion, but I'm too busy right now. In addition, I find that there is no just reason to delay
this decision, so I will direct the clerk to enter final judgment in favor of Sylvania." The next
day, the clerk obliged, entering an order to enter final judgment in favor of Sylvania. At

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 8 of 9
                                 Law School of Harvard University / 2022-2023
Groucho's request, you filed a timely notice of appeal.
While the appeal is pending, Groucho asked you to call another lawyer named Dumont, who was
representing him in a different piece of litigation. You made the call. Dumont explained that
Groucho had suffered another theft, this one of a different item of digital art from his Sylvania
account. The second theft was of a work of art called "To War!", and was by an artist other than
Harposy. This separate theft took place about three months after the theft of "Duck in Soup."
She (Dumont) was representing Groucho in another suit against Sylvania, also for negligence,
and against one Trentino, for theft. Trentino was a former friend of Grouco's who grew up in
Florida and still lived there, and Groucho believed that Trentino had accessed his Sylvania
account without permission to steal "To War!" after Trentino had experienced this work in
Groucho's San Antonio home. Dumont was Groucho's lawyer in a suit in state court in
Oklahoma against both Trentino and Sylvania. This lawsuit also alleged that Sylvania's security
measures were insufficient. The evidence in the Oklahoma litigation showed that by the time of
the theft of "To War!", Groucho had left his username as "Groucho" but had changed his
password to "Groucho!@#$%^&07311980" and that Groucho had never written the new
password down. That said, Groucho had not enabled 2FA.
Question 8 (12 points): Dumont asked you two questions. First, was Judge Marcal's use of
Rule 54(b) to issue final judgment in favor of Sylvania in the Delaware litigation correct?
Second, would Sylvania be able to use in some way, in the Okhlahoma state court litigation,
Judge Marcal's summary judgment ruling and subsequent Rule 54(b) entry of judgment in
favor of Sylvania?

                                                END OF EXAM

                          © 2022-2023 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
                                                    Page 9 of 9

## 2022-final · Instructor model answer

Question 1

Because the lawsuit would end under state law (filed in year 2.5, Massachusetts SoL is two years) but
continued under federal law (3-year SoL), the difference is outcome determinative, so use state law and
dismiss. Under Harlan in Hanna, limitations period differences drive the investment strategies of large
(especially insurance) companies, so use state law. Under Hanna majority, Scalia version, Rule 87 is valid
because it really regulates procedure (Sibbach) in that limitations can be reasonably characterized as
time limits only (as they are in HCOL). Rule 87 is applicable because by its plain language ("any lawsuit
filed in a federal district court") it provides an SoL, so use federal law, and deny the motion. Ginsburg
would find Rule 87 valid for the same reason, but might interpret Rule 87 as silent on whether a lawsuit
must be filed in a time period shorter than three years, so long as it could be not be filed after three
years. She might thus say that state law can apply, and dismiss.

Stevens would take seriously the validity inquiry and ask whether the seemingly procedural
Massachusetts limitations period was part of the state's scheme of rights, as its placement in the MGL in
the same section that defines the rights, and his use of York/SoL as an example of such laws in Shady
Grove, might suggest. If so, Stevens might hold that Rule 87 is invalid under the REA as applied here. He
might also hold, given his presumption that "there must be little doubt" that a state rule is bound up in a
state's substantive scheme, that SoLs are simply time limits, and thus do not implicated 2072(b).
Despite his preference for Ginsburgian sensitivity to state interests in interpretation, he is less willing to
twist plain language and would likely agree with Scalia that the Rule is applicable.

Erie is best understood as a rule of prudence informed by federalism, separation of powers and
questions about the nature of law. Separation of powers, together with congressional power to create
the lower federal courts (augmented as necessary by the necessary and proper clause), provides
Congress latitude to prescribe mechanisms for the creation of doctrines rationally characterizable as
procedural for use therein. Congress did so in the REA, followed to create Rule 87, a limitations period,
something that in many contexts (e.g., HCOL) is procedural. The goal of horizontal uniformity across the
lower federal courts (e.g., national market for lawyers) suggest, prudentially, honoring the congressional
choice. The difference in SoLs might induce forum shopping, but likely not much more so than, say,
class action availability (Shady Grove), or SoP methods (Hanna). As Harlan points out, Erie was about
more than forum shopping and inequitable administration of the laws; Congress has a say in how
federalism operates. This is not a situation in which the federal courts have purported to "find"
something called the general law; the court system acknowledged that it made the law, doing so
through a congressionally created process open to public comment.

Question 2

Class actions can promote access to justice ("A2J") by assuring that members of the class can borrow
and pool resources to achieve at least some legal representation (Rule 23(g)), although not an attorney
who will become familiar with the specific aspects of each member's facts. Thus, there will be counsel
even though Lassiter/Turner would not so provide. Class actions reduce class members' effort costs
(Shutts, "sitting back"). Class actions are useful in promoting A2J when the unnamed plaintiffs have low-
value claims (per our Fells Wargo class hypo), allowing litigation of those claims when it would otherwise
make no economic sense to do so. Class actions are ordinarily of little value for parties who are in a
defensive posture, or for parties whose claims are fact-specific (such as tenants in summary eviction
cases) and thus cannot meet Walmart's stringent commonality requirements. Put another way, class
actions provide at best mass, not individualized, justice. The "loss" of individualized justice, a fictional
concept in modern society, is not much of a loss; the Tooth Fair is not real. All that said, class actions
can promote A2J only if courts are willing to certify them and push their administration; Shutts and
Walmart give little reason for hope here.

Question 3

There is no federal issue at all, so no form of 1331 SMJ. 1332 provides SMJ on Groucho's (Texas) Zeppo
(Delaware, $250,000 AIC) claim. The Sylvania (Delaware, Oklahoma, $50,000 AIC) claim features
diversity, but it is AIC deficient. 1367(b) prohibits SSMJ for this claim by a plaintiff (Groucho) against a
person made a party (Sylvania) under Rule 20 (OK because the two claims concern the "Duck" theft and
have a common issue of the work of art's value) (Situation 3). Exxon dictum. Only the Sylvania claim
must be remanded.

Assuming denial of the remand motion, the defendants might move to dismiss for lack of IPJ. Neither is
from TX, and neither does > 50% of its "business" in TX. Given 4(k)(1)(A) and the Texas long-arm to
constitutional limits, we look for minimal contacts. Zeppo traveled to Dallas where he first encountered
"Duck," a key event. For Sylvania, it does not solicit business in TX, and it has few TX customers other
than Groucho. But it used a TX-based server to store its part of its customers' data, including "Duck,"
and it accepted TX-based customers like Groucho (McGee), which is likely sufficient. True, Groucho
opened the Sylvania account elsewhere and then returned to his TX home, Hanson, but the server is an
additional, sizeable contact. Regarding fair & reasonable (for both), TX has an interest in regulating
allegedly wrongful conduct connected to its borders; TX is convenient for Groucho; TX is not
inconvenient for Sylvania (server) or Zeppo (got there once); evidence (Chico's testimony) is in TX; TX
law could apply in this R2 state (Klaxon) as overwhelmingly the center of gravity of the parties'
relationship (despite the R1 presumption in favor of OK law, see below).

Question 4

1332 now provides SMJ for both claims because the Sylvania claim now has sufficient AIC. See above re
IPJ. Venue is improper. 1391(b)(1) is unavailable because Zeppo is domiciled in DE, not W.D. Tex
(1391(c)). 1391(b)(2) is unavailable because the Chico meeting occurred in the N.D. (not W.D.) Tex. No
event (aside from Groucho's pre-lawsuit-facts viewings) occurred in W.D. Tex. As discussed below,
"Duck" itself is not best located with Grouch in W.D.Tex. The case must be transferred or dismissed.

Question 5

D. Del. can exercise GIPJ over both defendants, as both are citizens/domiciliaries there. For the same
reason venue would be proper there under 1391(b)(1). Per Question 3, SIPJ is likely available in a TX
federal court, and 1391(b)(2) venue is proper in the N.D. Tex. because the Chico meeting is a key event.
Under Hoffman, no other district is available. IPJ over Sylvania is unavailable in a Cal. court and a S.D.
court, and over Zeppo in D. Massachusetts and W.D. Ok. and D.S.C., because there are no minimal
contacts relating those defendants to those districts, respectively.

Channeling the Gulf Oil factors, of the two, N.D. Tex. is more likely because a key event (the Chico-house
meeting) and thus key evidence (Chico's testimony, Chico's computer if required) is there, and a Texas
court (R2) might well apply Texas law, even though D. Del. has the advantage of eliminating any
questions about IPJ and venue. Texas may not be convenient for Zeppo, but Delaware is equally
inconvenient for Groucho.

Question 6

There is no appellate jurisdiction. The transfer is not a final order under 1291, there was no judgment
under 54(b), there were none of the 1292(b) findings/recitations, the transfer order is effectively
reviewable later so no collateral order (Lauro Lines), and the order does not involve an injunction. A
mandamus petition might get the circuit court's attention (this is the fact pattern of Hoffman), but there
is no urgency/exigency here. If the circuit court did review the propriety of the transfer, it would review
issues regarding interpretation of the venue statute de novo; it would review any findings of fact (e.g.,
where Zeppo is domiciled) for clear error; and in the unlikely event that it found venue in W.D. Tex.
proper, it would review the transfer order for abuse of discretion because that decision involves the
application of the law to facts.

Question 7

Sylvania bears the trial burden of persuasion on the affirmative defense of either contributory or
comparative negligence. It discharged its burden by producing evidence that, if uncontroverted, would
compel a rational trier of fact (despite all reasonable inferences in Groucho's favor) to conclude that it
met its persuasion burden that Groucho was negligent in failing to enable 2FA (engineer affidavit) and,
independently, his failure to keep his password secure (obvious password and waived it around) (Chico
depo) (Celotex, Brennan). Groucho did not meet his now-shifted production burden by testifying that
he could not remember 2FA or his treatment of his password. The absence of evidence is not
contradictory evidence. Judge Marcal was likely to grant Sylvania's motion, finding that Groucho was
negligent on either or both the 2FA or password theories.

Whether Groucho's negligence constitutes a complete defense depends on HCOL. Under Ferrens,
because venue in the W.D. Tex. was unquestionably improper, D. Del. should employ Delaware's R!, so
we look for the last event. Per the prompt, the negligence tort was complete at the location of "Duck"
when it was stolen. Where was that? The artwork is incorporeal, and it does not consist solely of its
code; the code is worthless without an interface to play it, and as this case demonstrates, code can exist
in an large number of places. To locate incorporeal things, we consider policy arguments.

    · Pierre: this is where the account-holder opened the Sylvania account (and here, the place
         where the account-holder relinquished total control of the item), but as this fact pattern
         demonstrates, this location could be gratuitous, depending on the vagaries of travel plans, and
         thus is both indefinite and accidental.

    · San Antonio: This is where the account holder is domiciled, which is definite and ascertainable,
         and might comply with the holder's expectations. But it might not comply with company
         (Sylvania) expectations, and it would mean that property provided to Sylvania is located all over
         the world, which SCOTUS might find objectionable.

    · Delaware or Oklahoma are ascertainable and definite, and they have connections to the events.
         Either is reasonable in a party-expectation sense because Sylvania is strongly connected to both,
         and a customer can be charged with knowledge of the location of companies with whom they
         choose to deal. Of the two, OK is more connected to events because decisions relevant to the
         security policy of digital items were likely made there, and thus key evidence would be there.
    · Dallas, Columbia, or Boston: the data constituting "Duck" were physically in all three, so part of
         what constituted "Duck" was there when stolen. But the fact that there are three locations,
         each with an equal claim to reasonableness, demonstrates that each is an unwise choice.

If the artwork was in MA, SC, DE, or TX, then the SJ ruling that Groucho was negligent would reduce but
not eliminate Groucho's recovery. If it was in OK or SD, recovery would be barred. OK, and thus the
complete bar, seems most likely.

Question 8

The Rule 54(b) certification was likely improper. Litigation against Zeppo will continue, and the facts of
the Zeppo lawsuit are intertwined with those of the Sylvania claim. Groucho's theory is that Zeppo was
able to steal "Duck" only because Sylvania was negligent. It would be more efficient for the appellate
court to treat Groucho's lawsuit as a single unit.

Because venue was unquestionably improper in W.D. Tex., the transfer presents no special preclusion
problems (i.e., no possible Happy Court rule application to preclusion). Under Semtex, the OK state
court must provide as least as much preclusion as a DE state court would. Because the OK litigation
concerned events that occurred well after the "Duck" facts (likely after the first lawsuit was filed),
concerning a different artist's work viewed in a different location and stolen by a different alleged
assailant, it relies on different evidence. In addition, each artwork (like each bond) should be considered
unique to preserve market structures. This a different "claim." There is no claim preclusion despite the
same plaintiff (Grouch) and a final judgment (the appeal does not matter) on the merits (summary
judgment).

There are common issues in the DE and OK litigation, namely, whether Groucho's failure to enable 2FA
was negligent (contributory or comparative). The password change and the cessation of the use of a
paper record meant that negligence on this basis was not present in the OK litigation. DE follows FCL for
preclusion. We have actually litigated (summary judgment grant) and full mutuality. But under Russell,
we do not have necessary to the decision because Judge Marcal did not specify which of the two
independent grounds (password carelessness, 2FA) supported her decision. There will be no issue
preclusion unless the OK state court, following Hart, chooses to apply more preclusion than the
rendering court would.

## 2023-final · Exam text
