## 2024-final · Exam text

# Civil Procedure 2
## D. James Greiner — Fall 2024

**Available for download:** Monday, December 16, 2024, starting at 8:15 am ET
**Must be electronically submitted by:** Monday, December 16, 2024, 8 hours from download or by 4:30 pm, whichever is earlier.

The exam mode is TAKEHOME.

This exam is 14 pages long. Please check to see that you have all 14 pages.

While taking this examination you may consult any online or written materials, although materials other than those provided in class are likely to be more of a hindrance than a help. You must comply with all Harvard University and Law School policies, including the prohibition on the use of AI, chatbots, and similar software. You may not discuss the examination with anyone, either while taking it or thereafter, until the Registrar has returned 1L Fall grades.

<!-- page: 1 -->

1. The word limit on this exam is 2300 words. If you exceed that limit, only the first 2300 words will be graded.
2. Exam4 will automatically print your Anonymous ID and word count on the exam.
3. If you feel that there is a mistake in the exam, such as that a question is unclear, or that it refers to something that it should not, or that there is an important typographical error, then do the following. First, in your answer, explain what you believe to be the mistake. Second, explain the implications of the mistake. Third, make an assumption that is reasonable given the fact pattern and applicable law to resolve the error. Fourth, proceed with the remainder of the exam.
4. Each question's weight is specified (out of 65 total points). Time and word-count may be issues for you during the exam. Answer questions efficiently, and feel free to take into account the number of points available for each question. Don't forget your test-taking skills.
5. Do not put your name, or otherwise identify yourself, on your response.
6. Deal with all issues fairly raised, even if you think another issue would be dispositive of a matter in the real world.

<!-- page: 2 -->

7. If you believe additional information (factual or legal) is needed to deal with an issue, say so and make explicit whatever assumptions you need to proceed, but make these assumptions reasonable and interesting. Similarly, if at any point in this question you find that you do not know what the law is on a particular subject, identify the issue as to which there is uncertainty, then discuss the various options as to what the law could or should be, noting the advantages or disadvantages of each option.
8. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: "SMJ" for subject matter jurisdiction; "IPJ" for in personam jurisdiction; "DJ" for diversity jurisdiction; "FQJ" for federal question jurisdiction; "CNOF" for common nucleus of operative facts; and "DPC" for due process clause.
9. All of the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the law described in this exam. Use the scenarios only for the purposes of this exam.
10. Citing authority is optional. You can get full credit without citation if you explain and apply the relevant principle. That said, citing authority could well save you time & words. For example, you might say, "Minimum contacts, *International Shoe*," instead of, "Under the test the Court articulated in *International Shoe* and subsequent cases, a court may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has 'minimal contacts' with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be fair and reasonable under the circumstances."
11. If an issue in an earlier question recurs in a later question, you need not repeat your answer in the later question. In your later question answer, you may simply note that the answer here is the same as in the earlier question (please provide the number of the earlier question).

Good luck!

---

<!-- page: 3 -->

*All of the facts and law in the following hypothetical are fictional.*

## The Law

**Forum non conveniens and forum selection**: A Colorado state statute prohibits the enforcement of forum selection clauses and bans the use of forum non conveniens. Arizona common law and federal common law both provide for enforcement of forum selection clauses, and Arizona law is the same as federal common law with respect to forum non conveniens.

**Respondeat superior**: All states relevant to this exam follow the doctrine of respondeat superior, meaning that an employer/company can be held liable for the acts or omissions of its employees performed during the discharge of their duties.

**HCOL Restatements**: Colorado follows the First Restatement for Horizontal Choice of Law, Arizona the Second Restatement.

**Long-arms**: All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes going to constitutional limits.

**Limitations**: In both Colorado and Arizona, the statute of limitations for personal injury lawsuits is 1 year from the events that gave rise to the lawsuit. In both states, the statute of limitations for breach of contract is six years from the events constituting the breach.

**Preclusion**: Under federal common law, statute of limitations dismissals are not on the merits for claim preclusion purposes. Under the law of both Colorado and Arizona, statutes of limitations dismissals are on the merits for claim preclusion purposes. Both Colorado and Arizona require strict mutuality for issue preclusion purposes. You already know federal issue preclusion law.

**Districts**: Colorado and Arizona each have only one federal district court.

**Congressional statute**: In early 2021, Congress passed the Intoxicated Pilots Act. Section T-800 of the act provided as follows:

> The Congress finds that intoxicated airplane pilots pose a danger to passengers, to people on the ground in airports and elsewhere, and to property. The Congress hereby directs the Secretary of Transportation and the Secretary of Commerce to promulgate rules to protect the public against threats that drunk pilots pose. Such rules shall include a prohibition on the possession and consumption of any amount of alcohol in the cockpit of an airplane capable of flight. The United States Department of Justice, but not any private individual, may sue to effectuate rules that either Secretary promulgates.

Neither Secretary has as of yet promulgated regulations pursuant to section T-800.

<!-- page: 4 -->

**Prejudgment seizure scheme**: A Colorado statute called the "Prejudgment Seizure Act ("PSA") of 2017," still in force, provides as follows:

> A plaintiff may initiate a civil action in this state by filing a motion with the court requesting the seizure of any thing of value located in this state at the time of the seizure and owned by the defendant in the lawsuit only if the purpose of the seizure is to assure payment of liability in that lawsuit. Such seizure shall be effectuated only by a sheriff of the court, and only upon an order by a judge of that court. To request a seizure, the plaintiff shall file all the following documents with the court:
>
> - An affidavit, sworn under penalty of perjury, from the plaintiff/petitioner detailing the facts, and identifying the cause of action, that plaintiff/petitioner believes will result in the imposition of liability on the defendant;
> - True and correct copies of any and all documents that the plaintiff/petitioner contends will support its contentions;
> - A description of the item to be seized, along with a statement of its anticipated value;
> - A list of the names and contact information, if known, of any witnesses to the events leading to the civil action, which list must identify the witnesses that the petitioner/plaintiff might call to testify in support of their contentions;
> - A statement from the petitioner/plaintiff summarizing the anticipated testimony that the witnesses identified as possibly supporting the petitioner/plaintiff;
> - A bond from a properly financed issuer for double the value of the property to be seized, guaranteeing the payment of damages to the defendant if the plaintiff/petitioner loses the litigation and is unable to pay; and
> - A statement of reasons as to why the seizure is required to protect the plaintiff/petitioner's interests, including a brief discussion of why the lawsuit cannot effectively proceed in personam. Note: The fact that the defendant is a corporation located outside the United States, holding no assets in the United States, shall be deemed a reason sufficient to justify a seizure under this statute (provided that all of the other requirements of this statute are met).[^1]
>
> A judge receiving a petition for a writ of seizure, upon being satisfied that the foregoing are met, that (based on the information thus far available) the plaintiff/petitioner is likely to succeed on the merits and likely to face substantially uncertainty or difficulty collecting on any judgment, shall issue a writ of seizure. The sheriff will execute the writ of seizure forthwith. Thereafter, the judge shall
>
> - Require that the petitioner/plaintiff immediately provide notice of the action, including a copy of all papers described above, to the defendant;
> - Within two weeks of the seizure, or as soon as possibly practicable thereafter, hold a hearing in which the defendant may contest the seizure with any

<!-- page: 5 -->

> evidence or legal arguments, and in which the court shall consider the plaintiff/petitioner's likelihood of success in the lawsuit, the harm suffered to both parties if the seizure does or does not occur, and the public interest;
> - Allow the defendant to post a counter-bond from a properly financed issuer for an amount up to the damages the plaintiff/petitioner alleged to have been owed, and upon the posting of such a counterbond, return the seized item to the defendant immediately; and
> - If the order of seizure is not vacated at the above-described hearing, expedite adjudication of the merits of the plaintiff/petitioner's claim.

**Negligence cause of action**: All states relevant to this exam have a cause of action called negligence. The elements of the negligence cause of action are

- the existence of a duty from the defendant to the plaintiff (all persons have a duty to exercise due care in their interactions with other persons),
- the breach of that duty through the defendant's failure to exercise ordinary care under the circumstances,
- the breach caused the plaintiff an injury, and
- the injury to the plaintiff resulted in quantifiable damages.

A plaintiff may prove a negligence cause of action in two ways, first by alleging and proving that the defendant's actions or inactions fell below the common understanding of ordinary care, and second that the defendant violated a statute or agency rule (federal or state) enacted to protect persons like the plaintiff (if you've taken torts, this latter theory is called "negligence 'per se.'")

**Breach of contract cause of action**: All states relevant to this exam have a cause of action called breach of contract. The elements of this cause of action are the existence of a valid contract, the defendant's breach of that contract, and damages to the plaintiff caused by the breach.

---

## The Characters

**Connor**: Sarah Connor was born and raised in Phoenix, AZ, where her parents still lived. She hoped one day to be a pilot. When she graduated from high school, she began college at the University of Colorado in Boulder, CO, but kept her Arizona drivers' license. She was 21 years old at the time of the events described below, and in her junior year of college. After college, she hoped to attend a pilot's school somewhere, or perhaps join the Air Force to become a pilot.

**Reese**: Kyle Reese was a pilot. He grew up in North Dakota, joined the Air Force in his early 20s, and flew military transport planes until his retirement in 2019. Reese then moved to Tucson, Arizona, bought a small house there, and planned to live there for the rest of his life. He looked for a way to supplement his military pension, heard about an opening for a pilot at Skynet, Inc., applied, and got the job.

**Skynet**: Skynet, Inc., was a business incorporated in Mexico with its principal place of business in Nogales, Mexico, right across the U.S.-Mexico border from Tucson. Skynet offered scenic

<!-- page: 6 -->

low-altitude flying tours of beautiful natural sites exclusively in Mexico, with one exception, namely, the Grand Canyon Tour, described below. Skynet operated a fleet of six airplanes, all based in Nogales: one that sat two passengers, four that sat eight passengers, and one that sat twelve. Skynet did no advertising and maintained only a single-page non-interactive website describing its business as recreational/touring flights (with no information about where it flew) and inviting interested parties to call its 24-hour information and booking telephone line. It neither held assets nor maintained offices in the United States.

---

## The Events

**The Grand Canyon Tour**: In April of 2022, dialing from her college apartment in Boulder, CO, Sarah Connor called Skynet's booking number, hoping to find a trip she could take in June. She reserved a seat on Skynet's two-seater airplane for the "Grand Canyon Tour" in early June. The Grand Canyon tour's flight plan consisted of taking off from Nogales, Mexico; traveling northwest for a two-hour low-altitude flyover of several of the Grand Canyon's most beautiful locations;[^2] following the Colorado River north into Colorado for a 20-minute flight "so low that you'll feel like you're swimming" (in the words of the Skynet telephone salesperson); and then following a straight line back to Nogales. In part because of the amount of fuel required, the price of the "Grand Canyon Tour" was expensive, but Connor's relatives and friends chipped in to make the trip an early graduation present.

In late May, after taking her last exam, Connor took a bus from Boulder, CO to her childhood home in Phoenix. She spent a couple of weeks relaxing with her family. On the appointed morning, in early June of 2022, she drove her parents' car across the border to Nogales and checked in at Skynet's office. When she did so, she signed a standard contract. The contract had two clauses relevant to this exam.

> **Best Efforts at Safety:** Skynet, Inc. promises that its employees will obey all applicable laws and regulations, and will exercise their best efforts and due care, to provide a safe flight.

> **Forum Selection:** The parties agree that any and all lawsuits related in any way to this flight, whether tort or contract, shall be litigated in a court in Nogales, Mexico.

After signing the contract, Connor boarded the Skynet two-seater for her tour. The other passenger was a woman named Ginger Ventura, a student at the University of Denver, in Colorado. Connor and Ventura struck up a conversation, and before takeoff exchanged contact information so as to stay in touch after their adventure. Both passengers were in their seats when pilot Kyle Reese arrived. Connor noticed that he carried a 750 ml bottle of Woodford Reserve

<!-- page: 7 -->

Bourbon Whiskey in his left hand as he entered the cockpit. The bottle was full. Connor also noticed that Reese did not lock the door to the closed cockpit.

At first, the flight went off as planned. The ride was smooth, and both Connor and Ventura enjoyed themselves. Toward the end of the time over the Grand Canyon, while the plane was still in Arizona, the plane's path became erratic. The occupants of the plane disagree on some of what happened next, but they agree on the following facts. The plane's flight went from bad to worse. Eventually, the plane began to climb to higher altitudes, but as it did so, it underwent several sudden and extreme dips and rises. These dips and rises slammed Connor and Ventura multiple times into their seats, into the interior of the airplane, and into each other. At that point, the cockpit door opened violently. Connor saw the bottle of Woodford Whiskey roll across the plane's floor and stop at her feet.

The plane had by then crossed the border from Arizona into Colorado, continuing on its intended path over the Colorado River. But the flight continued to experience sudden changes in altitude and unexpected swerves, sending Connor and Ventura tumbling in their seats anew. Connor in particular was banged around even more. At that point, Reese turned the plane back toward Nogales and flew home. The flight back was a nightmare, with the plane's wings swinging back and forth. Luckily, although the plane was weaving as it approached the runway, the landing in Nogales was without incident. Ventura and Reese emerged from the flight with only a few bumps and bruises. Connor was less lucky. She fractured her shoulder and eye socket, requiring painful reconstruction of her face. Her medical bills were about $400,000.

**Connor's Threatened Lawsuit**: In August of 2022, Skynet received a letter from the law firm of Silberman and Traxler. The letter stated that Silberman and Traxler represented Sarah Connor in the matter of her Grand Canyon Tour flight. Connor intended to sue Skynet and perhaps Reese asserting common law negligence and breach of contract. With respect to negligence, Connor would prove breach of the duty of care by proving that Reese was drunk at key times during the flight in violation of section T-800 of the Intoxicated Pilots Act. With respect to breach of contract, Connor would assert that Reese's drunkenness breached the "Best Efforts at Safety" clause in the contract. Silberman and Traxler asked whether Skynet wanted to settle before litigation began.

**State Court Seizure of the Airplane**: After hearing nothing from Skynet for a month, Silberman and Traxler went into action, with the following results. Near the end of the month of October of 2022, Connor (through her law firm) filed a motion in a Colorado state court in Denver; the state courthouse was across the street from the primary courthouse for the U.S. District Court for the District of Colorado. The state court had geographic jurisdiction over all Colorado. The motion requested seizure of the Skynet two-passenger-seat airplane involved in Connor's ride. The motion included the following attachments:

- An affidavit from Connor describing the facts detailed above, along with the following:
  - When the plane's gyrations slammed open the cockpit door in the Tour ride that caused Connor's injuries, Connor noticed that

<!-- page: 8 -->

  - the Whiskey bottle that rolled across the floor was capped but was three-quarters empty; and
  - a strong smell of alcohol emanated from the cockpit.
  - When the plane first began to swerve and fly erratically, Connor looked for dust swirls, rainclouds, rain, blowing debris, grass movements, or any other sign of wind or a storm to explain the strange plane movement, and saw none.
  - Immediately after the flight, as he was deplaning, Reese's speech was slurred and his walking ungainly.
- A copy of the contract she signed with Skynet.
- A description of the Skynet two-seater airplane (Connor's experience as a pilot allowed her to provide detail), along with an estimate of its value in the amount of $800,000.
- Ventura's name and contact information, along with a statement saying that Ventura would testify that she also noticed the Whiskey bottle's being three-quarters empty, and that Reese's speech was slurred and walk erratic after the trip.
- A litigation bond from a well-financed company in the amount of $1.6 million.
- An affirmation that Skynet was a Mexican corporation with its principal place of business in Nogales, and that it had no assets in the United States (Connor stated that these facts were her reasons for why the lawsuit could not effectively proceed in personam).

After reviewing the filings, Colorado state court judge Matt Buchanan issued a writ of seizure for the airplane. Shortly thereafter, Colorado state court sheriff Danny Dyson booked a ride on the Skynet Grand Canyon Tour in the two-seater airplane, purchasing both seats so as to have the plane to himself. On the appointed day, Dyson boarded the Skynet two-seater and waited until the plane had flown from the Grand Canyon over the border into Colorado. As the plane began to trace the path of the Colorado River, Dyson obtained the attention of the pilot (another of Skynet's employees, not Reese), showed the pilot his Sheriff's credentials and the writ of seizure, and ordered the pilot to land the plane in a nearby Colorado airport. The pilot complied.

Upon learning that the plane had been seized, Judge Buchanan ordered immediate service upon Skynet of the papers Connor had filed with her motion. Skynet agreed to accept service by mail and email. In a brief telephone conference among the judge and the parties, Judge Buchanan ordered that the hearing contemplated by the Prejudgment Seizure Act would take place in the second week of November of 2022 (Skynet agreed that the date was as soon as practicable under the circumstances).

**QUESTION 1 (10 points)**: What arguments could Skynet make at the upcoming state court hearing to persuade the court to vacate the seizure order and to return airplane to Skynet? Explain. (Note that Skynet does not wish to pursue a counterbond at this time, nor does it think to invoke the contract's forum selection clause, so do not discuss it in your answer to Question 1.

<!-- page: 9 -->

In the first week of November of 2022, before Judge Buchanan's hearing, Reese and Skynet in a single complaint jointly sued Connor in the United States District Court for the District of Colorado. The complaint alleged that Reese had not been drinking during the flight, and that powerful and continuing wind gusts had caused the flight problems. Skynet and Reese requested a declaratory judgment that because Reese was not drunk at the time of Connor's flight, there had been no violation of Section T-800 of the Intoxicated Pilots Act, and thus neither Reese nor Skynet could be held liable for negligence. Similarly, the complaint alleged, because Reese had not been drunk, Skynet could not be liable for breach of contract. Service of process was made properly and timely by leaving a copy of the summons and complaint with Connor's 21-year-old roommate at their shared apartment in Boulder.

**QUESTION 2 (13 points)**: Does Connor have any grounds to move to dismiss, or in the alternative to stay, this lawsuit? Explain. (Note: Connor does not think to invoke the forum selection clause, so do not discuss it in your answer to Question 2.)

---

In the second week of November of 2022, before the hearing before Judge Buchanan and before Connor had responded to the federal court lawsuit, the parties agreed to try settlement negotiations. Skynet arranged with a Colorado bank to guarantee payment to Connor should Connor succeed in a lawsuit against it (Skynet); how that guarantee worked is not relevant here. Connor withdrew her motion to seize Skynet's plane, and Judge Buchanan at the parties' joint request dismissed Connor's state court lawsuit "without prejudice to refiling in any court of competent jurisdiction." Similarly, the parties jointly requested dismissal of Skynet and Reese's federal court lawsuit, which the federal judge granted "without prejudice to refiling in any court of competent jurisdiction."

For the next three months, the parties engaged in settlement negotiations, but could not come to an agreement. At that point, the law firm of Silberman and Traxler went out of business. Connor struggled at first to find a new law firm to represent her. Eventually, she did so, a law firm called Tarissa and Miles. Through her new law firm, in late December of 2023, Connor sued Skynet only, not Reese, in the United States District Court for the District of Colorado. In this complaint, Connor no longer relied on the Intoxicated Pilot's Act, preferring instead to assert that Reese's alleged drunkenness was negligent because it was a violation of the everyday understanding of ordinary care. Specifically, Connor's complaint read as follows:

---

*In the United States District Court for the District of Colorado*

1. This Court has subject matter jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1332.
2. Venue is proper under 28 U.S.C. § 1391.
3. Plaintiff Sarah Connor is a citizen of Arizona.

<!-- page: 10 -->

4. Defendant Skynet is a Mexican Corporation with its principal place of business in Nogales, Mexico.
5. In early June of 2022, Connor was a passenger on a site-seeing plane ride that the defendant operated. The defendant owned the relevant aircraft, and one of its employees, Kyle Reese, was the pilot.
6. When Reese boarded the aircraft and entered the cockpit, he was holding a 750 ml bottle of Woodford Whiskey in his left hand.
7. Beginning while the plane was over the Grand Canyon in Arizona, and continuing while it was over the Colorado River in Colorado, the plane experienced extraordinary and severe changes in altitude, swerving, and erratic deviations from its flightpath.
8. During these severe changes in altitude and deviations from smooth flightpath, the cockpit door opened. The whiskey bottle rolled across the floor into the passenger compartment. The bottle, though stoppered, was three-quarters empty. In addition, a strong smell of alcohol emanated from the cockpit. Later, when Reese deplaned at the end of the flight, his speech was slurred, and he had difficulty walking normally.
9. Reese drank whiskey during the flight and was drunk during its operation.
10. The severe changes in altitude and deviations from a smooth flightpath caused Connor to slam into her seat, the interior of the aircraft, and a fellow passenger, resulting in a fractured eye socket and a broken shoulder.
11. These injuries resulted in medical expenses in the amount of $400,000 and extensive pain and suffering.
12. Reese's drinking whiskey during the flight constituted negligence, and that negligence caused Connor's injuries.

**Sole Cause of Action: Common Law Negligence**

13. As operator of the aircraft, Skynet owed Connor a duty of ordinary care. It breached this duty through the actions of its employee, pilot Reese, who flew the plane while drunk. Reese's and thus Skynet's breach resulted in plaintiff's injuries. Thus, Skynet is liable for common law negligence.
14. Wherefore, Connor requests that the Court award her $400,000 in medical expense; $350,000 to compensate her for pain and suffering; and such other relief as the court deems proper.

Jury trial demand: Plaintiff demands a jury for all issues so triable.

[signatures]

---

**QUESTION 3 (9 points)**: Could Skynet move to dismiss the lawsuit on any of the grounds listed in Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)? Explain. (Please do not discuss limitations or the forum selection clause in your answer to Question 3.)

<!-- page: 11 -->

Skynet chose not to move to dismiss immediately. Instead, in its answer, Skynet asserted that Connor's lawsuit was untimely and had to be dismissed on limitations grounds. In the alternative, Skynet asserted that the forum selection clause in the contract required that the litigation must be dismissed because litigation could occur only in Nogales, Mexico.

Before the litigation could proceed further, Colorado federal court judge sua sponte transferred the case, under the transfer statutes, to the United States District Court for the District of Arizona. You may assume for the purposes of this exam that the District of Arizona was a "happy court" (*i.e.*, that venue was proper and that D. Ariz. could exercise personal jurisdiction over Skynet).

Skynet then moved for an order dismissing the lawsuit due to the contract's forum selection clause, or in the alternative on forum non conveniens grounds due to the Connor-Skynet contract allegedly selecting Nogales, Mexico as the only permissible location for lawsuits. Note: assume that the only argument for FNC is the forum selection clause.[^3]

**QUESTION 4 (9 points)**: Should Arizona federal court grant this motion? Explain.

---

The Arizona federal court denied Skynet's forum selection/forum non conveniens motion.

**QUESTION 5 (2 points)**: Can Skynet immediately obtain appellate court review of this denial, before the case goes to final judgment? Briefly explain.

---

Skynet moved to strike Connor's jury trial demand.

**QUESTION 6 (2 points)**: Should the federal court grant Skynet's motion to strike, or should a trial, if one occurs, proceed with a jury? If a jury trial should proceed, identify one issue that the jury, not the judge, should decide.

---

The parties proceeded to rapid discovery. At the close of discovery, Skynet moved for summary judgment on the grounds that Connor's lawsuit was untimely under applicable limitations law. The Arizona district court denied the motion and set the case for trial. In June of 2024, the trial occurred, with the district court having empaneled a jury.

<!-- page: 12 -->

At the trial, the parties agreed that Connor and Ventura had taken the Skynet Grand Canyon tour in early June of 2022, that the plane had experienced severe changes in altitude and changes in its flightpath, that these gyrations had caused Connor's injuries, that the injuries resulted in $400,000 in medical bills, and that if Skynet were liable, $350,000 was a reasonable sum to compensate Connor for her pain and suffering. The case thus turned on the source of the plane's altitude and flightpath aberrations. On that point, the jury heard the following testimony.

- Connor testified that she saw Reese board the airplane with a 750 ml bottle of Woodford Whiskey in his left hand. During the plane's gyrations, Connor testified, the cockpit door opened, and the bottle came rolling out to bump up against her seat. Connor saw that the bottle, though stoppered, was three-quarters empty. Connor further testified that a strong smell of alcohol emanated from the cockpit until Reese slammed the door shut. Connor further testified that, using the pilot's training she had received before the flight, she looked for any sign of unusual air patterns or turbulence, such as sandstorms, blowing debris, rain, storm clouds, or unusually choppy water in the Colorado River, and saw none. She also testified that she had never heard of wind patterns widespread and severe enough to explain the plane's erratic flight path all the way back to Nogales (across the length of Arizona) without some visible indication of a storm. Finally, Connor testified that as he deplaned, Reese's speech was slurred and his walking strange and ungainly.

- Ventura testified that she, also, saw Reese board the airplane with a 750 ml bottle of Woodford Whiskey. She also saw the bottle roll out in the passenger compartment during the plane's gyrations, and saw that it was stoppered but three-quarters empty. She also testified that Reese's speech was slurred and his walking strange and ungainly as he deplaned.

- Reese testified that he had a bottle of whiskey with him at the time of the flight. He bought it as a gift for a friend of his, and was intending to drive to his friend's house after the Connor/Ventura flight to give it to them. He agreed that the bottle rolled into the passenger compartment of the plane during the flight, but testified that it was full at the time. After the flight, he could not find the bottle. He testified that he neither opened the bottle nor drank any of its contents during the flight. He stated that he had had an alcohol problem during his younger years, but had stopped drinking in 2017, had been sober ever since, and had not touched a drop of alcohol from 2017 to the present. He testified that he felt unusual air turbulence during the flight, "The worst I've ever experience in all my years of flying, and it just went on and on" all the way back to landing. Reese testified that he was proud of the piloting he had done on the flight.

- After Reese testified, Connor's lawyers called as a rebuttal witness a private detective from Mexico named Linda Hamilton. Hamilton testified that, at the request of Connor's lawyers, she had visited bars near Skynet's Nogales headquarters. She testified that six bartenders, working for four separate bars,

<!-- page: 13 -->

knew Kyle Reese by name and sight (from a photo Hamilton provided), and that he regularly frequented all four. Each of the bartenders stated that Reese had been a regular customer for the past five years and was a heavy drinker. His favorite drink, each of the bartenders said, was Woodford Whiskey.[^4]

Assume no violation of the rules of evidence. Connor moved for judgment as a matter of law at the close of the evidence; the court refused to grant the motion.

Suppose, ONLY FOR THE PURPOSES OF THIS QUESTION, that the jury returned a verdict in favor of Skynet.

**QUESTION 7 (5 points)**: What motions might Connor make in the trial court at this time? Should the court grant any of them? Explain.

---

Now suppose, FOR THE REMAINDER OF THIS EXAM, that the jury returned a verdict in favor of Connor. The district court entered judgment in accordance with the verdict. Skynet appealed, renewing before the appellate court its limitations and forum selection clause/forum non conveniens arguments. In early October of 2024, the Ninth Circuit reversed. It held that Connor's lawsuit was untimely under applicable limitations law. It ordered the case dismissed for failure to comply with the statute of limitations. In late October of 2024, in accordance with the Ninth Circuit's ruling, the Arizona district court entered a new final judgment in favor of Skynet on limitations grounds.

In early November of 2024, Connor filed suit against Skynet in a Colorado state court, asserting breach of contract. Connor's theory was that Reese was drunk on the flight, that his drunkenness breached the "Best Efforts at Safety" clause in the contract, and that the drunkenness and resulting airplane gyrations caused her injuries.

**QUESTION 8 (8 points)**: Could either party make use of preclusion in this lawsuit? Explain.

---

*This question does not make use of the fact pattern described above.*

**QUESTION 9 (7 points)**: Fed. R. Civ. P. 23(g) requires a federal district court certifying a class action to appoint a class lawyer. But subsection 23(g) was added only in 2003. Before 2003, there was no prohibition on a named plaintiff's litigating pro se on behalf of a class. In your view, should pro se parties be allowed to litigate pro se on behalf of a class? What kind of resources might help a pro se party do so, and would those resources be sufficient? Explain. Note One: I suggest you limit your discussion on this point to two paragraphs. Note Two: this is a

<!-- page: 14 -->

"should" question. I am interested in your opinion.

---

[^1]: A committee report accompanying the passage of the PSA explained that the Colorado legislature recognized the difficulty that plaintiffs/petitioners encountered in collecting on judgments that Colorado courts rendered against non-U.S. corporations with no assets in the United States, given that full faith and credit principles do not apply easily if at all across national boundaries.

[^2]: All portions of the Grand Canyon relevant to this exam are in Arizona.

[^3]: In other words, for purposes of this exam, consider the forum selection and forum non conveniens issues/doctrines as one issue/doctrine in your analysis. The only reason that Skynet is citing the forum non conveniens doctrine is to enforce the forum selection clause (which might be the correct way to do so).

[^4]: You may consider this testimony as proper. Do not worry about whether it is hearsay.

## 2024-final · Instructor model answer

Question 1
QIRJ: Under the narrow view of Shafer, this is traditional QIRJ, i.e., a seizure pursuant to an authorizing
statute (PSA) of an item in Colorado (Grace; if not in CO, what law would a murder on the plane violate?)
to assure payment of a hoped-for judgment. Under the broad version of Shafer, following Shoe, for
statutory authorization we have the PSA plus the Colorado constitutional-limits long-arm. With no
argument for GIPJ, we look for minimal contacts. Skynet does no advertising in CO and has no office or
assets there. But under plaintiff’s theory, Skynet’s injured the plaintiff by drunk flying this this particular
plane into CO following Skynet’s regular business plan to make money from flying over CO landscape. A
single incident easily establishes SIPJ because the liability-inducing conduct and the injury occurred in CO
during execution of Skynet’s business plan (McGee, Kane, Hess). Regarding F&R, a witness (Ventura) lives
in CO; CO is convenient for the plaintiff, who mostly lives there; CO law might apply (injury occurred in
either AZ or CO); and CO is not too inconvenient for Skynet, which flies there.
PDP (Matthews):
• Connor & Skynet interests: Skynet’s interest is pecuniary/business, nothing close to subsistence
(less than Matthews). Seizure is available only if the plaintiff states a reason why their interests
require it, as here, because a judgment against a non-U.S. company with no U.S. assets is hard to
collect on (Skynet could discontinue its only flight route in the U.S, admittedly at some business
cost). To minimize duration of an erroneous seizure, a quick post-seizure hearing on essentially
FRCP 65 factors is required, and unless the seizure is vacated, merits adjudication must be
expedited. Connor’s bond assures compensation for any wrongful seizure, reducing Skynet’s
interest. Although Connor has no property interest in the airplane, and there is little risk that
Skynet would waste its revenue-producing airplane, she needs her extensive medical bills paid
(less than Goldberg-type subsistence but somewhat important).
• Error risk: A judge is the decision maker; a bond is required, a counterbond allowed but not filed
(market signals); the plaintiff must (did here) file a sworn statement, a specific factual
recounting, a list of supporting witnesses with contact information, a summary of testimony, and
a justification of why the prejudgment seizure is needed. If seizure occurs, plaintiff must push
the lawsuit forward, and the court must expedite. That said, this is a live testimony, not a
documents, case (Doehr).
• The government interest is standard, low adjudication expense and an accurate result, plus CO’s
interest in helping CO plaintiffs collect on judgments against international defendants.
A counterfactual is requiring the hearing before the seizure. That would provide greater protection for
Skynet, who could provide its own evidence and argument in this requires-live-testimony whathappened dispute. But it would decrease protection of the plaintiff’s interest, because (as here) the
owner can easily remove its assets beyond U.S. courts’ reach (although there is as yet no affirmative
evidence Skynet will (Doehr)).

Question 2
Rule 20 authorizes the two-plaintiff suit; there was a single T&O (the flight, Heyward), and a common
issue was whether Reese was drunk.
There is likely no SMJ. Looking to an imaginary coercive Connor lawsuit, student Connor with an AZ
drivers’ license and AZ parents is an AZ domiciliary/citizen (AZ DL, parents, Mas), as is Reese (house), so
complete diversity is lacking (Strawbridge), even though the AIC ($400K > $75K) is sufficient. Exxon


contamination renders 1367 unavailable. There is no federal COA. If Smith/Grable were available for the
negligence, we’d have Rule 18 authorization, and 1367(a) would provide SSMJ for breach of contract,
there being only one accident (so same C or C), no 1367(b) problem (AUJ anchoring), and no 1367(c)
factors suggesting declination. But even if the federal issue (IPA violation, Reese’s drunkenness) is
embedded and drunkenness is contested and substantial, exercising S-G over this negligence per se case
would sweep numerous disputes into federal court (Merrill Dow), the issue is purely factual, and
Congress specified other remedies (agency regulation/suit) it preferred.
IPJ: If Daimler applies to humans (not sure), Connor has for three-plus years spent, and currently spends,
almost all her life in CO, providing “at home” GIPJ. If not, we have the CO constitutional-limits long-arm
and 4(k)(1)(A). For minimal contacts, CO student Connor while in CO reserved a flight to fly over CO
where she was injured. F&R factors are the same as in Question 1 except now-plaintiff Skynet’s
indifferent convenience is less of an issue, and CO is highly convenient for “at home” defendant Connor.
1391(b)(1) is unavailable because Connor is a resident of AZ (1391(c)(1), above). 1391(b)(2) is likely
proper because Connor’s initial phone reservation plus the plane gyrations and injury over CO constitute
a substantial part of the lawsuit events. 1391(b)(3) is unavailable because venue would be proper in D.
Az.
A Colorado River stay motion will likely be denied. State court litigation among substantially the same
parties on substantially the same issues began (only) a week earlier. Counterbalancing the unflagging
federal court obligation are that the state court has seized a key piece of property (although the parties
are not disputing the plane’s ownership), and that Judge Buchanan is already familiar with the issues and
has already made one consequential ruling. That said, there is no indication of congressional intent
regarding state court litigation, and the state and federal courts are close so relative convenience is not
an issue.

Question 3
SMJ is available via diversity, with Connor an AZ citizen (above), Skynet an “abroad” citizen, and AIC of
$750,000 > $75,000.
IPJ here is the same as discussed in Question 1 on the broad view of Shafer.
1391(b)(1): venue is proper because foreign corporation Skynet may be sued in any district, 1391(c)(3).
1391(b)(2) venue is likely proper per Q2, above. (b3) venue is unavailable because any venue is proper
elsewhere (anywhere).
Under Iqbal, we ignore Complaint Paragraphs 12 and 13 (perhaps 9) as conclusory recitations of the
elements of the cause of action. Looking at the remainder, duty is alleged in Paragraph 5 (interaction);
breach in the form of Reese’s drinking is plausible, not merely possible, from Paragraphs 6 and 8 (nearly
empty bottle, alcohol smell, slurred speech, difficulty walking); causation is plausible from Paragraphs 7
and 10 (drunk plane driving causing passengers’ slamming into everything nearby); and damages are
plausible from Paragraph 10. Skynet’s possible alternative explanation (wind) doesn’t come close to
rendering Connor’s version of events implausible.


Question 4
Neither CO (where Connor filed) nor AZ (where the case was transferred) is the FSC-specified forum, and
transfer to Nogales is impossible (Stewart, Atlantic Marine are inapplicable). So we default to standard
Happy Court rules. Q3 discusses IPJ and venue.
If D. Colo. was unhappy, then under Arizona HCOL, Arizona would apply its own law to this arguably
procedural (broad) → procedural issue and enforce the FSC. An FNC analysis with a presumption in favor
of the Mexico and a consideration of only the public interest factors (Atlantic Marine) shows nothing
close to a persuasive reason to ignore the FSC. If, as seems likely, D. Colo. was happy, a Colorado HCOL
analysis (Ferrens) results in CO procedural (see previous) law banning the FSC, resulting in an Erie
conflict.
If FSC enforceability is HCOL substantive (highly unlikely), the injury might have occurred in either AZ or
CO, probably both. No R2 Section 6 factors overcome the LLD presumption (parties’ relationship is only
mildly center in Mexico, all jurisdictions have strong interests in preventing drunk flying), so the court’s
choice on the injury would give us CO or AZ law re FSC enforceability.
If we end up with CO enforceability law, then under York, the difference in dismiss (FCL) versus don’t
dismiss (CO law) is outcome-determinative, use state law. That said, an end to this case would not result
in an end to litigation (which might continue in Nogales), so a contrary argument exists.
Under Harlan in Hannah, it’s likely that litigating only their home jurisdictions will make companies more
less careful, in which case we should apply state law. On the other hand, lawyers, not clients, usually
select the forum, so a contrary argument exists.
Under the Hannah majority, both Scalia and Ginsburg apply the twin aims to this contrast between FCL
and state law. Because dismissal/no dismissal is at stake, lawyers will forum shop to keep the litigation in
federal court, so both apply state law.

Question 5
No. The denial is not a final order. There is only one claim, 54(b), no 1292(b) finding(s), no Cohen
exception (Lauro Lines), and no exigency for a mandamus petition.

Question 6
No. The plaintiff requests damages. If it matters, negligence is a traditional common law CoA. A key
factual issue a jury should decide is whether Reese was drunk.

Question 7
Connor can renew her JAMOL motion, 50(b), but the Court will likely not grant it. The case turns on
whether the jury believed (a) Reese or (b) Connor, Ventura, and Hamilton. Even if Reese lied about his


prior drinking, the jury could believe his testimony that he did not drink during Connor’s flight. There is
no purely objective evidence to trigger Scott, it’s all testimony.
Connor can move for a new trial on the grounds that the verdict is against the great weight of the
evidence, 59. The Court has discretion to grant the motion if it believes Connor, Ventura, and Hamilton.
Hamilton’s testimony strongly suggests Reese was lying about his drinking habit. Ventura may like
Connor but has no other motive to lie. That said, no one else saw Reese drinking during the Connor
flight, and Reese testified under oath. The Court will likely grant a new trial but has discretion to deny.

Question 8
The first two without-prejudice dismissals are irrelevant.
Connor is the same party, there was only one flight (same T&O), and the D. Az. case reached final
judgment. With a federal diversity rendering court and a state subsequent court, we use FCL to borrow
either Colorado or Arizona law (they are the same, so transfer is irrelevant), Semtek, and conclude that
the SoL dismissal was on the merits. Marresse is a non-issue: Rule 18 would have authorized the claim
joinder in L1, SMJ (DJ) and IPJ (SIPJ) were available under Q3 reasoning. Skynet can invoke claim
preclusion.
Issue preclusion, Connor against Skynet, turns on whether any jury finding constitutes “necessary to
decision,” almost certainly not. The parties (Connor and Skynet, same procedural posture, strict
mutuality) actually litigated (jury verdict) the two factual issues (whether Reese was drunk, whether
drunkenness caused Connor’s injuries). Skynet had adequate incentive and opportunity to battle. The
jury knew nothing of the SoL issue and so likely adjudicated well; it is silly to relitigate. But final judgment
rested on SoL. The jury finding was not necessary to the prior court’s judgment/decision. Perhaps the
subsequent court here might preclude even though the rendering court might not, given this posture
(Hart). Skynet cannot issue preclude Connor; the only issue necessary to the decision was the one-year
SoL, irrelevant to the breach COA.

Commented [GJ1]: An alternative way to phrase the
same concept is that issue preclusion can only be invoked
against parties that lost litigation one, and it cannot be
invoked against Skynet. If students choose this route, they
should also note that Skynet cannot use IP against Connor
because the only issue necessary to the decision was the
SoL, which is not an issue in L2.

Question 9

[Editorial note: The supplied instructor model-answer PDF ends after Question 8 and does not provide a model answer for this open-ended policy question.]

© 2025-2026 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
Page 2 of 10


Law School of Harvard University / 2025-2026

Georgia uses the Second Restatement for Conflicts of Laws. Florida uses the First Restatement.
All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes extending to constitutional limits.
Under Florida Law, a limitations-based dismissal is considered on the merits for claim preclusion
purposes. Florida allows issue preclusion without regard to mutuality, meaning that it allows both
offensive and defensive nonmutual collateral estoppel (so Florida has adopted the New York rule).
Under Georgia law and federal common law, a limitations-based dismissal is considered not on the
merits for claim preclusion. Georgia requires mutuality for issue preclusion but allows the parties to have
shifted procedural postures. So for issue preclusion to run under Georgia law, the same parties must be
involved in lawsuits one and two, but a defendant in lawsuit one can be the plaintiff in lawsuit two, and
vice versa.
Except as noted in the two previous paragraphs, all jurisdictions relevant to this exam use the standard
preclusion law we discussed in class.
On January 4, 2022, Congress passed the Limitations in Federal Courts Act (LFCA), which provided as
follows: “For any cause of action filed after the date of this statute’s passage in a federal district court
sitting in diversity, the applicable limitations period shall be three years from the last event needed for
the action’s accrual.”
Also on January 4, 2022, Congress created a federal tort cause of action that private plaintiffs can assert
called Unauthorized Computer System Access (UCSA). To prevail in an UCSA cause of action, a plaintiff
must prove the following:
• An employee of a state government or the federal government,
• accessed a password-protected computer account belonging to another person,
• without either the account owner’s permission or a court order authorizing such access.
An UCSA cause of action accrues when the defendant accesses the account, and that access is the last
event necessary for this tort to be complete. A defendant found liable for an UCSA tort must pay the
plaintiff $500 for each time an account is accessed. Further, Congress provided for exclusive subject
matter jurisdiction in the federal district courts for an UCSA cause of action, meaning state courts could
not adjudicate a cause of action under UCSA.[^1]
Georgia and Florida have a tort cause of action called Invasion of Privacy by a State Official (IPSO). To
prevail in an IPSO cause of action, a plaintiff must prove the following:
• A state employee,
• accessed a source of information that a person reasonably expects to remain private, such as a
diary kept in a secured location or a password-protected computer account,
• without either the owner’s permission or a court order authorizing such access.
An IPSO cause of action accrues when the defendant accesses the information, and that access is the last
event necessary for this tort to be complete. A defendant found liable for an IPSO tort must pay the
plaintiff $1000 for each instance of information access plus any actual damages the plaintiff can prove. In
both Georgia and Floria, the limitations period for an IPSO cause of action is four years from the last
event needed for the cause of action’s approval.
[^1]: In case you’re wondering: Congress also provided that nothing in the statute preempted state law causes of
action. If you don’t know what this means, ignore it.

© 2025-2026 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
Page 3 of 10


Law School of Harvard University / 2025-2026

In early 2023, the Florida legislature passed a statute called the Limitations Demand Act (LDA). Under the
LDA, any person who reasonably believes that they may become the defendant in a civil lawsuit may
send the likely plaintiff a “demand to sue” letter. Here’s what a “demand to sue” letter means: If a
demand to sue letter references the LDA and demands that the plaintiff decide as soon as possible
whether they will in fact sue, then the plaintiff has six months to commence a lawsuit. If a lawsuit is filed
outside of the six-month period, the defendant may move to dismiss the complaint as time-barred, and
the dismissal is to be treated as though the statute of limitations had run. In passing the LDA, the Florida
legislature stated that the purpose of the LDA was “to reduce burdens on the court system from
dithering plaintiffs and to assure that lawsuits are adjudicated when the evidence is fresh.” No other
state has a law similar to the LDA, nor does the federal government.
Florida, Georgia, and Alabama have three federal districts each. In each state, the names are identical:
the Southern, the Middle, and the Northern.
South Carolina has only one district, the District of South Carolina. Massachusetts has only one federal
district, the District of Massachusetts. Nebraska has only one federal district, the District of Nebraska.
****************
Facts[^2]
1) Leia Princess was a pilot living and working in Miami, FL. Miami is located in the Southern
District of Florida. Princess was 40 years old and had worked as a pilot for Millennium Airlines for
18 years. She had a perfect flying record. In her spare time, Princess was a test pilot for the U.S.
Air Force, earning an extra $80,000 per year from her military work apart from her Millennium
salary.
2) Princess was also an active photographer, shooting everything from landscapes to an occasional
shot from the cockpit of an airplane she happened to be flying. Princess did not exhibit her
photographs. Instead, she stored digital versions of her shots for her private enjoyment in a
password-protected online account she maintained on a website called Tatooine.com.
Tatooine.com was administered by Tatooine Industries, Inc., a Massachusetts corporation with
its principal place of business in Boston, Massachusetts. Tatooine.com allowed a user to open an
account from anywhere in the world simply by specifying a username and a password and by
providing either a major credit card payment or a payment in cryptocurrency. Princesses always
paid in crypto to avoid disclosing her name or address, which she never stored in her
Tatooine.com account. Tatooine maintained servers and a data storage center in a large facility it
owned outside Springfield, Massachusetts. Princess had opened her Tatooine.com account in
2018 from a tablet computer she carried with her while she was on an overnight layover in
Omaha, Nebraska. At the time she opened her account, she uploaded 1.5 gigabytes of photos
that she had stored on her tablet as well as a file called “PrincessPersonal.” The PrincessPersonal
file included photos of and memorabilia about her family, including her mother, about whom she
never spoke. Unbeknownst to anyone who knew her, Princess’s mother was the notorious art

[^2]: Some of the paragraphs here have numbers so that this exam can refer to them later by number. This is not the
text of a complaint.

© 2025-2026 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
Page 4 of 10


Law School of Harvard University / 2025-2026

thief (and speaker of lousy dialog) Padmé Amidala. From 2010-2015, Padmé had been involved
with a gang called “The Empire” that executed high-profile heists of precious jewelry from
famous museums. In 2015, Padmé had been caught. She had pled guilty to multiple felony
counts and was currently serving a 15-year sentence in a federal prison.
3) In January of 2023, Princess was doing a two-week stint in a U.S. Air Force base in Sumter, South
Carolina testing a new model of a military transport plane. One morning, while eating breakfast
in a Sumter hotel, Princess received an email from an anonymous email account with the subject
line, “Is this you?”. The text of the email said, “Princess, as someone who cares about your
welfare, you need to check this out. It looks like this website has personal information about
you.” There was a link to a website at the end of the email. After running anti-malware programs
on the email, Princess retyped the link’s url into a secured browser. The url turned out to be for a
website called IKnowYourSecrets.com. The particular link in the email popped to a page on
IKnowYourSecrets that showed a photo of Princess (copied from the Millenium airlines page),
her home address, and her telephone number. The page also had hundreds of Princess’s photos
and the complete contents of her PrincessPersonal file, including the information about the
identity of her mother.
4) That evening Princess visited Hans Solo, a friend of hers who owned a bar a few miles from her
Sumter (South Carolina) hotel. Solo maintained a side business as a private investigator
specializing in cyber investigations. Solo agreed to investigate the matter to ascertain what
happened and to see if he could figure out a way to get the contents of the PrincessPersonal file
taken down from IKnowYourSecrets.
5) A couple of weeks later, after Princess had returned home to Miami, she received a call from
Solo. Solo’s investigation had disclosed the following:
a. IKnowYourSecrets was maintained from servers in Russia. The site allowed anyone to
upload content and to request the creation of a new page.
b. Solo had hacked the IKnowYourSecrets software to trace the computer that had
uploaded the information on Princess. The address turned out to belong to a computer
in a Georgia state police office in Bainbridge, Georgia. Bainbridge is located in the Middle
District of Georgia.
c. Solo happened to have an old friend who worked in the Bainbridge office of the Georgia
state police. The friend informed Solo that the computer was a desktop assigned to
Officer Darth Vader. Darth was assigned to the cyber-investigation unit of the Bainbridge
office of the Georgia state police, and he took assignments on cyber crimes throughout
the state. He also had a criminal record as a hacker. He had served a two-year stint in
Georgia prison for hacking into bank systems and stealing small amounts of money. A
newspaper article published at the time of his arrest said that Darth was once involved
with “The Empire,” the same gang to which Princess’s mother had belonged. At the time
Darth had been arrested, he’d said, “I think I know who betrayed us all to the cops, and
she goes by the name of ‘Padmé.’ I’ll get back at her one day.”
d. When Solo told his Bainbridge friend that someone Solo knew might have been hacked
from Darth’s Bainbridge desktop, the friend arranged for Solo to have temporary
electronic access to Darth’s Bainbridge desktop. That required that the friend give Solo a
special, temporary backdoor password because the Georgia state police had excellent
security software that, without a backdoor password, prevented all forms of remote
© 2025-2026 by the President and Fellows of Harvard College.
Page 5 of 10


Law School of Harvard University / 2025-2026

access. Solo found a program running on the computer that was slowly exploiting a flaw
in Tatooine.com’s system to download information from password-protected accounts.
The program accessing Tatooine’s systems had begun running in December, 2022.
e. Solo investigated Darth, including by talking with a few of Darth’s former friends he
found online. Solo found that Darth was living in a rented apartment in Abbeville,
Alabama, located in the Middle District of Alabama, where he’d moved after getting

f. Solo said that his hack into IKnowYourSecrets had been discovered and shut down, and
that he had not been able to get back in. Because the IKnowYourSecrets site was hosted
from Russia, he could not do anything to take down the information about Princess
without a court order authorizing someone to attack the site. Princess thanked Solo but
said that she was afraid to seek the court order for fear that doing so would draw more

6) A week later, in February of 2023, Princess received an email and, that same day, a letter sent via
overnight mail. The letter and email were from the U.S. Air Force. They told her that the Air
Force had received an anonymous tip to search a website called IKnowYourSecrets for
information about Princess. The Air Force had done so and had found about her mother’s
criminal history. The letter informed Princess that as a result of learning the information about
her mother, the Air Force was discontinuing her employment as a test pilot and revoking her
security clearance. Princess appealed the loss of her clearance and her job, pointing out that the
Air Force had never asked her about her mother and that she had a perfect flying record, but the
appeal was denied.
7) In March of 2023, Princess sent Darth a letter to his Abbeville and Cerrogordo addresses, with a
copy to his work address in Bainbridge. In the letter, Princess said that she held Darth
responsible for the posting of her personal information on IKnowYourSecrets and the loss of her
Air Force job. Princess informed Darth that she intended to take legal action.
8) In April of 2023, Princess received a letter from the law firm of Jabba & Hutt, which stated that it
was representing Darth in the matter of Princess’s letter. The letter cited to Florida Limitations
Demand Act (LDA) and demanded that Princess decide whether to sue and, if she decided to do
so, that she file any lawsuit(s) within six months.
In July of 2023, Princess sued Darth in Florida state court in Miami, seeking $1000 under an IPSO cause
of action. Call this litigation “Lawsuit #1.” Service of process was properly effectuated in accordance with
Florida law via certified mail.
Question 1 (5 points): What civil procedure defenses, if any, could Darth assert to this lawsuit? Explain.

Law School of Harvard University / 2025-2026

The lawsuit continued. Because Princess sought such a small amount of money, her lawsuit proceeded
under Florida’s summary procedures. Under Florida’s summary process rules,
• no party could take discovery,
• there were no pretrial motions allowed,
• adjudication would proceed via a trial scheduled to last at most a single day with each party
allowed no more than two hours to present its case,
o with the judge initiating the questioning of all witnesses (the parties also had an
opportunity ask their own questions of witnesses), and
o with no party allowed to have a lawyer present at the trial.
The one-day trial took place in September of 2023. Princess and Solo testified for the plaintiff. Princess
testified to the facts described in paragraphs numbered 1-4 and 6, above. Solo testified to the facts
described in paragraphs 5.a through 5.f, above. Darth testified in his own defense. He testified that he
was a state employee and that he had obtained neither a warrant nor permission to access Princess’
account. He also admitted that he had once held a grudge against Padmé, but stated that the passage of
time had caused that grudge to fade away. Darth testified that after receiving Princess’ letter, he had
searched his Bainbridge work computer and had in fact found on it the code that was maliciously
obtaining information from Tatooine.com. He stated that he had not written that code and had no idea
who had. Darth said that he had been a victim of hacking himself, meaning that someone else had
written the code and maliciously installed it on his work computer. He testified that he had deleted the
code accessing Tatooine.com upon finding it.
Three days after the trial, the Florida state court issued a three-page written opinion deciding in Princess’
favor. The opinion recited the facts in paragraphs 1-4, 6, and 5.a through 5.f, above, citing Princess’ and
Solo’s testimony. The court’s opinion then stated, “The natural inference from these facts is that Darth, a
former hacker who knew how to write such code, wrote the malware that hacked into the Tatooine.com
system. Darth had a motive in the form of his hatred for Princess’ mother Padmé. Darth’s testimony that
someone else wrote the program that accessed Princess’ account, and that he no longer bore Padmé a
grudge, is not credible. The security software on the Bainbridge computer prevented (absent a backdoor
password) all forms of remote access, suggesting that the malware was likely written on the Bainbridge
computer as well as running there. Moreover, Darth’s own background as a former hacker makes it
unlikely that he would allow his work computer to be hacked. In short, the court finds that Darth was in
fact the person who accessed Princess’ Tatooine.com account in violation of IPSO. The court awards
Princess $1000.” A week later, Darth wrote Princess a check for $1000.
In the winter of 2023, Darth completed his move to his house in Cerrogordo, Florida, located in the
Northern District of Florida. He obtained a Florida drivers’ license and library card, and he registered to
vote in Florida.
In November of 2024, Princess sued Darth in the United States District Court for the Northern District of
Florida. Call this “Lawsuit #2.” Her complaint stated two causes of action, both based on the
unauthorized access of her Tatooine account in 2022/2023. First, she asserted an UCSA cause of action
and sought $80,500, representing $500 in statutory damages plus $80,000 in damages as one year’s
worth of earnings from her lost test pilot work for the U.S. military. Second, she asserted an IPSO cause
of action as an in-the-alternative theory to recover $80,000 in damages as one year’s worth of lost
earnings from her lost test pilot work for the U.S. military. A process server personally served Darth at his
Cerrogordo (located in the Northern District of Florida) home on the same day the lawsuit was filed.

Question 2[^3]

Darth filed a timely answer, and in this answer demanded a jury trial for the UCSA cause of action.

Princess argued that when she placed her private information in the custody of Tatooine, Inc. by
uploading it to Tatooine.com, she in essence created a trust relationship, with Tatooine, Inc. as the
trustee. She stated that breach of fiduciary duty by a trustee was an equity cause of action.

namely information (and the right to exclude others from that information), from Princess. Thus, the
UCSA lawsuit was like conversion (the old term for “theft”), which was a law cause of action.
Question 3 (4 points): Does Darth have a right to a jury trial in this case on the UCSA cause of action?
Explain.

Assume that Princess withdrew her motion to strike the jury trial, so the court scheduled the case for a

deposition testimony, which covered the facts in paragraphs 1-4 and 6, above. She also attached to her
motion Solo’s deposition testimony, which covered the facts in paragraphs 5.a through 5.f., above. Darth
opposed the motion. He attached to his opposition his own deposition testimony in which he related the
same version of events he had given in Lawsuit #1.[^4]

Chewbacca Rey was one of those who received the Tatooine email. Chewbacca was angry at Darth. In

Question 7 (7 points): How should Lando respond to Chewbacca’s question? Explain. Your answer
should address SMJ, IPJ, and venue in at least two federal district courts in which Chewbacca might
sue, as well as whether a motion to certify a class is likely to be granted.
********************************
This question does not use the fact pattern from Questions 1-7.
Suppose the Supreme Court grants certiorari on the issue of whether it should adopt two versions of the
Matthews v. Eldridge test, one for incorporeal parties (corporations, labor unions, partnerships,
unincorporated associations) and one for human beings. The idea is that the due process needs of
human beings might be different from those of incorporeal entities. Perhaps, for example, in certain
circumstances, given the Supreme Court’s holdings in Lassiter and Turner, for human beings the more
important the interest at issue, the less process there should be. The reason for less process would be to
render what process there is something that human beings could actually use. Perhaps for incorporeal
entities, who frequently have lawyers to represent them, the rule should be the opposite. So for
incorporeal entities, more important interests should lead to more process, the way the Matthews test
operates now. One possible implication of such a proposal is that it might also create a reform
movement to adopt a different set of non-constitutional procedural rules for federal district court
litigation involving only incorporeal entities versus litigation that involves at least one human party.
Question 8 (10 points): Write a short (suggested length 350 words) essay discussing the
background/impetus for this proposal as well as its advantages and disadvantages of this proposal,
including the possible implication (meaning non-constitutional procedural rules that vary based on
whether a human being is a party). Remember to include both, advantages and disadvantages. Focus
your essay on the federal court system.

## 2025-final · Instructor model answer

2025 Civ Pro 1 & 2 Exam Model Answer
1. Darth can challenge IPJ. Darth, not yet moved to Cerrogordo (Mas), likely remains an Alabama (but
definitely not FL) domiciliary, and there is no suggestion of advanced consent or tag in Florida. Targeting
and causing an effect on a FL domiciliary without targeting FL (whatever that means) isn’t enough
(Griffis, Walden). For SIPJ, despite the Florida constitutional-limits long-arm, there are few minimal
contacts relating Florida, litigation content, and Darth. The new house lacks connection to the litigation.
The malware ran in Georgia. The incorporeal Tatooine.com account was likely not “located” in Florida:
Princess opened it in Nevada and discovered the hack in SC. Tatooine’s place of incorporation, PPoB and
servers were in Massachusetts. Few would not expect the account to be in the account-holder’s home
address because Princess never disclosed it to Tatooine. The F&R factors likely permit IPJ in FL. Florida is
not too inconvenient for Darth (he’s moving there), it is convenient for Princesses, Florida has an interest
in protecting Princess, there is some evidence (Princess’ damages) in Florida but most is elsewhere (Solo
in SC, the computer in GA), no misconduct occurred there, and re HCOL the last event necessary to
constitute the tort was the account access (in GA or MA, or perhaps in SC, but not FL).

Commented [JG1]: I awarded a (very) small bonus to
students who seriously and legitimately discussed FNC. The
facts don’t really raise the issue because we don’t have a
physical injury that occurred outside the forum, which is the
fact pattern that most frequently induces courts to consider
FNC, and there is no (for example) wreckage or terrain to
inspect. But the argument is creative.
Commented [JG2]: The LDA letter Darth sent to Princess
is a litigation document. It has no relevance to IPJ.

2.
a. Darth can assert claim preclusion against the IPSO but not the USCA theory. Lawsuits ## 1 & 2
involved the same claim/transaction (access of Princess’s Tatooine.com account) and same party
(Princess). The Lawsuit #1 judgement was final ($1000 awarded) and on the merits (trial judgment).
Under 1738 (state → federal interaction), because FL (using standard preclusion law) would preclude the
IPSO coa, the federal district court(s) must also. Because the USCA is exclusive federal SMJ COA, Princess
could not have asserted the UCSA coa in Lawsuit #1, so the UCSA coa is not claim precluded (Marrese).
Princess can try collateral estoppel. We have same issue (whether Darth wrote/used the
Tatooine.com malware), actually litigated (conflicting trial testimony), necessary to decision (otherwise
no Darth liability), and full mutuality (same plaintiff and defendant) although we don’t need it under Fl
preclusion law. Darth can argue that the procedures in Lawsuit #1 were so summary (no lawyer or
discovery, single-day trial, two hours per side) as to deny a full and fair chance to litigate, and that the
$1000 AIC provided insufficient incentive to litigate. That might work. The procedures meet all
requirements of the Restatement of Conflicts, section 82, but can disallow IP for inadequate procedures
short of a DPC violation. However, the only dispute is whether Darth wrote the hacking code that
operated on his Bainbridge computer, a purely testimonial issue. The L1 court had all relevant evidence
before it on that issue. Darth did testify, and he’s produced no evidence in L2 that he didn’t present in L1,
so it’s hard to see how any supposed lack of opportunity or incentive affected him.
b. Rule 18 allows the joinder of the two coas (same transaction in access of Princess’ account,
common issue is whether Darth did it). The federal UCSA coa triggers 1331 SMJ. There is no DJ (Princess
and now Darth are both FL domiciliaries), but UCSA/IPSO come from same transaction→CorC (only one
hack, same evidence needed for both coas), AUJ renders 1367(b) irrelevant, and the identicality of the
factual issues for the two coas renders a 1367(c) declination unlikely.
Both the N.D. Fla. (general IPJ & (b)(1),(c)(1) venue because Darth is domiciled in Cerrogordo)
and the M.D. Ga. are happy courts. For M.D. Ga. IPJ, the GA long-arm goes to constitutional limits and we
have 4(k)(1)(A); minimal contacts exist in the form of the code running in Bainbridge (a key event
providing 1391(b)(2) venue); and for F&R, key evidence (computer) is in GA, GA is convenient for Darth
(employed there), GA is not too far for Princess, see below re HCOL. Because M.D. Ga. was a happy
court, no prospect of reversion transfer to N.D. Fla.
SoP was perfected by hand (4(e)(2)(A)).

Commented [JG3]: It’s also OK to use M v. E here.


Darth might seek an LDA dismissal because Lawsuit #2 was filed more than six months after the
Jabba demand-to-sue letter. Because N.D. Fla. was a happy court, the M.D. Ga. will use Florida HCoL
(Ferrens, which applies not just to diversity but also SSMJ coAs). The LDA is likely procedural for HCoL
because the scope of “procedural” is wide under HCoL, the LDA resembles a SoL (regulates time limits),
and the FL legislature stated procedural purposes for it. If the LDA is HCOL substantive, it is unlikely that
the last event needed for either coa (the unlawful access, or even the injury) occurred in R1-using FL, so
the LDA would not apply.
For whether the LDA applies in federal court, there are several ways to think about Erie analyses, here
are two. First, the LFCA by its own terms applies only in diversity cases, and this is a 1331/1367 case.
Under this reasoning, the federal law that conflicts with the LDA is the FCL doctrine of laches, discussed
in York. York itself is not directly on point because it concerned the SoL, not a mechanism to shorten the
SoL. Under the outcome determinative test, we’d dismiss under the LDA but we have no suggestion of
problems under laches, so use the LDA and dismiss. Under Ginsburg & Scalia, the source of federal law is
FCL, we use the twin aims, and the outcome-determinative nature of the federal/state law contrast
would lead to forum shopping, so use state law. Under Harlan, the FL legislative findings (court burden,
adjudication accuracy) suggest procedural concerns, not primary conduct, so we likely use the LFCA and
deny any MTD.
Second, perhaps “diversity jurisdiction” in the LFCA is read broadly to mean any time a state coa is extant
(just as, per class discussion, Erie principles generally apply to SSMJ and Sm-Gr coas, even though Erie
itself speaks only of diversity). If so, then under York, the LDA would lead to dismissal of the IPSO coa
(perhaps not the federal USCA), while under the federal LFCA the suit is timely, so we apply the LDA and
dismiss. The Harlan test is the same as before. For the Hannah majority, the LFCA is a valid exercise of
congressional Article III power to create the lower federal courts (plus the necessary & proper clause, if
needed). Scalia would find that it applicable because it answers the question of whether the lawsuit was
timely (Supremacy Clause), so no dismissal, but Ginsburg (although more wary of narrowly construing a
statute) would say that the LDA speaks to the different issue (and thus does not apply here) of whether a
defendant can shorten any applicable limitations period with a demand letter, so use the LDA and
dismiss.

3. Yes. One factual issue on the fact/law divide include whether it was Darth who hacked Princess’
computer. Under Chauffeurs step 1, Princess is suing Darth, the person who allegedly stole something
from her, not Tatooine, Inc., the entity who is analogous to the trustee under the equity coa. So Princess’
lawsuit is not analogous to a suit for fiduciary breach because she is not suing the “trustee.” Conversion
is a better analog. Moreover, under Chauffeurs step 2, the more important step, Princess seeks damages
caused by her loss of a job, a law remedy.
4. Brennan’s dissent, situation 1. It is likely that Princess’ evidence that the malware was found on
Darth’s Bainbridge computer, which had excellent security software, along with Darth’s knowledge of
hacking from his past and his reason to have a grudge against her (Padmé), met her burden to produce
evidence that, if unrebutted, compels a rational jury drawing reasonable inferences in Darth’s favor to
find that Princess met her trial burden of persuasion that Darth programmed/used the Tatooine.com
malware, i.e., no GIMF and Princess is entitled to JAMOL. Darth likely but not certainly met his nowshifted burden to produce evidence to create a genuine issue of material fact by testifying that he did
not program the malware and was a victim of hacking. This is a testimony conflict case. Hans’ hack (he

Commented [JG4]: Students can earn full credit by
conducting only one of these two analyses. In the
alternative, they can receive full credit by contending that
York tells us that the four-year Florida limitations period
applies to the IPSO claim, but whether the LDA applies
requires a separate Erie analysis because the LDA concerns
empowering a party to shorten the SoL. The contrast here
would be with FCL that either is unclear or prohibits

Students will not receive full credit by saying only that the
