or saying that it applies only to diversity during the
Scalia/Ginsburg Hanna tests and concluding no applicability.


had insider help) of Darth somewhat supports Darth’s story. Scott is unlikely to apply because we have
no “irrefutable” video or documentation that Darth did the programming.
5. Princess could renew her JAMOL/50(a) motion and file under R59 for a new trial. On the former,
whether a rational jury, drawing reasonable inferences in Darth’s favor, could rule for Darth is the same
as the MSJ analysis in question 4 minus the burden-shifting (Reeves). Because the trial transcript was
identical to the discovery record, see above. The court under R59 could hold that the verdict was against
the great weight of the evidence; that will depend on the court’s view as to the credibility of Darth’s
testimony (see question 4), which the court may assess on R59.
6. The appellate court will sua sponte dismiss for lack of appellate jurisdiction. Because litigation is
continuing, there is no final order, 1291. There is no certification under 1292(b), no injunction under
1292(a)(1), no finding regarding just reason for delay under R54(b). The new trial is about the merits, so
no Cohen exception. There is no urgency for mandamus.
7. AUJ for the federal UCSA coa works regardless of AIC for all class members (DJ works for none, all are
AIC-deficient). General IPJ and 1391(b)(1) venue are available in the N.D. Fl., Darth’s domicile, see
question 2.b. Specific IPJ is available, and (b)(2) venue proper for all class members’ cases, in the M.D.
Ga., see question 2.b, Darth’s hack being common to all claims regardless of joinder-on-steroids or single
entity.
Two million class members are sufficient for numerosity. Regarding predominance/commonality, with no
evidence of individualized damages, each class member could sue for the UCSA $500. The facts are
identical for all. UCSA is a federal statute, eliminating HCoL. There are no class conflicts, and Chewbacca
is in the same position as oher class members, so typicality/representativeness are present. For (b)(3)
superiority, few will file individual suits for $500, and there is no particular interest in litigating these
cases individually. The court will likely certify.
8. The current one-size fits all approach to civil procedure in federal court means that few pro se litigants
can hope to use the federal system effectively. It is hard to see how such a scheme is consistent with
what the Framers intended when they empowered Congress to create the lower federal courts. The
result is a bizarre version of federalism: only (certain) state courts, those that use procedures that look
nothing like the FRCP, are usable to human beings.
Hypothesize that the goal of Matthews was to assure that adjudicators used procedures that allowed
protection and effectuation of the interests at issue in litigation. (Federal courts have consistently
refused to consider dignity, procedural justice, or other goals in their Matthews analyses). Stipulate that
incarceration (Turner) and loss of a parent-child relationship (Lassiter) are insufficiently important to
compel SCOTUS to require state-appointed counsel. Finally, recognize that additional procedural
protection unusable by an easily identifiable class of litigants (such as human beings) is pointless,
perhaps cruel. On the last point, what was the point of the live hearing in Lassiter, other than to
humiliate the parent? What is the point of requiring a litigation bond to protect a property interest
(Doehr) that a human being might want to attached prefiling if, in 99.99% of cases, no human would-be
attacher could either understand the bond procedures or raise the money to pay for the bond? What is
the point of allowing FRCP-like discovery tools in a $50K AIC case when taking a single deposition would
easily consume 1/5 of the AIC? How does mandating or allowing these procedures further the Matthews
goal identified above? Generalizing Justice Powell’s point in Vitek, the procedural structure of a


proceeding determines its character. Procedures that an identifiable class of litigants cannot use inhibit,
not further, efforts to reach the “right” legal result.
Drawing a line between human and incorporeal parties is a blunt distinction. Some humans (Gates,
Musk) will have unlimited resources, some incorporeals will be David to their adversaries’ Goliaths. It’s
not clear what to do with human versus incorporeal cases. Nothing in the proposal address the
advantages that repeat players have over one-shotters, or the educated have over the less educated. But
all those problems exist (in bucketloads) now. The question should not be whether this proposal solves
all problems but whether it’s likely to address some. Adding more accessibility in human-party litigation
might make the world better; perfection will remain with the angels.
The proposal would mean giving up on trans-substantive procedural structures. Some might argue doing
so increases the kind of complexity that this proposal attempts to address. But civil procedure law
already treats incorporeal entities and humans differently for SoP (FRCP 4), SMJ (domicile rules), venue
(1391(c)), and elsewhere. Moreover, in an Internet age, that would frequently mean little more than
“Click here if you’re a human, otherwise here.” And the small increase in complexity could open up a
vast area for creative solutions for human litigation to access to justice issue while preserving the ideals
(neutral & disinterested decision maker, sufficient notice, etc.) included in, for example, the Restatement
of Judgments: a judge’s inquisitorial questioning, or a court employee empowered to gather obviously
relevant and easily available documents.

Civil Procedure
D. James Greiner
Practice Exam for In-class, no-electronics administration
The exam mode is In-class.
This exam is 9 pages long. Please check to see that you have all 9 pages. This exam has 6 questions.
Make sure that your copy has 6 questions.
While taking this examination you may consult only written materials. Materials other than those
provided in class are likely to be more of a hindrance than a help. You may not discuss the
examination with anyone, either while taking it or thereafter, until the Registrar has returned 1L Fall
grades.

By submitting your exam answer(s), you acknowledge the above instructions, and certify
that the work you are submitting is your own, that you have not received unauthorized
assistance on the exam, including use of AI (such as ChatGPT and other large language
models) unless explicitly permitted by your instructor, and that you have followed
applicable rules, including rules for accessing reference and other materials during the
exam.

1. The word limit on this exam is 1500 words. If you exceed that limit, only the first 1500
words will be graded.
2. Exam4 will automatically print your Anonymous ID and word count on the exam.
3. If you feel that there is a mistake in the exam, such as that a question is unclear, or that it
refers to something that it should not, or that there is an important typographical error,
then do the following. First, in your answer, explain what you believe to be the mistake.
Second, explain the implications of the mistake. Third, make an assumption that is
reasonable given the fact pattern and applicable law to resolve the error. Fourth, proceed
with the remainder of the exam.
4. Each question’s weight is specified (out of 65 total points). Time and word-count may be
issues for you during the exam. Answer questions efficiently, and feel free to take into
account the number of points available for each question. Don’t forget your test-taking
skills.
5. Do not put your name, or otherwise identify yourself, on your response.
6. Deal with all issues fairly raised, even if you think another issue would be dispositive of a
matter in the real world.
7. If you believe additional information (factual or legal) is needed to deal with an issue, say
so and make explicit whatever assumptions you need to proceed, but make these
assumptions reasonable and interesting. Similarly, if at any point in this question you find
that you do not know what the law is on a particular subject, identify the issue as to
which there is uncertainty, then discuss the various options as to what the law could or
should be, noting the advantages or disadvantages of each option.

8. Feel free to use obvious abbreviations to save time and space: “SMJ” for subject matter
jurisdiction; “IPJ” for in personam jurisdiction; “DJ” for diversity jurisdiction; “FQJ” for
federal question jurisdiction; “CNOF” for common nucleus of operative facts; and
“DPC” for due process clause.
9. All the scenarios described below might be, and probably are, fictional; that includes the
law described in this exam. Use the scenarios only for the purposes of this exam.
10. Citing authority is optional. You can get full credit without citation if you explain and
apply the relevant principle. That said, citing authority could well save you time &
words. For example, you might say, “Minimum contacts, International Shoe,” instead of,
“Under the test the Court articulated in International Shoe and subsequent cases, a court
may exercise in personam jurisdiction over an objecting, non-resident defendant only if a
long-arm statute authorizes such exercise, and only if the defendant has ‘minimal
contacts’ with the forum, and only if the exercise of such jurisdiction would be fair and
reasonable under the circumstances.”
11. If an issue in an earlier question recurs in a later question, you need not repeat your
answer in the later question. In your later question answer, you may simply note that the
answer here is the same as in the earlier question (please provide the number of the
earlier question).
Good luck!

Georgia uses the Second Restatement for Conflicts of Laws. Florida uses the First Restatement.
All states relevant to this exam have long-arm statutes extending to constitutional limits.
Under Florida Law, a limitations-based dismissal is considered on the merits for claim preclusion
purposes. Florida allows issue preclusion without regard to mutuality, meaning that it allows both
offensive and defensive nonmutual collateral estoppel (so Florida has adopted the New York rule).
Under Georgia law and federal common law, a limitations-based dismissal is considered not on the
merits for claim preclusion. Georgia requires mutuality for issue preclusion but allows the parties to have
shifted procedural postures. So for issue preclusion to run under Georgia law, the same parties must be
involved in lawsuits one and two, but a defendant in lawsuit one can be the plaintiff in lawsuit two, and
vice versa.
Except as noted in the two previous paragraphs, all jurisdictions relevant to this exam use the standard
preclusion law we discussed in class.
On January 4, 2022, Congress passed the Limitations in Federal Courts Act (LFCA), which provided as
follows: “For any cause of action filed after the date of this statute’s passage in a federal district court
sitting in diversity, the applicable limitations period shall be three years from the last event needed for
the action’s accrual.”
Also on January 4, 2022, Congress created a federal tort cause of action that private plaintiffs can assert
called Unauthorized Computer System Access (UCSA). To prevail in an UCSA cause of action, a plaintiff
must prove the following:
• An employee of a state government or the federal government,
• accessed a password-protected computer account belonging to another person,
• without either the account owner’s permission or a court order authorizing such access.
An UCSA cause of action accrues when the defendant accesses the account, and that access is the last
event necessary for this tort to be complete. A defendant found liable for an UCSA tort must pay the
plaintiff $500 for each time an account is accessed. Further, Congress provided for exclusive subject
matter jurisdiction in the federal district courts for an UCSA cause of action, meaning state courts could
not adjudicate a cause of action under UCSA.[^1]
Georgia and Florida have a tort cause of action called Invasion of Privacy by a State Official (IPSO). To
prevail in an IPSO cause of action, a plaintiff must prove the following:
• A state employee,
• accessed a source of information that a person reasonably expects to remain private, such as a
diary kept in a secured location or a password-protected computer account,
• without either the owner’s permission or a court order authorizing such access.
An IPSO cause of action accrues when the defendant accesses the information, and that access is the last
event necessary for this tort to be complete. A defendant found liable for an IPSO tort must pay the
plaintiff $1000 for each instance of information access plus any actual damages the plaintiff can prove. In
both Georgia and Floria, the limitations period for an IPSO cause of action is four years from the last
event needed for the cause of action’s approval.
[^1]: In case you’re wondering: Congress also provided that nothing in the statute preempted state law causes of
action. If you don’t know what this means, ignore it.

In early 2023, the Florida legislature passed a statute called the Limitations Demand Act (LDA). Under the
LDA, any person who reasonably believes that they may become the defendant in a civil lawsuit may
send the likely plaintiff a “demand to sue” letter. Here’s what a “demand to sue” letter means: If a
demand to sue letter references the LDA and demands that the plaintiff decide as soon as possible
whether they will in fact sue, then the plaintiff has six months to commence a lawsuit. If a lawsuit is filed
outside of the six-month period, the defendant may move to dismiss the complaint as time-barred, and
the dismissal is to be treated as though the statute of limitations had run. In passing the LDA, the Florida
legislature stated that the purpose of the LDA was “to reduce burdens on the court system from
dithering plaintiffs and to assure that lawsuits are adjudicated when the evidence is fresh.” No other
state has a law similar to the LDA, nor does the federal government.
Florida and Georgia have three federal districts each. In each state, the names are identical: the
Southern, the Middle, and the Northern.
South Carolina has only one district, the District of South Carolina. Massachusetts has only one federal
district, the District of Massachusetts. Nebraska has only one federal district, the District of Nebraska.
****************
Facts[^2]
1) Leia Princess was a pilot living and working in Miami, FL. Miami is located in the Southern
District of Florida. Princess was 40 years old and had worked as a pilot for Millennium Airlines for
18 years. She had a perfect flying record. In her spare time, Princess was a test pilot for the U.S.
Air Force, earning an extra $80,000 per year from her military work apart from her Millennium
salary.
2) Princess was also an active photographer, shooting everything from landscapes to an occasional
shot from the cockpit of an airplane she happened to be flying. Princess did not exhibit her
photographs. Instead, she stored digital versions of her shots for her private enjoyment in a
password-protected online account she maintained on a website called Tatooine.com.
Tatooine.com was administered by Tatooine Industries, Inc., a Massachusetts corporation with
its principal place of business in Boston, Massachusetts. Tatooine.com allowed a user to open an
account from anywhere in the world simply by specifying a username and a password and by
providing either a major credit card payment or a payment in cryptocurrency. Princesses always
paid in crypto to avoid disclosing her name or address, which she never stored in her
Tatooine.com account. Tatooine maintained servers and a data storage center in a large facility it
owned outside Springfield, Massachusetts. Princess had opened her Tatooine.com account in
2018 from a tablet computer she carried with her while she was on an overnight layover in
Omaha, Nebraska. At the time she opened her account, she uploaded 1.5 gigabytes of photos
that she had stored on her tablet as well as a file called “PrincessPersonal.” The PrincessPersonal
file included photos of and memorabilia about her family, including her mother, about whom she
never spoke. Unbeknownst to anyone who knew her, Princess’s mother was the notorious art

thief (and speaker of lousy dialog) Padmé Amidala. From 2010-2015, Padmé had been involved
with a gang called “The Empire” that executed high-profile heists of precious jewelry from
famous museums. In 2015, Padmé had been caught. She had pled guilty to multiple felony
counts and was currently serving a 15-year sentence in a federal prison.
3) In January of 2023, Princess was doing a two-week stint in a U.S. Air Force base in Sumter, South
Carolina testing a new model of a military transport plane. One morning, while eating breakfast
in a Sumter hotel, Princess received an email from an anonymous email account with the subject
line, “Is this you?”. The text of the email said, “Princess, as someone who cares about your
welfare, you need to check this out. It looks like this website has personal information about
you.” There was a link to a website at the end of the email. After running anti-malware programs
on the email, Princess retyped the link’s url into a secured browser. The url turned out to be for a
website called IKnowYourSecrets.com. The particular link in the email popped to a page on
IKnowYourSecrets that showed a photo of Princess (copied from the Millenium airlines page),
her home address, and her telephone number. The page also had hundreds of Princess’s photos
and the complete contents of her PrincessPersonal file, including the information about the
identity of her mother.
4) That evening Princess visited Hans Solo, a friend of hers who owned a bar a few miles from her
Sumter (South Carolina) hotel. Solo maintained a side business as a private investigator
specializing in cyber investigations. Solo agreed to investigate the matter to ascertain what
happened and to see if he could figure out a way to get the contents of the PrincessPersonal file
taken down from IKnowYourSecrets.
5) A couple of weeks later, after Princess had returned home to Miami, she received a call from
Solo. Solo’s investigation had disclosed the following:
a. IKnowYourSecrets was maintained from servers in Russia. The site allowed anyone to
upload content and to request the creation of a new page.
b. Solo had hacked the IKnowYourSecrets software to trace the computer that had
uploaded the information on Princess. The address turned out to belong to a computer
in a Georgia state police office in Bainbridge, Georgia. Bainbridge is located in the Middle
District of Georgia.
c. Solo happened to have an old friend who worked in the Bainbridge office of the Georgia
state police. The friend informed Solo that the computer was a desktop assigned to
Officer Darth Vader. Darth was assigned to the cyber-investigation unit of the Bainbridge
office of the Georgia state police, and he took assignments on cyber crimes throughout
the state. He also had a criminal record as a hacker. He had served a two-year stint in
Georgia prison for hacking into bank systems and stealing small amounts of money. A
newspaper article published at the time of his arrest said that Darth was once involved
with “The Empire,” the same gang to which Princess’s mother had belonged. At the time
Darth had been arrested, he’d said, “I think I know who betrayed us all to the cops, and
she goes by the name of ‘Padmé.’ I’ll get back at her one day.”
d. When Solo told his Bainbridge friend that someone Solo knew might have been hacked
from Darth’s Bainbridge desktop, the friend arranged for Solo to have temporary
electronic access to Darth’s Bainbridge desktop. That required that the friend give Solo a
special, temporary backdoor password because the Georgia state police had excellent
security software that, without a backdoor password, prevented all forms of remote

access. Solo found a program running on the computer that was slowly exploiting a flaw
in Tatooine.com’s system to download information from password-protected accounts.
The program accessing Tatooine’s systems had begun running in December, 2022.
e. Solo investigated Darth, including by talking with a few of Darth’s former friends he
found online. Solo found that Darth was living in Cerrogordo, Florida, located in the
Northern District of Florida. He had purchased a house in Cerrogordo and moved in
about two years earlier. He’d changed his driver’s license to Florida and registered to
vote there.
f. Solo said that his hack into IKnowYourSecrets had been discovered and shut down, and
that he had not been able to get back in. Because the IKnowYourSecrets site was hosted
from Russia, he could not do anything to take down the information about Princess
without a court order authorizing someone to attack the site. Princess thanked Solo but
said that she was afraid to seek the court order for fear that doing so would draw more
attention to the site.
6) A week later, in February of 2023, Princess received an email and, that same day, a letter sent via
overnight mail. The letter and email were from the U.S. Air Force. They told her that the Air
Force had received an anonymous tip to search a website called IKnowYourSecrets for
information about Princess. The Air Force had done so and had found about her mother’s
criminal history. The letter informed Princess that as a result of learning the information about
her mother, the Air Force was discontinuing her employment as a test pilot and revoking her
security clearance. Princess appealed the loss of her clearance and her job, pointing out that the
Air Force had never asked her about her mother and that she had a perfect flying record, but the
appeal was denied.
7) In March of 2023, Princess sent Darth a letter to his Cerrogordo addresses, with a copy to his
work address in Bainbridge. In the letter, Princess said that she held Darth responsible for the
posting of her personal information on IKnowYourSecrets and the loss of her Air Force job.
Princess informed Darth that she intended to take legal action.
8) In April of 2023, Princess received a letter from the law firm of Jabba & Hutt, which stated that it
was representing Darth in the matter of Princess’s letter. The letter cited to Florida Limitations
Demand Act (LDA) and demanded that Princess decide whether to sue and, if she decided to do
so, that she file any lawsuit(s) within six months.
In July of 2023, Princess sued Darth in Florida state court in Miami, seeking $1000 under an IPSO cause
of action. Call this litigation “Lawsuit #1.” Service of process was properly effectuated in accordance with
Florida law via certified mail. Because Princess sought such a small amount of money, her lawsuit
proceeded under Florida’s summary procedures. Under Florida’s summary process rules,
• no party could take discovery,
• there were no pretrial motions allowed,
• adjudication would proceed via a trial scheduled to last at most a single day with each party
allowed no more than two hours to present its case,
o with the judge initiating the questioning of all witnesses (the parties also had an
opportunity ask their own questions of witnesses), and
o with no party allowed to have a lawyer present at the trial.

The one-day trial took place in September of 2023. Princess and Solo testified for the plaintiff. Princess
testified to the facts described in paragraphs numbered 1-4 and 6, above. Solo testified to the facts
described in paragraphs 5.a through 5.f, above. Darth testified in his own defense. He testified that he
was a state employee and that he had obtained neither a warrant nor permission to access Princess’
account. He also admitted that he had once held a grudge against Padmé, but stated that the passage of
time had caused that grudge to fade away. Darth testified that after receiving Princess’ letter, he had
searched his Bainbridge work computer and had in fact found on it the code that was maliciously
obtaining information from Tatooine.com. He stated that he had not written that code and had no idea
who had. Darth said that he had been a victim of hacking himself, meaning that someone else had
written the code and maliciously installed it on his work computer. He testified that he had deleted the
code accessing Tatooine.com upon finding it.
Three days after the trial, the Florida state court issued a three-page written opinion deciding in Princess’
favor. The opinion recited the facts in paragraphs 1-4, 6, and 5.a through 5.f, above, citing Princess’ and
Solo’s testimony. The court’s opinion then stated, “The natural inference from these facts is that Darth, a
former hacker who knew how to write such code, wrote the malware that hacked into the Tatooine.com
system. Darth had a motive in the form of his hatred for Princess’ mother Padmé. Darth’s testimony that
someone else wrote the program that accessed Princess’ account, and that he no longer bore Padmé a
grudge, is not credible. The security software on the Bainbridge computer prevented (absent a backdoor
password) all forms of remote access, suggesting that the malware was likely written on the Bainbridge
computer as well as running there. Moreover, Darth’s own background as a former hacker makes it
unlikely that he would allow his work computer to be hacked. In short, the court finds that Darth was in
fact the person who accessed Princess’ Tatooine.com account in violation of IPSO. The court awards
Princess $1000.” A week later, Darth wrote Princess a check for $1000.
In the winter of 2023, Darth completed his move to his house in Cerrogordo, Florida, located in the
Northern District of Florida. He obtained a Florida drivers’ license and library card, and he registered to
vote in Florida.
In November of 2024, Princess sued Darth in the United States District Court for the Northern District of
Florida. Call this “Lawsuit #2.” Her complaint stated two causes of action, both based on the
unauthorized access of her Tatooine account in 2022/2023. First, she asserted an UCSA cause of action
and sought $80,500, representing $500 in statutory damages plus $80,000 in damages as one year’s
worth of earnings from her lost test pilot work for the U.S. military. Second, she asserted an IPSO cause
of action as an in-the-alternative theory to recover $80,000 in damages as one year’s worth of lost
earnings from her lost test pilot work for the U.S. military. A process server personally served Darth at his
Cerrogordo (located in the Northern District of Florida) home on the same day the lawsuit was filed.
Before Darth could answer or file any motions, the Northern District of Florida court sua sponte
transferred the case to the United States District Court for the Middle District of Georgia (which includes
Bainbridge).
Question 1
(a) (12 points): Could either party make use of the doctrine of preclusion (either kind) in
Lawsuit #2? Explain.
(b) (17 points): Assume that neither party can use preclusion. What other civil procedure
defenses/motions could Darth assert in response to the Litigation #2 complaint? Explain.

Assume that the court (rightly or wrongly) denied any motions that Darth filed, so Lawsuit #2 continued.
Darth filed a timely answer, and in this answer demanded a jury trial for the UCSA cause of action.
Princess moved to strike the jury trial demand. The parties brief the issue of whether Darth had a right
to a jury trial.
Princess argued that when she placed her private information in the custody of Tatooine, Inc. by
uploading it to Tatooine.com, she in essence created a trust relationship, with Tatooine, Inc. as the
trustee. She stated that breach of fiduciary duty by a trustee was an equity cause of action.
Darth argued that the UCSA allegation against him were as though he stole something from Princess,
namely information (and the right to exclude others from that information), from Princess. Thus, the
UCSA lawsuit was like conversion (the old term for “theft”), which was a law cause of action.
Question 2 (5 points): Does Darth have a right to a jury trial in this case on the UCSA cause of action?
Explain.

the information of approximately two million account holders. Tatooine’s engineers wrote a program to
search the Internet and the Dark Web for any of the information stored in Tatooine’s accounts. They ran
that search program on a super-computer and found no evidence that anyone other than Princess had

any posting/publication of their private information. Princess received the Tatooine email.
Princess decided to ask leave from the court to file a motion for class certification to represent a class
asserting only a UCSA cause of action for the statutorily specified $500 per class member against Darth.

This question does not use the fact pattern from Questions 1-5.
Suppose the Supreme Court grants certiorari on the issue of whether it should adopt two versions of the
Matthews v. Eldridge test, one for incorporeal parties (corporations, labor unions, partnerships,
unincorporated associations) and one for human beings. The idea is that the due process needs of
human beings might be different from those of incorporeal entities. Perhaps, for example, in certain
circumstances, given the Supreme Court’s holdings in Lassiter and Turner, for human beings the more
important the interest at issue, the less process there should be. The reason for less process would be to
render what process there is something that human beings could actually use. Perhaps for incorporeal
entities, who frequently have lawyers to represent them, the rule should be the opposite. So for
incorporeal entities, more important interests should lead to more process, the way the Matthews test
operates now. One possible implication of such a proposal is that it might also create a reform
movement to adopt a different set of non-constitutional procedural rules for federal district court
litigation involving only incorporeal entities versus litigation that involves at least one human party.
Question 6 (10 points): Write a short (suggested length 250 words) essay discussing the
background/impetus for this proposal as well as its advantages and disadvantages of this proposal,
including the possible implication (meaning non-constitutional procedural rules that vary based on
whether a human being is a party). Remember to include both, advantages and disadvantages. Focus
your essay on the federal court system.

## 2025-shortened-final · Instructor model answer

2025 Civ Pro 1 & 2 Exam Model Answer, Shortened
1. a. Darth can assert claim preclusion against the IPSO, not the USCA. Lawsuits ## 1 & 2 involved the
same claim/transaction (access of Princess’s Tatooine.com account) and same party (Princess). The
Lawsuit #1 judgement was final ($1000 awarded) and on the merits (trial judgment). Under 1738 (state
→ federal interaction), because FL (using standard preclusion law) would preclude the IPSO COA, the
federal district court(s) must also. Because the USCA is exclusive federal SMJ COA, Princess could not
have asserted the UCSA COA in Lawsuit #1, so it isn’t claim precluded (Marrese).
Princess can try collateral estoppel – same issue (whether Darth wrote/used the Tatooine.com malware),
actually litigated (conflicting trial testimony), necessary to decision (otherwise no Darth liability), and full
mutuality (same plaintiff/defendant), though FL preclusion law doesn’t require it. Darth could
persuasively argue that Lawsuit #1’s procedures were so summary (no lawyer/discovery, single-day trial,
two hours each) they denied a full and fair chance to litigate, and the $1000 AIC provided insufficient
incentive to litigate. The procedures meet all requirements of the Restatement of Conflicts, section 82,
although courts can disallow IP for inadequate procedures short of a DPC violation. However, the only
dispute is whether Darth wrote the hacking code, a purely testimonial issue about which the L1 court
had all the relevant evidence. Darth testified, and he’s produced no evidence in L2 that he didn’t present
in L1, so it’s unclear how any supposed lack of opportunity or incentive affected him.
b. Rule 18 allows the joinder of the two COAs (same transaction in access of Princess’ account, common
issue is whether Darth did it). The federal UCSA COA triggers 1331 SMJ. There is no DJ (Princess and now
Darth are both FL domiciliaries), but UCSA/IPSO come from same transaction→CorC (one hack, same
evidence needed for both COAs), AUJ renders 1367(b) irrelevant, and facts’ identicality for the two COAs
renders a 1367(c) declination unlikely.
Both the N.D. Fla. (general IPJ & (b)(1),(c)(1) venue because Darth is domiciled in Cerrogordo) and the
M.D. Ga. are happy courts. For M.D. Ga. IPJ, the GA long-arm goes to constitutional limits/4(k)(1)(A);
minimal contacts exist in given the code running in Bainbridge (a key event providing 1391(b)(2) venue);
and for F&R, key evidence (computer) is in GA, which is convenient for Darth (employed there) and not
too far for Princess, see below re HCOL. Because M.D. Ga. was a happy court, there is no prospect of
reversed transfer to N.D. Fla.
SoP was perfected by hand (4(e)(2)(A)).
Darth might seek an LDA dismissal because Lawsuit #2 was filed over six months after the demand-tosue letter. Because N.D. Fla. was a happy court, the M.D. Ga. will use Florida HCoL (Ferens, which applies
to DJ and SSMJ COAs). The LDA is likely procedural for HCoL because “procedural” is broad under HCoL,
the LDA resembles a SoL (regulates time limits), and the FL legislature stated procedural purposes. If the
LDA is HCOL substantive, it is unlikely the last event needed for either COA (the unlawful access, or even
the injury) occurred in R1-using FL, so the LDA is inapplicable.
For whether the LDA applies in federal court, there are several approaches to Erie. Here’s one: perhaps
“diversity jurisdiction” in the LFCA is read broadly to mean any time a state COA is extant (just as, per
class, Erie principles generally apply to SSMJ and Sm-Gr COAs, even though Erie speaks only of diversity).
Then, under York, the LDA would lead to dismissal of the IPSO COA (perhaps not the federal USCA), while
under the federal LFCA the suit is timely, so apply the LDA and dismiss. Under Harlan, the FL legislative
findings (court burden, adjudication accuracy) suggest procedural concerns, not primary conduct, so we

Commented [JG1]: It’s also OK to use M v. E here.


likely use the LFCA and deny any MTD. For the Hanna majority, the LFCA is a valid exercise of
congressional Article III power to create lower federal courts (plus N&P clause, if needed). Scalia would
find it applied by answering whether the lawsuit was timely (Supremacy Clause), so no dismissal, but
Ginsburg (although warier of narrowly construing statutes) would say the LDA speaks to the different
issue (and thus is inapplicable) of whether a defendant can shorten any applicable limitations period
with a demand letter, so use the LDA and dismiss.
2. Yes. One factual issue on the fact/law divide includes whether Darth hacked Princess’ computer.
Under Chauffeurs step 1, Princess is suing Darth, the person who allegedly stole something from her, not
Tatooine, Inc., the entity analogous to the trustee under the equity COA. Princess’ lawsuit isn’t analogous
to a fiduciary breach suit because she’s not suing the “trustee.” Conversion is a better analog. Moreover,
under Chauffeurs step 2, the more important step, Princess seeks damages from job loss, a law remedy.
3. AUJ for the federal UCSA COA works regardless of AIC for all class members (DJ works for none given
AIC-deficiencies). General IPJ and 1391(b)(1) venue are available in the N.D. Fl., Darth’s domicile, see
question 1.b. Specific IPJ is available, and (b)(2) venue proper for all class members, in the M.D. Ga., see
question 1.b, Darth’s hack being common to all claims regardless of joinder-on-steroids/single entity.
Two million members satisfy numerosity. Regarding predominance/commonality, with no evidence of
individualized damages, each class member could sue for the UCSA $500. The facts are identical. UCSA is
a federal statute, eliminating HCoL. There are no class conflicts. Princess is in the same position as other
members, satisfying typicality/representativeness. For (b)(3) superiority, few will file individual suits for
$500, and there’s no particular interest in litigating these cases individually. The court will likely certify.
4. Princess could renew her JAMOL/50(a) motion and file under R59 for a new trial. On the former, could
the rational jury, drawing reasonable inferences in Darth’s favor, rationally rule for Darth? Princess’
evidence that the malware was on Darth’s Bainbridge computer, which had excellent security, along with
Darth’s past hacking and reason to begrudge her (Padmé), is strong but not dispositive. Darth testified he
did not use the malware and was a victim of hacking. This is a testimony conflict case. Hans’ hack
(though he had insider help) of Darth somewhat supports Darth’s story. Scott is likely inapplicable
because there’s no “irrefutable” video or documentation that Darth did the programming. The court
under R59 could grant a new trial on the grounds that the verdict was against the great weight of the
evidence; that will depend on its view as to the credibility of Darth’s testimony, which the court may
assess on R59.
5. The appellate court will sua sponte dismiss for lack of appellate jurisdiction. There’s no final order,
1291 (litigation is continuing), certification under 1292(b), injunction under 1292(a)(1), finding regarding
just reason for delay under R54(b), Cohen exception (new trial is about the merits), or urgency for
mandamus.
6. Federal courts’ indiscriminate approach to civil procedure prevents pro se litigants from effectively
using the federal system, undermining the Framers’ intent for lower courts and creating a bizarre version
of federalism: only state courts that use procedures unlike the FRCP are usable for humans.
Hypothesize that Mathews goal was procedures that protected and effectuated interests at issue in
litigation. (Federal courts’ Mathews analyses ignore dignity, procedural justice, or other goals). Stipulate
that incarceration (Turner) and loss of a child (Lassiter) fail to warrant state-appointed counsel.
Additional procedural protection unusable by an easily identifiable class of litigants is pointless, perhaps

Commented [JG2]: Students can earn full credit by
conducting any one of (a) the analysis in the model answer,
here, or (b) those provided below.
* The LFCA by its own terms applies only in diversity cases,
and this is a 1331/1367 case. Here, the federal law that
conflicts with the LDA is the FCL doctrine of laches,
discussed in York. York isn’t directly on point because it
concerned the SoL, not a mechanism to shorten the SoL.
Under the outcome determinative test, we’d dismiss under
the LDA but have no suggestion of problems under laches,
so use the LDA and dismiss. Under Ginsburg & Scalia, the
source of federal law is FCL, use the twin aims, and the
outcome-determinative nature of the federal/state law
contrast would encourage forum shopping, use state law.
Under Harlan, the FL legislative findings (court burden,
adjudication accuracy) suggest procedural concerns, not
primary conduct, so likely use the LFCA and deny any MTD.
* Or, students can argue that York tells us that the four-year
Florida limitations period applies to the IPSO claim, but
whether the LDA applies requires a separate Erie analysis
because the LDA concerns empowering a party to shorten
the SoL. The contrast here would be with FCL that either is
unclear or prohibits shortening of SoLs by letter (students
can hypothesize which).
* Students will not receive full credit by saying only that the
LFCA applies only to diversity and avoiding any Erie analysis,
or saying that it applies only to diversity during the
Scalia/Ginsburg Hanna tests and concluding no applicability.


cruel. Lassiter’s hearing only humiliated the parent. Few humans can afford or understand litigation
bonds (Doehr). FRCP-like discovery tools in a $50K AIC case could be exhausted by one deposition. Such
procedures do not further Mathews’ goal. Generalizing Justice Powell’s Vitek argument, a proceeding’s
structure determines its character. Unusable procedures inhibit “right” legal results.
The proposal ignores repeat or educated players’ advantages, and its human/incorporeal distinction is
blunt (consider Musk vs a small business). However, what’s important is not whether it solves all
problems but that it increasing accessibility in human-party litigation addresses some.
Abandoning trans-substantive procedural structures risks increasing complexity. But incorporeal entities
and humans are already distinct for SoP, domicile rules, venue etc. In practice, that could mean, “Click
here if you’re human, otherwise here.” Complexity could stimulate creative solutions to democratize
justice while preserving ideals like a neutral & disinterested decision maker, sufficient notice, etc.
